Quantum Frequential Computing: a quadratic runtime advantage for all computations
Mischa P. Woods

Abstract
An enduring challenge in computer science is reducing the runtime required to solve computational problems. Quantum computing has attracted significant attention due to its potential to deliver asymptotically faster solutions to certain problems compared to the best-known classical algorithms. This advantage is enabled by the quantum mechanical nature of the logical degrees of freedom. To date, it was unknown if permitting other parts of the computer to be quantum mechanical, rather than semi-classical, could yield additional runtime speed-ups as a function of resource utilization (e.g., power consumption or cooling requirements). In this work, we prove that when the control mechanisms associated with gate implementation are optimal quantum mechanical states, a quadratic runtime speedup (with respect to power consumption or cooling rate) is achievable for any algorithm, relative to optimal classical or semi-classical control schemes. Moreover, we demonstrate that only a small fraction of the computer’s architecture needs to employ optimal quantum control states to realize this advantage, thereby significantly simplifying the design of future systems. We call this new device a quantum frequential computer , since the quantum speedup arises from an increase in gate frequency. In current state-of-the-art designs, gate frequency is often limited by the coupling strength between components. Notably, our approach achieves the speedup without requiring an increase in coupling strength.



I
Introduction

I.1
Background and challenge

Reducing the runtime required to solve computational problems is the end goal of many different research fields in computer science. For example, better algorithms, improved system architectures, or faster computer clock and gate speeds, can all lead to a reduction in the runtime.

Historically, all three areas of research have led to significant runtime gains. For example, algorithms for improved matrix multiplication
[
21
]
have led to a speedup in a wide number of computational problems. Architectural methods such as instruction pipe-lining and out-of-order execution have also led to speedups
[
15
,
29
]
. Likewise, the clock speed of computers has increased dramatically since their inception. In 1938 the first fully mechanical analogue computer, the Z1, operated at one Hz. Later electronic computers further increased this frequency with decade-on-decade speedups until around 2003, when progress stalled in the GHz range. Since then, there has been a significant slowdown due to a breakdown in Dennard’s scaling, which predicts how transistor speed increases when chips follow Moore’s law
[
17
]
. The breakdown was caused by changes in transistor geometry required for the continuation of Moore’s-law scaling.

Similarly, improvements in classical algorithms can only take us so far. The development of quantum computing was motivated precisely because it was realised that quantum mechanics allowed for better scaling than the best-known (or optimal) classical algorithm in a select group of problems. Grover’s search algorithm
[
30
]
, and Shor’s factoring algorithm
[
58
]
are some of the most well-known examples. This said, the number of problems with significant algorithmic quantum speed-up is, to date, quite restrictive
[
3
,
32
]
. Additionally, some of the key fields which quantum computing is set to revolutionise—materials research, chemistry and drug design, to name a few
[
41
,
68
,
56
,
4
]
—are becoming increasingly superfluous due to the advent of AI (as the AlphaFold program may have already achieved for protein folding
[
1
,
8
,
2
]
).

In addition to the hurdle of decreasing runtime, there is also the desire to consume less power. Computation is estimated to consume over 3% of global power generation
[
59
,
65
]

and the world’s still-exploding boom in AI is predicted to drive that number up significantly—and fast
[
37
]
.

I.2
Approach taken in this
manuscript
and overview of results

The above-mentioned runtime speedups originating from using quantum algorithms require a quantum rather than classical logical register.

Figure 1:
a)
Pictorial illustration of a conventional classical and quantum computer: the physical implementation of an algorithm realises a trajectory through physical state space of the information-bearing degrees of freedom (d.o.f.) and their control d.o.f.: This trajectory (dark blue line) passes through a sequence of “waypoints” (logical states) corresponding to the sequence of states dictated by the algorithm when compiled in machine code, i.e. the logical states generated by the logical gate set. Path-length corresponds to the algorithm’s runtime. The difference between a classical and a quantum computer is that in the former the waypoints can only be classical logical states while in the latter they can be quantum. It is this “quantization of waypoints” along the trajectory which gives quantum computers their runtime advantage (for select problems), and why there is much excitement surrounding them. But this is only half the story: it is not
only
the total number of required waypoints which determines an algorithm’s runtime, but also the time required to move the logical state between waypoints. The latter is determined by the nature of the gate control (e.g. “control pulses”). In quantum and classical computing, the gate control is considered to be a classical or semi-classical state. For a given energy in the control, this restricts the available trajectories of the logical state between two consecutive waypoints.

FIG. 1.
b)
The same two sequences of waypoints as fig. 1 a) but now allowing for the control to be a quantum state: for the same energy in the gate control, the permissible trajectories between two consecutive waypoints is much larger and the path-length (now in orange) between consecutive waypoints, i.e. the time required to implement each gate, for the same given energy in the control, can be drastically reduced. This holds true regardless of whether the waypoints are purely classical or quantum. This constitutes a new type of computer, since the quantum runtime speedup holds for quantum and classical algorithms alike. We call it a
quantum frequential computer
.

Here we explore the possibility of using quantum properties in a different way in order to achieve a runtime speedup. Namely, rather than using quantum properties to reduce the gate count itself, we will aim to reduce the runtime by reducing the time required to apply each gate.

It is well-known that one can increase the gate frequency by increasing both the energy/power required to run the computer and the coupling strength between the control and logical bits/qubits. However, what had not been explored, is whether quantum effects in the control itself can speed up the application of gates, without an increase in power nor coupling strength. Here we prove that there is such a quantum advantage by using quantum squeezed states for the control of the application of logical gates. Moreover, we show that one can achieve a quadratic increase in frequency as a function of power consumed without increasing the interaction strength and within a completely autonomous model. The root cause of this phenomenon, as we will see, is that squeezed states effectively couple more strongly than their semi-classical counterparts. A key feature of this speed-up is that, unlike conventional quantum algorithmic runtime speed-ups, our quantum speed-up applies to all algorithms—quantum and classical ones alike. In
fig.

1
we compare the classical and conventional quantum computing paradigms to this new type of quantum computer paradigm.

I.3
Organisation of this
manuscript

Here is a high-level overview of what is achieved in each section of the main text. Note that
Sections

II
and
IV
are concerned with Hamiltonian dynamical models while
Sections

V
and
VI
develop dynamical semigroup models. As a general rule throughout the main text, we will start by demanding the most basic feature required for our results and steadily introduce more complexity as it is required. At each step, the added complexity will be motivated by a shortcoming highlighted in the previous result.

•

Section

II
:

–

Derives upper limits for gate frequency in both quantum and semi-classical systems that meet basic computational requirements.

–

These two upper bounds exhibit a quadratic separation as a function of initial energy.

–

This motivates the definition of a quantum frequential computer.

•

Section

III
:

–

Specializes the model from
Section

II
to ensure it meets additional key properties required for computation.

–

Demonstrates that within this specialized model, computers can saturate both bounds from
Section

II
.

–

Establishes the theoretical existence of optimal classical and optimal quantum frequential computers.

•

Section

IV
:

–

Introduces an additional architectural feature: an internal data bus for the computer with its own dedicated control.

–

This enhancement permits computation to be carried out over multiple cycles while requiring only semi-classical control for the bus—even in an optimal quantum frequential computer.

•

Section

V
:

–

Reformulates the quantum frequential computer from
Section

IV
within a dynamical semigroup framework allowing for computation in a nonequilibrium steady-state.

–

This formulation allows for the notion of power consumption and heat dissipation.

–

Shows that a quantum frequential computer can run indefinitely with a gate frequency proportional to its power consumption.

•

Section

VI
:

–

Establishes two upper bounds on gate frequency as a function of power consumption per cycle:

1.

In the case of a quantum frequential computer: Gate frequency is upper-bounded by the power consumed per cycle, confirming that the linear scaling found in
Section

V
is optimal.

2.

In the case of semi-classical control: Gate frequency is upper-bounded by the square root of power consumed per cycle.

–

Taken together, these results prove a quantum quadratic advantage for computation runtime versus power consumption.

•

Sections

VII
and
VIII
:

–

Conclude with a discussion, relation of the framework to previous frameworks, and final remarks.

II
Classical and quantum upper limits to computation as a function of energy

II.1
General Hamiltonian model and theorem

In this section, we derive upper bounds the gate frequency as a function of mean initial energy. The computer’s dynamics implements a total of
N_{g}\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
gates sequentially on
L
during a runtime
T_{0}\in{\mathbbm{R}_{>0}}
, with the
j{{}^{\text{th}}}
gate at time
t_{j}:=j\,T_{0}/N_{g}
. For simplicity, we assume that the gate frequency
f=N_{g}/T_{0}
is independent of which gates are being implemented and in the following, study the scenario that the computer is implementing a classical algorithm. As such, the computational logical space
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{L}}
passes through a sequence of orthogonal states at times
t_{1},t_{2}
,
\ldots,

t_{N_{g}}
. A reasonable quantum computer should also be able to implement said algorithms, and allowing for the possibility that some gates in the sequence do not map the logical register between orthogonal states, while reasonable, would not change significantly the results derived in this section. (We comment on why this is the case later in
Section

VII.13
.)

Our strategy for deriving generic bounds will be to see how accurately one can deduce the elapsed time
t\in[0,T_{0}]
by measuring the logical state of the computer mid computation. We will then use results from the field of metrology to obtain frequency-energy relations.
In our model, the computer’s dynamics is governed by a time-independent Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{Com}}
evolving unitarily from its initial state
\rho_{\textup{Com}}
during a time interval
[0,T_{0}]
,

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t):=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(t/T_{0})H_{\textup{Com}}T_{0}}\rho_{\textup{Com}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(t/T_{0})H_{\textup{Com}}T_{0}},\quad\text{ such that }\quad\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t_{j})\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t_{k})]=0\text{ if }j\neq k.

(II.1)

It is multipartite and includes the computational logical register on
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{L}}
. (The details of the other systems are not relevant for our theorem, but it is natural to assume that it also includes all the other subsystems required to run the computer, such as control systems, memory registers and batteries required). It may even include a thermal bath and hence thermodynamic effects.

For simplicity, and w.l.o.g., we define the ground state energy to be zero (Hamiltonians without a ground state are excluded by definition). The mean energy of the system is thus

\displaystyle E:=\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{Com}}\rho_{\textup{Com}}].

(II.2)

Since we are working in so-called natural units (i.e. “units where
\hbar=1
”),
E
has units of inverse time.

Our notion of semi-classical states is that of non-squeezed states, and we define the set
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{Com}}^{\textup{class.}}
as the set of all such states of the computer. (See
Section

A.3.1
for formal definition.) As one should expect, it is a strict subset of
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{Com}})
, the set of density matrices on
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{Com}}
.

Theorem 1

(Upper bounds on computational speed)
.

Consider any Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{Com}}
and initial state
\rho_{\textup{Com}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{Com}})
with finite initial mean energy
E
.
The gate frequency
f:=N_{g}/T_{0}
, satisfies the following upper bounds:

Case 1)

\displaystyle f\leq C_{\textit{SQL}}\sqrt{\frac{E}{T_{0}}},

(II.3)

if the initial state of the computer is semi-classical, namely if
\rho_{\textup{Com}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{Com}}^{\textup{class.}}
.

Case 2)

\displaystyle f\leq C_{\textit{HL}}E.

(II.4)

C_{\textit{SQL}}>0
and
C_{\textit{HL}}>0
are dimensionless constants.

The proof can be found in
appendix

B
. It uses tools from quantum metrology, and in the language of this discipline, bounds
II.3
and
II.4
can be thought of as a the “Standard Quantum Limit” and the “Heisenberg Limit” respectively. The proof is as follows: We measure the logical space in the computational basis at an unknown elapsed time between starting the computation at
t=0
and it finishing at runtime
T_{0}
. We then choose an estimator function, which estimates the unknown elapsed time (given our measurement outcome). The Cramér-Rao bound and its generalizations
[
28
,
47
]
, then put upper bounds on how precisely we can use this estimate to work out the time. It allows one to generate bounds on the gate frequency as a function of the available mean initial energy.

While
eq.

II.3
is, to the best of knowledge of the author, completely unknown,
eq.

II.4
follows directly from the so-called quantum speed limit. We discuss the relation of this
manuscript
as a whole to quantum speed limits in
Section

VII.13
.

Note that if
\rho_{\textup{Com}}
is a probabilistic ensemble over semi-classical (non-squeezed) states in
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{Com}}^{\textup{class.}}
, then bound
II.3
applies to each state in the ensemble. Moreover, from
eq.

II.2
it follows that
E
is simply the ensemble average of the mean energies of each state in the ensemble. Therefore, as one should intuitively expect, taking ensemble averages of semi-classical states cannot help to increase the frequency beyond that of the highest frequency achievable by the optimal state of the enable.

II.2
T_{0}
dependency

Let us now discuss the
T_{0}
dependency. Observe that
eq.

II.3
has explicit
T_{0}
dependency while
eq.

II.4
does not. Recall that, by definition,
H_{\textup{Com}}
is such that it implements gates sequentially at times
(t_{j})_{j=1}^{N_{g}}
. Therefore,
H_{\textup{Com}}
(and thus
E
), depend on
T_{0}
. However, observe that we can eliminate all explicit
T_{0}
dependency from the equation of motion,
eq.

II.1
, by writing said equation in terms of dimensionless time
t/T_{0}\in[0,1]
, and the dimensionless Hamiltonian,
\bar{H}_{\textup{Com}}
defined via
H_{\textup{Com}}=:\bar{H}_{\textup{Com}}/T_{0}
with
\bar{H}_{\textup{Com}}
independent of
T_{0}
.
1
1

1

While one could consider
\bar{H}_{\textup{Com}}
being dependent on the runtime
T_{0}
, such dependencies would be “artificial”, in the sense that if there exists
\bar{H}_{\textup{Com}}(T_{0})/T_{0}
, which is a solution to
eq.

II.1
for runtime
T_{0}>0
, then
H_{\textup{Com}}(x)/T_{0}
for any parameter
x>0
and runtime
T_{0}>0
, is also a solution to
eq.

II.1
. Similarly, we assume the initial state
\rho_{\textup{Com}}
to be
T_{0}
-independent because any such dependency would be “artificial”.
It thus follows that
T_{0}E
is
T_{0}
-independent. Thus writing the r.h.s. of
eqs.

II.3
and
II.4
in the form
\sqrt{E/T_{0}}=\sqrt{T_{0}E}/T_{0}
and
E=T_{0}E/T_{0}
respectively, we observe that both upper bounds have the same inverse dependency on
T_{0}
. For any dynamics given by a generator (such as
eq.

II.1
), this scaling with
T_{0}
is well-known. Physically, it corresponds to re-scaling time, i.e. “speeding up everything in equal measure”. In
Section

VII.1
we discuss why it is impractical to speed-up computation arbitrarily by simply decreasing
T_{0}
. In a similar vein, one could work in units where
T_{0}=1
analogously to how we are currently working in units where
\hbar=1
(note the absence of
\hbar
from
eq.

II.1
). However, to avoid confusion, we refrain from doing so in this
manuscript
. As will be made clear, all Hamiltonians in this
manuscript
which are constructed to saturate upper bounds, will be of this form, i.e. their sole
T_{0}
dependency will be that of a multiplicative factor of
1/T_{0}
.

II.3
Some questions and motivation for
Section

III

The bounds from
Theorem

1
naturally lead to a new question: is the linear and square-root scaling of
eqs.

II.3
and
II.4
actually achievable in the context of a model capable of universal (quantum or classical) computation?

It is known from
[
46
]
that the linear scaling of
eq.

II.4
is achievable, but it is unknown if it is achievable in the context of computation. Indeed, the abound
II.4
only demands that the logical space passes through a sequence of orthogonal states. It could, in principle, be saturable only for systems which cannot perform universal computation, such as a quantum system oscillating in a fixed basis. At a minimum, one needs to be able to apply any sequence of gates to the logical space where said sequence is read from a memory. As regards to
eq.

II.3
, it is not known if the bound is saturable at all, let alone in the context of universal computation.

We will answer both these questions in this
manuscript
going into substantial detail, starting in
Section

III
. Moreover, in anticipation of a positive answer to both these questions, let us introduce the following nomenclature.

II.4
Concept of a quantum frequential computer

We call a computer a
quantum frequential computer
when it uses distinctly quantum effects in the control (such as squeezing) to a achieve a gate-frequency scaling beyond what is possible with merely semi-classical control, namely super-square-root scaling. The name reflects the fact that the high-frequencies would not be achievable without quantum effects in the control. The nomenclature should not be taken to be a mathematical definition, and is intentionally a little vague. This is in analogy with the nomenclature
quantum computer
, where the exact boundary between classical and quantum computer is also a bit vague, and where a formal definition would not be helpful.

There are two main reasons not to simply refer to it as a quantum computer. On the one hand, the conventional notion of a quantum computer does not require quantum effects in the control. Second, even a computer solely capable of computing classical algorithms can have a quantum-enabled quadratic speed-up in its gate frequency if its control is quantum. However, it would be very confusing to refer to such a computer as a quantum computer even though its full description would require quantum theory.

Extending on the latter point, it is convenient to put quantum frequential computers into two classes: a
type-1 quantum frequential computer
is solely capable of universal classical computation, while a
type-2 quantum frequential computer
is capable of universal quantum computation.

Since we are dealing with Hamiltonian dynamical models, we thus far do not have a definition of power. As will be seen later in a more complete model, power is required for the computation to run forever in a steady-state. In such a scenario, the super-square-root scaling will be with respect to power consumed, not the mean energy in the system. These definitions will suffice in this
manuscript
since we will only discuss the asymptotic scaling behaviour, but more generally one can define a quantum frequential computer as any computer with quantum states whose gate-frequency-to-energy/power-consumption relation is unobtainable via classical or standard-quantum-limited states.

III
Existence of optimal quantum frequential computers

III.1
Specialised Hamiltonian model with control space, logical space and gate-sequence memory

We have seen how quantum metrology imposes upper bounds. We will now prove optimal type-1 and type-2 quantum frequential computers exist. We start by introducing a specialised model capable of universal classical and quantum computation. To achieve this, we will specialise the Hamiltonian generating the dynamics in
Theorem

1
to one with more structure. We will model explicitly the relevant 3 subsystems: 1) the physical or logical space on which the gate sequence is implemented; labelled
L
. (Since we will not consider error correction here, there is no requirement to distinguish between the logical and physical spaces.) 2) the memory space
\textup{M}_{0}
which stores the to-be-implemented gate sequence
(\mathbb{m}_{1},\mathbb{m}_{2},\ldots,\mathbb{m}_{N_{g}})
, where each element belongs to an alphabet
\mathcal{G}
.
2
2

2

To avoid ambiguities stemming from notation, we assume
\mathcal{G}
to not contain purely numeric symbols nor symbols of the form
t_{x}
where
x
is any symbol.
3) the control space
C
, which will encode the degrees of freedom of the system which implements the gate sequence.

For every element
\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}
, there is a corresponding unitary
U(\mathbb{m})
on
L
. The set of these
\{U(\mathbb{m})\,|\,\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}\}=:\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{G}}
is the gate set and is of finite cardinality but otherwise arbitrary. As such, the model can accommodate both universal classical and universal quantum computing. (Note that for simplicity, we have referred to both
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{G}}
as “gate set”. The former refers to their symbolic representation, while the latter to the maps themselves. It will be clear from the context and notation which one we are referring to.)

We assume the memory,
\textup{M}_{0}
, is formed by
N_{g}\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
local memory cells, i.e.
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{0}}=\mathcal{C}_{\textup{Cell}}^{\otimes N_{g}}
, where each cell is of the dimension of the gate set:
\textup{Dim}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{Cell}})=\big|\mathcal{G}\big|
. Its initial state,
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
, belongs to the set representing the memory states corresponding to all possible gate sequences the computer can implement:
3
3

3

We omit tensor products with the identity and tensor product symbols between kets and bras by convention. Unless stated otherwise, we also refrain from using the common partial trace convention, i.e.
O_{\textup{B}}:\neq\textup{tr}_{\textup{A}}O_{\textup{AB}}
for operators
O_{\textup{AB}}
over a bipartite system.

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{0}}:=\big\{\ket{\mathbb{m}_{1}}_{\textup{M}_{0,1}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{2}}_{\textup{M}_{0,2}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{3}}_{\textup{M}_{0,3}}\ldots\ket{\mathbb{m}_{N_{g}}}_{\textup{M}_{0,N_{g}}}\,\,\big|\,\,\mathbb{m}_{1},\mathbb{m}_{2},\ldots,\mathbb{m}_{N_{g}}\in\mathcal{G}\big\}

(III.1)

where
\{\ket{\mathbb{m}_{l}}_{\textup{M}_{0,l}}\}_{\mathbb{m}_{l}\in\mathcal{G}}
forms an orthonormal basis for the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
memory cell. These states are classical in the sense that there is no coherence in the above basis. See
fig.

2
for a diagrammatic illustration of the setup.

Figure 2:
Diagram of the different systems involved: the memory
\textup{M}_{0}
, the logical space
L
, and control
C
. The logical space
L
, is further divided into sub-register spaces, as in a conventional quantum or classical computation register (this substructure only enters indirectly via the to-be-defined-later values of
\tilde{d}({\mathbb{m}})
,
\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}
).

Let
t_{j}:=jT_{0}/N_{g}
,
(j=0,1,2,\ldots,N_{g})
be the time necessary to implement the first
j
gates on
L
, so that
T_{0}>0
is the total time required for the computation (i.e. to implement all
N_{g}
gates sequentially). Likewise, we denote the state on
L
at time
t_{j}
by

\displaystyle\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{L}}:=U(\mathbb{m}_{j})U(\mathbb{m}_{j-1})\ldots U(\mathbb{m}_{1})\ket{0}_{\textup{L}},

(III.2)

where the initial state,
\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{L}})
, with
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{L}})
the set of normalised pure states on
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{L}}
.
The corresponding gate frequency is

\displaystyle f:=\frac{N_{g}}{T_{0}}.

(III.3)

We do not impose any further structure on the control state on
C
at time
t_{j}
other than it being a tensor product state with the rest of the computer. As such, in an ideal scenario, the total state of the computer at time
t_{j}
(for
j=0,1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
) is

\displaystyle\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}.

(III.4)

The question we will want to answer is with what frequency
f
can the gates be implemented as a function of available energy. To do so we introduce a family of Hamiltonians over
\textup{L},\textup{M}_{0}
and
C
and then ask how well Hamiltonians from this family can mimic the dynamics of the states of the computer we have introduced at times
\{t_{j}\}_{j}
.

These time-independent Hamiltonians have a particular structure, namely

\displaystyle H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}=H_{\textup{C}}+\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}}I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)},

(III.5)

where we use subscripts
{\textup{M}_{0}}
,
L
,
C
to indicate which subsystems the individual terms act upon.
H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
is self-adjoint and has a ground state energy of zero. While the Hamiltonians
H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
can depend on
\mathcal{G}
, they cannot depend on the initial memory state
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
. This is because we want the gate sequence we wish to implement to be encoded in
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
and
not
the Hamiltonian. Since the state of the memory here will not change during the computation, one could in principle have considered a model where the gate sequence were encoded directly into the Hamiltonian. However, in later sections we will consider models where the gate sequence is updated in real time during the computation. In this case, the Hamiltonian would be time dependent. Since we are investigating the fundamental frequencies at which gates can be applied, we need to consider time independent Hamiltonians and as such one should encode the gate sequence information
(\mathbb{m}_{1},\mathbb{m}_{2},\ldots)
into states, rather than into the Hamiltonian. We also impose the constraint that
T_{0}H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
is
T_{0}
-independent, for the reasons discussed in
Section

II.2
.

The control system
C
requires a non-trivial free Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{C}}
since the control state is permitted to freely evolve. Conversely, the initial state of the memory,
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
, does not evolve—hence the absence of a term of the form
H_{\textup{M}_{0}}
. It does however require interaction terms with the physical space,
\{I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}}^{(l)}\}_{l}
in order to be read. Similarly, there is no free Hamiltonian on the logical space, since the only evolution comes from the application of gates in
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{G}}
. The
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
interaction term,
I_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}}^{(l)}
, only acts non-trivially on
L
and
\textup{M}_{0,l}
. As such, we can associate the presence of
I_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}}^{(l)}
with the application of
U(\mathbb{m}_{l})
on
L
.

The Hamiltonians
H_{\textup{C}}
have a discrete spectrum which forms a basis
\{\ket{E_{n}}_{\textup{C}}\}_{n}
. The terms
\{I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
are diagonal in the discrete Fourier transform basis generated from
\{\ket{E_{n}}_{\textup{C}}\}_{n}
.

As before, we define the energy for this model as

\displaystyle E_{0}:=\textup{tr}[\rho^{0}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}],

(III.6)

where
\rho^{0}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
is the density matrix for the initial state (denoted
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}
) and is
T_{0}
-independent for the reasons discussed in
Section

II
(i.e. so that
T_{0}E_{0}
is
T_{0}
-independent).
For the following theorems, it is convenient to introduce the set of classical states of the control
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{clas.}}
: this is the class of non-squeezed states with respect to the free control Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{C}}
and its conjugate Hermitian operator
t_{\textup{C}}

(
which is diagonal in the same basis as operators
\{I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}

)
. In other words, the control states in
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{clas.}}
have equal uncertainty with respect to both operators (up to normalization and vanishing corrections in the large
E_{0}
limit). See
Section

A.3.2
for their full definitions.

For the following theorem we introduce some notation: For
\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}
,
\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m})
is the number of distinct eigenvalues of
U(\mathbb{m})
(which we assume to have point spectrum). We use
T(\ket{A},\ket{B})
to denote the trace distance between two normalised kets
\ket{A}
,
\ket{B}
. As such, in the following theorem the term
T\big(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j}H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}},\,\,\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\big)
can be understood colloquially as the error in running the computer up to time
t_{j}
.

Throughout the main text, we use the notation

\displaystyle{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(\cdot):{\mathbbm{R}_{>0}}\to{\mathbbm{R}_{>0}}

(III.7)

to describe a function which decays faster than any polynomial. Specifically, the two defining properties of the class of functions it belongs to are: for every real polynomial
{poly}(\cdot)
, there exists
\bar{\varepsilon}^{\prime}>0
such that for all
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}^{\prime})

1)

Super polynomial decay
:

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to\infty}poly(x)\,{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(x)=0.

(III.8)

2)

Increasing decay with decreasing
\bar{\varepsilon}
:

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(x)}{{{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}}\,}}(x)}=0.

(III.9)

For fixed input
x
, the functions
{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(x)
may diverge in the limit
\bar{\varepsilon}\to 0^{+}
; hence the need to define them in terms of the parameter
\bar{\varepsilon}
. For simplicity of expression, and with a slight abuse of notation, we will use the same notation
{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(\cdot)
when referring to different functions in said class.

The functions
{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(\cdot)
are independent of the elements in
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m})\}_{\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}}
(which will be defined later).

III.2
Theorem statement and motivation for
Section

IV

Theorem 2

(Optimal conventional and quantum frequential computers exist)
.

For all gate sets
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{G}}
, initial memory states
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
and initial logical states
\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{L}})
, there exists triplets
\{\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\}_{j=0}^{N_{g}}
,
N_{g}
,
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
parametrised by the energy
E_{0}>0
and a dimensionless parameter
\bar{\epsilon}
, such that for all
j=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
and fixed
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
the large-
E_{0}
scaling is as follows

\displaystyle T\Big(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j}H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}},\,\,\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\Big)\leq\left(\sum_{k=1}^{j}\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})\right){\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(E_{0}),

(III.10)

for the following two cases:

Case 1)

\displaystyle f=\frac{1}{T_{0}}\left({T_{0}}E_{0}\right)^{1/2-\bar{\varepsilon}}+\delta f,\qquad|\delta f|\leq\frac{1}{T_{0}}+{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(E_{0})

(III.11)

and
\big(\,\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\,\big)_{j=0}^{N_{g}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{clas.}}
.

Case 2)

\displaystyle f=\frac{1}{T_{0}}\left(T_{0}E_{0}\right)^{1-\bar{\varepsilon}}+\delta f^{\prime},\qquad|\delta f^{\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{T_{0}}+{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(E_{0})

(III.12)

The proof can be found in
Appendix

C
. It is by construction. In the proof, the parametrization of triplets
\{\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\}_{j=0}^{N_{g}}
,
N_{g}
,
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
is defined explicitly in terms of
\bar{\varepsilon}
, while its parametrization in terms of
E_{0}
is implicitly defined via
eq.

III.6
.

Thus, up to an error in trace distance which decays faster than any polynomial,
Theorem

2
shows the following. Case 1)
Theorem

2
: There exists both conventional quantum and classical computers which saturate the optimal semi-classical control scaling (i.e that saturate the scaling in
eq.

II.3
). Case 2)
Theorem

2
: There exists optimal quantum frequential computers, either of type 1 or 2, (i.e that saturate the scaling in
eq.

II.4
).

The coefficients
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})\}_{k}
only depend on the number of qubits in
L
which the gate
U(\mathbb{m}_{k})
acts non-trivially on. E.g. if
U(\mathbb{m}_{k})
is a 2-bit/qubit gate, then
\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})\leq 4
. It is important that the error only grows with the number of qubits the gates act upon, rather than the total dimension
d_{\textup{L}}
of the computation space
L
, which could be orders of magnitude larger. Note that the units in
eqs.

III.11
and
III.12
are those of frequency as required since in this
manuscript
we are using units such that
\hbar=1
.

From
Theorem

2
it also follows how
N_{g}
scales with
E_{0}
. In case 1)
N_{g}=T_{0}f\sim\sqrt{T_{0}E_{0}}
as
E_{0}\to\infty
, while in case 2)
N_{g}=T_{0}f\sim T_{0}E_{0}
as
E_{0}\to\infty
. This might be undesirable from a physical standpoint, since one may wish to increase the total number of gates
N_{g}
to be implemented at a give frequency
f
, without needing to increase the frequency itself. The reason behind this is because in our construction, every interaction term
I_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}
is only “used” once, to implement one gate (since the number of interaction terms and number of gates are equal). Such a setup would be highly wasteful from an engineering perspective. The underlying reason can be traced back to the fact that each interaction term only reads one memory cell.

One way to circumvent this shortcoming, would be to partition the gate sequence
\mathbb{m}_{1},\mathbb{m}_{2},\mathbb{m}_{3},\ldots
one wishes to implement into sequences of length
N_{g}
, run the computer with the
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
partition in the initial memory state
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
and then reset the control state to its initial state
\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}
at time
t_{N_{g}}
and
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
to the state which encodes the next partition. Then run the computer again. This however would require external control and thus unforeseeable costs, including, potentially, a drop in frequency and or more energy.

IV
Quantum frequential computers only require a semi-classical internal bus

IV.1
Motivation

In this section we will show how to extend the setup from the previous section to overcome the shortcoming discussed in the previous two paragraphs. Moreover, we will show that even when we are operating in the quantum frequential computer regime, we only require additional classical resources to overcome these aforementioned difficulties. In other words, these additional classical resources in total will only require the same initial mean energy as the quantum ones, thus their addition will not change how the gate frequency of the quantum frequential computer scales with total initial mean energy.

To achieve this we will add to the setup an additional memory and control system which together can be thought of as an “internal bus” which will refresh the memory cells in
\textup{M}_{0}
at an appropriate rate. We will show that an optimal quantum frequential computer (either of type 1 or 2), only requires an internal bus whose control state is semi-classical, and requires the same amount of initial energy as the quantum control system on
C
. In light of
Theorem

2
and the upper bounds from
Section

II
, this may seem a priori surprising, since this additional control system has to perform the same number
N_{g}
of unitaries as the control on
C
in the same time interval
T_{0}
. We will give an intuitive explanation of why this is the case later in
Section

VII.5
.

IV.2
Specialised Hamiltonian model with control space, logical space, gate-sequence memory, and internal data bus

Let us now introduce the additional computer architecture associated with this new setup. As with
Section

III
, we will define the exact states of the computer at times
t_{j}
just after the
j{{}^{\text{th}}}
gate has been applied, and later see how fast (as a function of initial mean energy) they can be reached under the dynamics of a time-independent Hamiltonian up to a small error.

We consider the setup in which the total number of logical gates implemented sequentially is
N_{G}=LN_{g}
within a total runtime of the computer of
(L+1)T_{0}
,
L\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
. The gate frequency is
f=N_{G}/LT_{0}=N_{g}/T_{0}
(since in the initial time interval
[0,T_{0}]
the gates applied are not computational logic gates in
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{G}}
). We denote by
t_{j,l}
the time at which the
(j+lN_{g}){{}^{\text{th}}}
gate is applied:
t_{j,l}:=t_{j+lN_{g}}=t_{k}+lT_{0}
(
j=0,1,2,\ldots,N_{g};\,l=0,1,2,\ldots,L
),
L\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
. The computer goes through
L+1
“cycles” (called
0{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle,
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
cycle,
2{{}^{\text{nd}}}
cycle, …) during the computation by which it is meant that the state on
C
is periodic with a period
T_{0}
, so that

\displaystyle\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{C}}=\ket{t_{j,0}}_{\textup{C}},

(IV.1)

for all
l=0,1,2,\ldots,L
and
j=0,1,\ldots,N_{g}
. As such,
T_{0}
is called the cycle time.
4
4

4

In the model of
Section

III
it was the runtime because the computer only ran for one cycle.

With the exception of the
0{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle, the computer will run analogously to that of the previous section during each cycle, but with the memory
\textup{M}_{0}
refreshed so that it implements a new gate sequence on every cycle. The quantum frequential computer will implement a logical gate sequence with elements in
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{G}}
, according to the sequence
(\mathbb{m}_{1,1}
,
\mathbb{m}_{1,2}
,
\ldots
,
\mathbb{m}_{1,N_{g}}
,
\mathbb{m}_{2,1}
,
\mathbb{m}_{2,2}
,
\ldots,

\mathbb{m}_{2,N_{g}}
,
\mathbb{m}_{3,1}
,
\ldots
,
\mathbb{m}_{3,N_{g}}
,
\ldots,

\mathbb{m}_{L,1},

\mathbb{m}_{L,2}
,
\ldots
,
\mathbb{m}_{L,N_{g}})
with elements in
\mathcal{G}
. The logical space will remain unchanged during the
0{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle:
\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}=\ket{t_{k,0}}_{\textup{L}}
,
k=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
, and the gate sequence is fully implemented on it over the subsequent cycles: For
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
;
l=1,2,\ldots,L

\displaystyle\ket{t_{k,l}}_{\textup{L}}:=U(\mathbb{m}_{l,k})U(\mathbb{m}_{l,k-1})U(\mathbb{m}_{l,k-2})\ldots U(\mathbb{m}_{2,2})U(\mathbb{m}_{2,1})U(\mathbb{m}_{1,N_{g}})\ldots U(\mathbb{m}_{1,3})U(\mathbb{m}_{1,2})U(\mathbb{m}_{1,1})\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}.

(IV.2)

The system responsible for refreshing the memory is what we call the internal bus. It has
N_{g}
bus lanes, where the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
lane is responsible for refreshing the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
memory cell on
\textup{M}_{0}
. All bus lanes cannot be turned on at once, but instead need to be turned on in a staggered manner over the
0{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle. This requires a switch space
W
consisting in
N_{g}
switches—one per lane. Each switch state can be in an “on” or “off” state at time
t_{k,l}
:

\displaystyle\ket{t_{k,l}}_{\textup{W}}:=\ket{t_{k,l}}_{\textup{W}_{1}}\ket{t_{k,l}}_{\textup{W}_{2}}\ldots\ket{t_{k,l}}_{\textup{W}_{N_{g}}},\qquad\ket{t_{k,l}}_{\textup{W}_{j}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{W}_{j}}:=\{\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{j}},\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{j}}\},

(IV.3)

where
\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{j}}
,
\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{j}}
form an orthonormal basis for the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{W}_{j}}
. The switches are initiated to the all-off state:
\ket{0}_{\textup{W}}:=\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{1}}\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{2}}\ldots\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{N_{g}}}
.
Similarly, the memory cells on
\textup{M}_{0}
are initialised to a state
{\bf 0}\notin\mathcal{G}
,

\displaystyle\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}:=\ket{{\bf 0}}_{\textup{M}_{0,1}}\ket{{\bf 0}}_{\textup{M}_{0,2}}\ldots\ket{{\bf 0}}_{\textup{M}_{0,N_{g}}},

(IV.4)

where
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
is orthogonal to all states in
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
. At later times, some of the memory cells in
\textup{M}_{0}
will have changed to take on values in
\mathcal{G}
, and as such the elements of
\big\{\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\big\}_{j,l}
belong to
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{0}}^{\prime}:={\mathcal{C}_{\textup{Cell}}^{\prime}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\otimes N_{g}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{Cell}}^{\prime}=\big\{\ket{\mathbb{m}}\big\}_{\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{{\bf 0}\}}
. We want to describe a scenario where the state
\ket{{\bf 0}}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}}
informs the control on
C
to turn the
k{{}^{\text{th}}}
switch from the off state,
\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}
, to the on state
\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}
. Moreover, we will now assume that the control system on
C
reads memory cell
{\textup{M}_{0,k}}
and implements the corresponding gate on
L
or
W
over a time interval
t\in[t_{k-1,l},t_{k,l}]
. Thus all switches are turned on by the end of the
0{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle and are not turned off again during the subsequent cycles. Explicitly, and defining
\ket{t_{k,0}}_{\textup{W}}
as the state of the switch at time
t_{k,0}
:

\displaystyle\ket{t_{k,0}}_{\textup{W}}:=\left[\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{1}}\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{2}}\ldots\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{k+1}}\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{k+2}}\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{k+3}}\ldots\right]_{N_{g}},

(IV.5)

for
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
and where the notation
[\,\cdot\,]_{N_{g}}
indicates that only the first
N_{g}
kets in the sequence are kept. In later cycles, the switches stay in their on position. I.e., for
k=0,1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
;
r=1,2,\ldots,L

\displaystyle\ket{t_{N_{g},0}}_{\textup{W}}=\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{W}}:=\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{1}}\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{2}}\ldots\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{N_{g}}}.

(IV.6)

In the
2{{}^{\text{nd}}}
cycle, the aim is for the control on
C
of the quantum frequential computer to implement the first
N_{g}
gates in the gate sequence. For this, we need the internal bus, with control space
\textup{C}_{2}
, to exchange the state on
\textup{M}_{0}
in
eq.

IV.4
for one containing the gate sequence for the first
N_{g}
logical gates, namely
\mathbb{m}_{1,1},\mathbb{m}_{1,2},\mathbb{m}_{1,3},\ldots,\mathbb{m}_{1,N_{g}}
. Similarly, in the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle, the control on
C
should implement the gates corresponding to the sequence
\mathbb{m}_{l,1},\mathbb{m}_{l,2},\mathbb{m}_{l,3},\ldots,\mathbb{m}_{l,N_{g}}
.

Each of the
N_{g}
internal bus lanes control their own memory block. In particular, the initial state of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
memory block,
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}
, is a tensor-product state encoding a partition of the to-be-implemented gate sequence and thus belongs to the set:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}:=\big\{\ket{{\bf 0}}_{\textup{M}_{0,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{2,l}}_{\textup{M}_{2,l}}\ldots\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L,l}}\,\big|\,\mathbb{m}_{1,l},\mathbb{m}_{2,l},\ldots,\mathbb{m}_{L,l}\in\mathcal{G}\,\big\},

(IV.7)

l=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
. Thus collectively, the initial state of the entire memory,
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}:=\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},1}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},2}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},3}}\ldots\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},N_{g}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},1}}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},2}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},N_{g}}}=:\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}}
encodes the entire logical gate sequence which is to be implemented on
L
. See
fig.

3
for an illustration of the subsystems involved in their initial-state configuration.

While the memory
\textup{M}_{0}
is initially in a product state over the distinct memory cells, since the control on
\textup{C}_{2}
will be operating at a much lower frequency than that of the logical gate implementation,
f=N_{g}L/(T_{0}L)=N_{g}/T_{0}
, it need not maintain its product-state nature at times
\{t_{k,l}\}_{k,l}
, (
k\neq 0
and
l\neq 0
). Intuitively, one may think of the state at these times as containing some memory elements
\{\mathbb{m}_{k,l}\}_{k,l}
which are in the process of being read/written to by the bus. Consequently, while initially the memory and control on
\textup{C}_{2}
will be in product-state form,
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
, they will be described by a non-product state a later times:
\{\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\}_{j,l}
. Nonetheless, it is important that the memory state being read during the interval
[t_{k-1,l},t_{k,l}]
by the control on
C
is in the appropriate state so that the gate sequence
eq.

IV.2
is implemented. Denoting
\ket{t}_{\textup{M}_{l,k}}
the state of the cell
\textup{M}_{l,k}
at time
t
; we thus require,

\displaystyle\ket{t}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}}=\ket{\mathbb{m}_{l,k}}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}},

(IV.8)

for all
t\in[t_{k-1,l},t_{k,l}]
, and
l=1,2,3,\ldots,L
;
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
. Note that this condition also necessitates that the state of the memory cell on
\textup{M}_{0,k}
at time
t\in[t_{k-1,l},t_{k,l}]
is a product state with the other systems. Moreover, it allows one to define the bus frequency
f_{\textup{bus}}
as the frequency at which each bus lane re-freshens the memory
\textup{M}_{0}
with new information about the logical gate sequence. From
eq.

IV.8
we see that this frequency, (i.e. the inverse time between updates of the memory cell on
\textup{M}_{0,k}
) is bus-lane independent (i.e.
k
-independent), and given by

\displaystyle f_{\textup{bus}}=\frac{1}{T_{0}}.

(IV.9)

Figure 3:
a)
Schematic of the computer’s architecture. The memory
M
and switches
W
are shown in their initial product states. The cells which the
k{{}^{\text{th}}}
bus lane can read and write to are highlighted in blue while the cells in
\textup{M}_{0}
on which logical gates
U(\mathbb{m})
,
\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}
are controlled on is depicted in orange. The
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
bus lane can copy and write to memory cells in memory block
\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}
and can be turned on or off via changing the state of the switch in
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{W}_{l}}
(which is located directly above the bus lane in the figure). During the
0{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle, the
{\bf 0}
instructions in
\textup{M}_{0}
are read, informing the control on
C
to turn the switches from off to on in a staggered fashion—No other change is present in
M
, and no computation is performed.
In the subsequent
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle, logical gates corresponding to the sequence
\mathbb{m}_{l,1}
,
\mathbb{m}_{l,2}
,
\ldots
,
\mathbb{m}_{l,N_{g}}
is implemented hence performing computation.

FIG. 3.
b)
A snapshot of the computer’s memory at time
t\in[t_{k-1,l},t_{k,l}]
,
l>0
(subscript
x
in
\mathbb{m}_{x,k}
is mod
L
and
\mathbb{m}_{0,k}:={\bf 0}
.). Memory cell
\textup{M}_{0,k}
stores the appropriate gate sequence information for the gate being applied during time
[t_{k-1,l},t_{k,l}]
, while other memory cells in other bus lanes (denoted by a question mark) are not necessarily in definite states in
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}
(the information is in transit between memory cells via the bus in preparation for later times).

As in
Section

IV
, we wish to find a time-independent Hamiltonian whose induced dynamics reproduces the above-described states at times
\{t_{j,l}\}_{l,j}
up to a small error, and at a particular gate frequency and mean energy. The locality structure of our new Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}

\displaystyle:=H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}+H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}},\quad H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}:=H_{\textup{C}_{2}}+\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}}I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)},\quad H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}:=H_{\textup{C}}+\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}}I_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{L}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)},

(IV.10)

where
I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(l)}
only acts non-trivially on memory cells and switch states on
\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}\textup{W}_{l}
i.e.—the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
bus lane and its corresponding on/off switch. The Hamiltonian
H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
is identical to those described in
eq.

III.5
up to the interaction terms
\{I_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{L}}^{(l)}\}
having additional support on the switch space—this is so that it can implement the turning on/off of the switches in the
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
cycle. The total Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}
is self-adjoint and has a ground state energy of zero.
As with Hamiltonians
eq.

III.5
, the Hamiltonians in
eq.

IV.10
can depend on
\mathcal{G}
but are independent of the initial memory state in
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}}
and are such that
T_{0}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}
is
T_{0}
-independent. Recall sections
Section

II
and
Section

III
for motivation.

Analogously to the pair
H_{\textup{C}}
,
\{I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\}_{l}
, the terms
\{I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)}\}_{l}
are diagonal in the discrete Fourier Transform basis generated from the energy-eigenbasis of
H_{\textup{C}_{2}}
.

As before, the energy is defined as the total initial state average energy

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\prime}:=\textup{tr}[\rho^{0}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}],

(IV.11)

where
\rho^{0}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}
is the density matrix for state
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\ket{0}_{\textup{W}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
and is
T_{0}
-independent by definition for the reasons discussed in
Section

II
(i.e. so that
T_{0}E_{0}^{\prime}
is
T_{0}
-independent).
Analogously to how we defined the set of semi-classical states for the control on
C
, we need to define the set of classical states for the control of the bus on
\textup{C}_{2}
as the set of non-squeezed states: the set
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{\textup{clas.}}
is the set of minimum-uncertainty states which share the same standard deviation with respect to
H_{\textup{C}_{2}}
and a canonically conjugate operator
t_{\textup{C}_{2}}
(up to normalization and vanishing corrections in the large
E_{0}
limit). See
Section

A.3.3
for their full definitions.

IV.3
Some clarifications

Importantly, while we plan to implement a total of
LN_{g}
logical gates sequentially, the Hamiltonian only has
2N_{g}
interaction terms. Since
L
will be large, this means we are “re-using” each interaction term many times during the computation which is far more efficient from an engineering perspective.

Note that the terms
H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
and
H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}
do not commute in general since they both act non-trivially on memory cells
\textup{M}_{0}
and switches
W
. Physical speaking, this is because
H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
generates the dynamics for performing gates on
L
and
W
(controlled on the state of
\textup{M}_{0}
), while
H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}
generates the dynamics to write to
\textup{M}_{0}
the necessary memory cells from
\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},1},\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},2},\ldots,\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},L}
; (controlling these operations on the state of
W
, i.e. controlled on whether the switches are on or off).

One may wonder why we included switch bits and did not simply use a simpler setup where the bus lanes are always on. The reason for including a switch state on
\textup{W}_{j}
(for turning on/off the control of the memory block on
\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},j}
), is because of initial-condition requirements. In particular, in our construction, we need to turn on the memory blocks
\{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},j}\}_{j}
sequentially to avoid malfunction. It is expected (we will not prove this here) that if the states on
\textup{C}_{2}
where quantum, that these switch bits would not be required and all bus lanes could always be on. However, they only add a relatively small overhead to the computational architecture and, as we will see, will permit us to use far fewer quantum resources to obtain the same performance—see
Section

VII.5
for a longer discussion.

IV.4
Theorem statement and motivation for
Section

V

In the following, we also extend the definition of
\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m})
for
\mathbb{m}\notin\mathcal{G}
:
\tilde{d}({\bf 0})=2
. What is more,
{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(E_{0}^{\prime})
is independent of the elements in
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m})\}_{\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{{\bf 0}\}}
.

Theorem 3

(Optimal quantum frequential computers only require a semi-classical internal bus)
.

For all gate sets
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{G}}
, initial memory states
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}}
and initial logical states
\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{L}})
, there exists
\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}
,
\ket{0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
,

\{\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{C}},\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\}_{j=1,2,\ldots,N_{g};\,l=0,1,\ldots,L}
,
N_{g}
,
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
parametrised by the energy
E_{0}^{\prime}>0
and a dimensionless parameter
\bar{\varepsilon}
(where elements
\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{C}}
,
\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}
satisfy
eqs.

IV.1
and
IV.8
respectively), such that for all
j=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
;
l=0,1,2,\ldots,L
and fixed
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
, the large-
E_{0}^{\prime}
scaling is as follows

\displaystyle T\Big(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j,l}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{0}_{\textup{W}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}},\,\,\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{C}}\Big)

(IV.12)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})\right){\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(E_{0}^{\prime}),

(IV.13)

where
\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
,
\textup{tr}_{\textup{M}}\big[\ket{t_{j,l}}\!\!\bra{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\big]\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{\textup{clas.}}
, and

\displaystyle f=\frac{1}{T_{0}}\left(T_{0}E_{0}^{\prime}\right)^{1-\bar{\varepsilon}}+\delta f^{\prime\prime},\qquad|\delta f^{\prime\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{T_{0}}+{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(E_{0}^{\prime})

(IV.14)

Thus, up to an error in trace distance which decays faster than any polynomial, we have that an optimal quantum frequential computer (either of type 1 or 2) can run over many cycles while only requiring a semi-classical bus.

The dependency on the cycle time
T_{0}
, is analogous to that of the previous sections—recall discussion in
Section

II.2
. As regards to the dependency on
L
, since
E_{0}^{\prime}
is
L
-independent,
L
can be increased without changing the gate frequency
f
(this is what one should expect, since
L
is the total number of cycles the computer runs through). Meanwhile, since
\left(\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})\right)
scales, at most, linearly in
L
(since
\mathcal{G}
is a finite set), the error, characterized by the r.h.s. of
eq.

IV.12
, only increases linearly with
L
while it decreases faster than any polynomial in
E_{0}^{\prime}
. As such, the quantum frequential computer can run over many cycles before errors become intolerable. More precisely, by setting
\bar{\varepsilon}
small enough, the number of cycles can increase arbitrarily fast as a function of energy:
L\sim{E_{0}^{\prime}}^{1/\sqrt{2\bar{\varepsilon}}}
, and the r.h.s. of
eq.

IV.12
still converges to zero for large
E_{0}^{\prime}
. However, in said limit, the initial state also tends to infinite energy, and after
L+1
cycles, would be degraded and would require renewing. This renewal would itself cost resources such as energy, among other things. As such, while this limit is in-principle physical,
E_{0}^{\prime}
in this case should not be considered as capturing the total cost. The principle goal of the next section is to remedy this.

V
Nonequilibrium steady-state dynamics, power consumption and heat dissipation

V.1
Motivation and the concept of self-oscillation

Theorems

2
and
3
illustrate how computation can be formulated in a Hamiltonian dynamics picture with finite energy. However, we have seen that the errors in the computation build up over time, and at some point would cause a malfunction. At least in theory this is a priori not a problem: since in practice all algorithms one runs terminate in finite time, one could simply reset the oscillators to their initial state at the end of the computation. One could then simply quantify the costs associated with the reset process. However, this is not how conventional computers work; indeed, they work in a nonequilibrium steady-state configuration where the computer’s clock is a self-oscillator
[
34
]
. The advantages of this is that self-oscillators automatically stabilise themselves leading to the above-mentioned nonequilibrium steady-state configuration where computation can (in principle) run indefinitely.

The physics of classical and semi-classical self-oscillators is well studied
[
34
,
9
,
62
,
69
,
13
,
12
]
, but the case of an oscillator in a non-classical state implementing gates, such as in the case of a quantum frequential computer in unknown. An important question is whether a quantum frequential computer can also run in a nonequilibrium steady-state where the oscillators are stabilised.

Since nonequilibrium steady-states can be formulated as open quantum systems, energy can be exchanged between the system and its environment. In this section, we will show how the need for power consumption arises naturally in this nonequilibrium stead-state setting. We will then show how, in the case of a quantum frequential computer, the linear relationship between gate frequency and energy of the Hamiltonian formulations in previous sections, is replaced with a linear relationship between gate frequency and power consumption. (The fact that this linear relationship is optimal, and that the quadratic quantum advantage shown in previous sections is maintained, will be proven in
Section

VI
.)

Since, on cycle average, the mean energy of the internal state of the computer remains constant, the power flowing into the computer must be dissipated back to the environment, so the role of the environment can be thought of as an entropy sink— similarly to how quantum error correction can be understood as a special kind of heat engine removing entropy from the logical space
[
60
]
.

Figure 4:
Qualitative illustration of representative dynamics of the oscillator on
C
in quadrature space over 3 cycles (
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
cycle in blue,
2{{}^{\text{nd}}}
in red,
3{{}^{\text{rd}}}
in purple). Arrows indicate direction of dynamics over time.

FIG. 4. ​
a)
State of the oscillator from
Section

III
: At the end of each cycle (of duration
T_{0}
) the oscillator gets close to the state it started in when initiating the cycle. In this way, small errors accumulate over many cycles ultimately leading to an intolerable error
E_{R}
. While we see from
Theorem

3
that said errors decrease rapidly with increasing power, for any given power, they eventually become large after sufficiently many cycles.

FIG. 4.
b)
State of the oscillator from
Section

V
: With high probability, the oscillator is renewed to its initial state at the end of each cycle, leading to a quantum frequential computer in a nonequilibrium steady-state. Observe that the dynamics during each cycle are not identical, due to differing perturbations caused by the implementation of different gate sequences in each cycle. As such, this renewal cannot be unique: it must map many states to the same initial state, correcting for these small errors towards the end of each cycle to prevent them becoming large over many cycles. This many-to-one nature is a form of self-error correction. And, as we will see, it requires work to be done on it to run—similarly to Landauer’s erasure principle or quantum error correction.

V.2
Specialised self-oscillating model (with control space, logical space, gate-sequence memory, and internal data bus)

We now describe the mathematical framework for this section. We use an open quantum system to stabilise the oscillator on
C
which is driving the computation. We will also have a bus playing the same role as in
Section

IV
. This is mainly because we wish the computation to be performed over many cycles. However, since we have already proved in
Theorem

3
that the bus can operate at the semi-classical limit, even in the case of an optimal quantum frequential computer, we will not need to model it explicitly this time around. This is because the physics of stable classical oscillators is well understood. In particular, a class of so-called self-oscillators are stable under small perturbations and thus when used for the bus in our protocol, would be stabilised. However, since these stabilization protocols are for classical oscillators, it is not clear if our quantum oscillator on
C
can also be autonomously stabilized while also implementing logical gates, and if so, at what cost.

We model the dynamics of the computer and its interaction with the environment via a dynamical semigroup.

Before we explain each term of said dynamical semigroup, let us pause for a moment to garner intuition from the Hamiltonian model of a quantum frequential computer in
Section

IV
about the properties said dynamical semigroup should posses: in the Hamiltonian model, the state of the oscillator on
C
at times
t_{0,l}=lT_{0}
is close to (but not equal to) its initial state,
\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}
. These deviations grow with increasing
l
; see
fig.

4
a) for an illustration. The origin of these deviations can be understood as a consequence of back-action on the control due to the implementation of gates, since when the interaction terms responsible for gate implementation,
\{I_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{L}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\}_{l}
, are removed, the resulting dynamics of the control on
C
is
exactly
periodic, i.e. it is returned to its initial state
\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}
at times
t_{0,l}=lT_{0}
,
l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
.

We therefore want the dynamical semigroup to have the property that it maps the state of the oscillator on
C
to its initial state periodically at the end of each time interval
[0,T_{0}]
; see
fig.

4
b). Since these perturbations are small, this only consists in a small correction per cycle orchestrated via the coupling to the environment. (Note that the coupling to the environment, and hence the dynamical semigroup, are necessary due to the irreversible nature of said interaction: Hamiltonian dynamics cannot perform it due to its reservable nature.) Another important subtilty, is that since the irreversible correction to the control is mediated though the interaction with the environment, it will be stochastic in nature. This implies that the time at which the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
renewal occurs is a random variable. So long as it’s mean is that of the cycle time
T_{0}
, and its standard deviation very small (in comparison with the time between the last gate of the cycle and the first gate of the
(l+1){{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle), this is not a troublesome situation and is in line with how computers function. Another reason to want the renewal time to coincidence to a good approximation with the cycle time
T_{0}
, is that the purpose of a self-oscillator is to correct for
small
errors in each oscillation which would otherwise build up over time—not to significantly alter its dynamics in each cycle.

We will prove that said deviations are indeed very small in the theorem later in this section and discuss the physical significance of this point later in
Section

VII.2
. In keeping with the convention from
Section

IV
, we label the cycles as zeroth, first, second, third, etc.

To avoid the action of this stabilisation mechanism inadvertently corrupting the application of the last gate in each cycle, we will refrain from applying said gate in each cycle. We will therefore only apply
N_{g}-1
gates per cycle and will only require
N_{g}-1
bus lanes (memory bloque
M_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},N_{g}}
is removed).
5
5

5

As we will see, this change will not affect the asymptotic behaviour since
N_{g}-1\sim N_{g}
for large
N_{g}
.
Furthermore, since we will not model the bus explicitly, we can use a Hamiltonian of the form
eq.

III.5
but without the interaction for the last gate. Recall that this Hamiltonian is bloque-diagonal in the memory-basis of the register,
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
(see
eq.

III.1
). Therefore, since the dissipative part of the dynamical semigroup will not couple to the register, the memory will not evolve under the dynamics we are modelling explicitly.

The time-independent generator of dynamics is thus of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\cdot)=-\mathrm{i}\big[H^{\prime}_{{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}},\,\cdot\,\big]+\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot),

(V.1)

where
\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}
is a dynamical semigroup dissipater on
C
and
T_{0}\,\mathcal{L}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\cdot)
is
T_{0}
-independent by definition for analogous reasons to those discussed for the Hamiltonian case in
Section

II
. For our purposes, it can be further decomposed as
\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}=\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{re}}+\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}
, where
\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{re}}
is a norm non-preserving and completely positive map called the renewal operator. It generates the renewal process: it maps all input states to one unique output state
\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{C}}
(up to a normalization which depends on the input and determines the probability of said process occurring). This irreversible (many-to-one) aspect is crucial for stability since the state of the control towards the end of each cycle depends on the gate sequence implemented in said cycle. And since this differs for each cycle, so does the control state. As such, it is important that all said states are dynamically and autonomously mapped back to the same state to complete the cycle of the oscillator exactly so that it is stable under the perturbations caused by gate implementations. The restoring channel is also called a “restoring force” in the literature on classical self-oscillators. The probability with which this renewal process occurs is however input-state dependent—this is also crucial, since it is important that the stabilisation events occur with overwhelming probability at the end of each cycle, and not at some other time earlier on in the cycle. This is the mechanism via which we will accomplish this. The other term
\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}
is required to ensure that
\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}
is a valid dissipater (i.e.
eq.

V.1
is a Lindbladian).

The Hamiltonian part is

\displaystyle H^{\prime}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}=H_{\textup{C}}+\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}-1}I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)},

(V.2)

and bloque-diagonal in the
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
basis.

The initial state of the computer we will consider in the theorem in this section, denoted
\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(0)
, is any pure state of the form
\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{W}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}
6
6

6

Although see
Section

VII.4
for generalisations to mixed states.
and independent of
T_{0}
, as in the previous sections. Similarly to the Hamiltonians in the previous sections, we demand that the generator of dynamics
eq.

V.1
is independent of the gate sequence
(\mathbb{m}_{l,k})_{l,k}
encoded into the initial state
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}
. The reason for imposing this constraint is the same as that explained in
Section

III
.

V.3
Partial unravelling of the dynamical semigroup

Let us denote by
\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\tau)
the state of the computer after evolving it for a time
\tau>0
according to the generator of dynamics,
eq.

V.2
. This ensemble at time
\tau
comprises of two distinct energy flows: energy flowing into the computer and heat dissipated out to the environment. It is common to perform a partial unravelling of the dynamics in order to appropriately distinguish and measure these two important quantities separately (see e.g.
[
33
,
40
]
). With this objective in mind, we start by observing that this state can be decomposed into a mixed state, where the states of the mixture form a semi-infinite sequence. The sequence can be indexed by
l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
, where
l
is the number of times the renewal operator has acted on it during the time interval
[0,\tau]
, i.e. by how many times it has been “renewed”. What is more, a classical signal is generated every time the renewal channel is autonomously applied. If said classical information is observed continuously on-the-fly, one would observe no change followed by a renewal event occurring, followed by no change, and then a another renewal event occurring, etc. (See
section

F.1.2
for details.) In order to understand the computational model, it is convenient to assume this classical information is observed. In the language of dynamical semigroups, this corresponds to a partial unravelling. It is a mathematically equivalent formulation of the dynamics (since one can reconstruct
\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\tau)
by computing the probabilities of the classical events occurring at different times).

To this end, let us denote by
\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}
the state at time
t+\tau_{l}>\tau_{l}
, which results from the following conditioning: no renewal event occurring in the interval
[t+\tau_{l},\tau_{l})
, the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
renewal event occurring at time
\tau_{l}
, the
(l-1){{}^{\text{th}}}
renewal occurring at time
\tau_{l-1}<\tau_{l}
, etc, and the
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
renewal occurring at time
\tau_{1}>0
. The state
\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}
is unnormalised, its trace corresponds to the conditional probability of said conditioned sequence of events occurring.

Meanwhile, we denote by
P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})
the probability associated with this state being renewed one more time at time
t+\tau_{l}
. (We use the convention
\tau_{0}:=0
, such that
P(t,+1|\tau_{0})
is the probability associated with the
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
renewal.)

V.4
Idealised dynamics we wish to approximately implement

In the case of
Theorems

2
and
3
, we defined idealised dynamics which we wished to approximate via Hamiltonian dynamics. Now we make analogous definitions but for the dynamical semigroup case.

Since we are not modelling the bus explicitly, the only requirement is that it can perform its function of updating the memory on
\textup{M}_{0}
analogously to how it did in
Section

IV
. We can write this condition in terms of
t
as

\displaystyle\ket{t}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}}=\ket{\mathbb{m}_{l,k}}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}},

(V.3)

for all
t\in[t_{k-1},t_{k})
,
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}-1
. Since we will be operating in a steady-state,
eq.

V.3
must hold for all
l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
which implies we are either assuming that the memory on
M
is unbounded or that the gate sequence is periodic (this is a merely mathematically convenient assumption for the obvious reasons).

As in
Sections

III
and
IV
, it is useful to introduce states which correspond to the state of the quantum frequential computer during its computational runtime under the hypothetical assumption that no errors occurred (i.e., among other things, all gates are implemented with zero error). In our current setup, this corresponds to states at times
\{t_{j}+\tau_{l}\}_{j,l}
(given that the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
renewal occurred at time
\tau_{l}
), where the dynamics of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle has not introduced any errors. The utility of introducing such states is that we can see how close the actual dynamics is to said states, thus quantifying errors. We denote these states by
\{\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{P}}{[t_{j}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}]}\otimes\rho_{\textup{C}}{[t_{j}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}]}\}_{j=0}^{N_{g}}
with purifications
\{\ket{[t_{j}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}]}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{P}}\ket{[t_{j}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}]}_{\textup{C}}\}_{j=0}^{N_{g}}
. Here
P
is a fictitious purifying system to allow us to work with pure states for simplicity of notation. It is useful for these states to only capture the idealised dynamics
between
each renewal. As such, they are defined to be equal to the actual dynamics just after each renewal event occurs:

\displaystyle\ket{[\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}]}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{P}}\ket{[\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}]}_{\textup{C}}:=\ket{\rho(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}\textup{P}}=\ket{\rho(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{P}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}},

(V.4)

where
\textup{tr}_{\textup{P}}[\ket{\rho(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}\!\!\bra{\rho(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}\textup{P}}]=\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}{(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}
and the last equality is due to the fact that the renewal process maps the state on
C
to
\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}
at time
\tau_{l}
.
The idealised states
\ket{[t_{k}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}]}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{P}}
update via the exact application of the logical gates
\{U(\mathbb{m}_{l,j})\}_{j=1}^{k}
on
L
or
W
and a set of local unitaries on the memory
\{U_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{P}}^{(l,j)}\}_{j=1}^{k}
to guarantee the fulfilment of
eq.

V.3
:

\displaystyle\ket{[t_{k}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}]}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{P}}=U_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{P}}^{(l,k)}\ldots U_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{P}}^{(l,2)}U_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{P}}^{(l,1)}U(\mathbb{m}_{l,k})\ldots U(\mathbb{m}_{l,2})U(\mathbb{m}_{l,1})\ket{\rho(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{P}}.

(V.5)

Since we assume this for all
l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
, this assumption is implicitly assuming that the oscillator on
\textup{C}_{2}
has been stabilized, otherwise small errors would add up over many cycles leading to the impossibility to implement
eq.

V.3
to high precision. The exact nature of the state of
\textup{M}_{0}\backslash\textup{M}_{0,k}
over the time interval
t\in[t_{k-1},t_{k}]
is not so relevant since the dynamics will have close to zero support on it. For concreteness, we will assume zero knowledge of this state i.e. that it is in a maximally mixed state. For the idealised states of the control, we require
\{\ket{[t_{j}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}]}_{\textup{C}}:=\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\}_{j,l}
, where
\{\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\}_{j}
can be any sequence of states. I.e. that the dynamics after each renewal is independent of which cycle
l
the oscillator is passing through.

V.5
Self-oscillator power consumption

As previously discussed, our model for computation where the control is a self-oscillator, requires the autonomous implementation of an irreversible process. Said irreversible process requires work to implement it. Here we will characterise the work done on the computer in order to achieve its self-oscillating behaviour.

In order to best understand the power consumption of the computer, it is convenient to examine the actual events which unfold during each run of the computer. (Recall that the dynamics under the generator of dynamics,
eq.

V.1
, only captures the dynamics averaged out over many runs, and by taking into account classically available information, the actual observed dynamics is given by a so-called partial unravelling of the dynamics as discussed in
Section

V.3
.) To this end, it is convenient to analyse the dynamics between no renewal and renewal events.

The dynamics immediately after a renewal event is, to a good approximation, Hamiltonian dynamics under
H^{\prime}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}
since initially the dissipater term plays, to a good approximation, no role (we quantify this later in
Section

VI.4
). We call this reversible and entropy-conserving time interval the
isentropic time interval
. It is only towards the end of the cycle where the dissipative coupling to the environment takes effect, that the dynamics enters an irreversible time interval. The irreversibility of dynamics in this later time interval is due to the renewal operator,
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)
, inducing irreversible dynamics on
C
, and results in non-isentropic dynamics on
\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}
