We acknowledge useful discussions with Álvaro Alhambra, Andrew Doherty, Christopher Chubb, Daniel Stilck França, Mark Mitchison, Omar Fawzi.
This work benefited from a government grant managed by the Agence Nationale de la Recherche under the Plan France 2030 with the reference ANR-22-PETQ-0006.

Appendix A
Semi-classical states

In this article, we deal with semi-classical states in some of the theorems. Here we define these.

A.1
Generic definition of semi-classical (non-squeezed states)

By semi-classical, we mean non-squeezed states and use the criterion introduced in
[
64
]
specialised to our setup. This class of states is a generalization of the characterization of coherent and squeezed coherent states.

In the context of metrology, the criterion is defined with respect to a Hamiltonian
H
generating dynamics and an observable
M
which is used to measure the system
[
47
]
. A state
\ket{\psi}
evolving unitarily under Hamiltonian
H
is said to be non-squeezed with respect to observable
M
if it is an eigenvector of the operator

\displaystyle L(\lambda):=\lambda\bar{M}+\mathrm{i}\bar{H},\qquad\lambda\in{\mathbbm{C}}

(A.1)

for
\lambda=1
, where
\bar{M}:=M/T_{0}
,
\bar{H}:=H\,T_{0}
are dimensionless time and Hamiltonian operators respectively. This defines the pure semi-classical states.

This removal of dimensions when defining non-squeezed states is common practice: the criteria is always with respect to dimensionless quantities as above, it is meaningless to say that two quantities are/aren’t equal if they have different units. E.g. when we say that a state is non-squeezed with respect to position and momentum, we need dimensionless measures of position and momentum in order to make the comparison. One defines dimensionless quantities by multiplying said quantities by the relevant length, time, and mass scales in the problem to find dimensionless representations.

It is useful to extend the above set of semi-classical states by considering all eigenstates with
|\lambda|=1
of the operator
L(\lambda)\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{P}}
, for all finite dimensional identity operators
{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{P}}
. Importantly, any element
\ket{\rho_{j}}
, of said set of pure states, has the property that under the evolution of the Hamiltonian
\bar{H}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{P}}
, the measurement statistics of
\bar{M}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{P}}
are the same as the measurement statistics of
\bar{M}
when evolving the state
\rho_{j}=\textup{tr}_{\textup{P}}\big[\ket{\rho_{j}}\!\!\bra{\rho_{j}}\big]
under
\bar{H}
.

Let us denote this set of semi-classical states more formally:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\bar{M},\bar{H}):=\Big\{\rho_{j}\,\big|\,\rho_{j}=\textup{tr}_{\textup{P}}\big[\ket{\rho_{j}}\!\!\bra{\rho_{j}}\big]\Big\}.

(A.2)

We call said set the set of semi-classical states for the operator
L(\lambda)=\lambda\bar{M}+\mathrm{i}\bar{H}
.

On an intuitive level, it is natural to also define probabilistic mixtures of the states in
\mathcal{C}(\bar{M},\bar{H})
as semi-classical states. This is not necessary when we want to prove that a semi-classical limit is obtainable since the above set will suffice. In the case of the upper bounds on what is achievable with semi-classical states, to avoid making the theorems more technical, we do not include such states directly. We simply comment on how it generalises or prove a more technical version of the theorem with its inclusion in an appendix.

A.2
The Robertson-Schrödinger uncertainty relation and the
L(\lambda)
operator

In this section, we give some context to the semi-classical states in
\mathcal{C}(\bar{M},\bar{H})
. We start by considering the eigenstates of the operator
L(\lambda)=\lambda A+\mathrm{i}B
, where
A
,
B
are any two dimensionless observables. (See
[
64
]
for proof and more details, properties and examples)

The Robertson-Schrödinger uncertainty relation is

\displaystyle\sigma_{A}^{2}\sigma_{B}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}\left(\braket{C}^{2}+4\,\sigma_{AB}^{2}\right),\quad C:=-\mathrm{i}[A,B],

(A.3)

where

\displaystyle\sigma_{AB}:=\frac{1}{2}\braket{AB+BA}-\braket{A}\braket{B},\quad\sigma_{D}:=\sqrt{\braket{D^{2}}-\braket{D}^{2}},\quad D\in\{A,B\}.

(A.4)

When
\sigma_{AB}=0
, the above Robertson-Schrödinger uncertainty relation reduces to the so-called Heisenberg uncertainty relation. The inequality holds for all pure states.

As proved in
[
64
]
, all eigenstates of any operator of
L(\lambda)=\lambda A+\mathrm{i}B
, (where
A
,
B
are any two observables), are Robertson-Schrödinger minimum uncertainty states for any
\lambda\in{\mathbbm{C}}
. This is to say, they satisfy
eq.

A.3
with equality. Moreover, in the special case
|\lambda|=1
, they are of equal uncertainty in
A
and
B
, namely

\displaystyle\sigma_{A}=\sigma_{B}.

(A.5)

Since operators
A\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{P}}
, and
B\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{P}}
are themselves observables, the above reasoning also applies to the operator
L(\lambda)\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{P}}
. It thus readily follows that the states in
\mathcal{C}(\bar{M},\bar{H})
in
eq.

A.2
are also Robertson-Schrödinger minimum uncertainty states with equal uncertainty in
A
and
B
. The remaining states in
\mathcal{C}(\bar{M},\bar{H})
are convex mixtures of said minimum and equal uncertainty states.

A.3
Semi-classical states for
Theorems

1
,
2
,
3
and
5

In the following subsections, we will specialise the definition of the sets of semi-classical states to the theorems which use them. This is because in
Theorems

1
and
5
upper bounds are generated which hold for all states in the sets, and as such, we wish to define them more generally so that the bounds hold under more general conditions. Meanwhile, in
Theorems

2
and
3
, we show that certain limits hold for a state in the set of semi-classical states. As such we want these sets of semi-classical states to be more restrictive, so as to not include states which one may argue are not “fully” semi-classical. Hence these distinctions allow for stronger results.

A.3.1
Semi-classical states in
Theorem

1

In this section, we define the set of semi-classical states considered in
Theorem

1
. It is a specialization of the framework from
Section

A.1
. Recall that in
Theorem

1
, we assume that the computer is implementing a classical algorithm with its logical state passing through a sequence of orthogonal states corresponding to the implemented gate sequence, namely
\big(\ket{l}_{\textup{L}}\big)_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
at times
\big(t_{l}\big)_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
respectively, where
t_{l}=l\,T_{0}/N_{g}
. We then measure the state of the logical state during the computation to deduce the time, via the observable

\displaystyle M=\sum_{l=0}^{N_{g}-1}t_{l}\ket{l}\!\!\bra{l}_{\textup{L}}.

(A.6)

We denote by
\mathcal{C}^{\text{clas.}}_{\textup{Com}}
the set of semi-classical initial states of the computer. It is defined as follows: A state
\rho_{\textup{Com}}
is in
\mathcal{C}^{\text{clas.}}_{\textup{Com}}
if there exists two consecutive pairs of states
\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t_{l}),\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t_{l+1})
(
t_{l},t_{l+1}\in 0,1,\ldots,N_{g}
) which are both in the set
\mathcal{C}(\bar{M},\bar{H}_{\textup{Com}})
, with
M
given by
eq.

A.6
. Here the states
\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t_{l}),\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t_{l+1})
are defined via

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t):=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{Com}}}\rho_{\textup{Com}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{Com}}}.

(A.7)

Note that with this definition, there could be some states in the sequence which are quantum (i.e. squeezed). This is well motivated because we want to use this set to prove an upper bound on what is possible with classical states, thus allowing for the possibility of only some of the states to be semi-classical only makes our results stronger.

A.3.2
Semi-classical states in
Theorem

2

At times
\{t_{j}\}_{j=0}^{N_{g}}
, the state of the memory of the computer described
Section

III
is always in a classical state in
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
. Likewise, in the case of a gate set
\mathcal{G}
which can only implement classical algorithms, the logical space of the computer
L
is also in a classical state at said times. Thus the only component which may be in a quantum state is the state of the control on
C
itself. Moreover, even in the case where
\mathcal{G}
permits the application of quantum algorithms, the logical gate frequency
f
is independent of the state of
L
; thus while the states of
L
may be quantum at times
\{t_{j}\}_{j=0}^{N_{g}}
, this should not lead to a quantum advantage in frequency. In this case (like with the classical algorithm case), the only source of quantumness which may lead to a better scaling of
f
with energy
E
, is the state of the control at times
\{t_{j}\}_{j=0}^{N_{g}}
. This setup allows us to see if a semi-classical control state can achieve the scaling of the optimal classical bound in
Theorem

1
. As such, here we introduce a special set of semi-classical states on
C
.

Here the relevant ones are the Hamiltonian of the control,
H_{\textup{C}}
, and the basis which diagonalises the interaction terms
\{I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
. The Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{C}}
we use in the proof of
Theorem

2
is
H_{\textup{C}}=\sum_{n=0}^{d-1}\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}n\ket{E_{n}}\!\!\bra{E_{n}}_{\textup{C}}
, while the basis which diagonalises the terms
\{I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\}_{l}
is the discrete Fourier transform basis of the orthonormal basis
\{\ket{E_{n}}_{\textup{C}}\}_{n}
, namely

\Big\{\ket{\theta_{k}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{n=0}^{d-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}2\pi nk/d}\ket{E_{n}}\Big\}_{k=0}^{d-1}.

One thus defines the “time observable” by
t_{\textup{C}}:=\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}2\pi k\,T_{0}\ket{\theta_{k}}\!\!\bra{\theta_{k}}_{\textup{C}}
.
8
8

8

See
[
70
]
for more insights into
t_{\textup{C}}
and
H_{\textup{C}}
.
Defining the operator
L_{\textup{C}}:=\lambda t_{\textup{C}}+\mathrm{i}H_{\textup{C}}
, we say that a pure state
\ket{\psi}_{\textup{C}}
on
C
is semi-classical (non-squeezed) if it is an eigenstate of
L_{\textup{C}}
for which
|\lambda|=1
up to vanishingly small additive corrections in the large
d
limit, i.e. if
L_{\textup{C}}\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}}=E(\psi)\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}}+\ket{\epsilon(d)}_{\textup{C}}
for
|\lambda|=1
,
E(\psi)\in{\mathbbm{C}}
and where
\|\ket{\epsilon(d)}_{\textup{C}}\|_{2}\to 0
as
d\to\infty
. We denote the set of such states by
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{clas.}}
. In
Theorem

2
, we show that all states of the control on
\textup{C}_{2}
are in
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{clas.}}
at times
\{t_{j}\}_{j=0}^{N_{g}}
.

While this set is defined asymptotically for large
d
, this quantity increases with energy
E_{0}
, so
d\to\infty
as
E_{0}\to\infty
and so the set is well-defined in the context of
Theorem

2
. Furthermore, since we want to use this set in a theorem which shows what is achievable with a set of states which does not include quantum ones, we want to make sure that
every
relevant state is semi-classical (not just a subset of said relevant states). For this reason, we do not to include other non-squeezed states in this set (e.g. certain types of mixed states).

Note that one can alternatively use
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
and
{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}}\otimes t_{\textup{C}}
in the above definition of
L_{\textup{C}}
(instead of
H_{\textup{C}}
and
t_{\textup{C}}
respectively) without modification to
Theorem

2
which uses this definition of semi-classical control states. Recall that
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
defined in
eq.

III.5
describes the dynamics of the entire computer, while
H_{\textup{C}}
is the free dynamics term of the control. This is because the relevant approximate eigenstates of
L_{\textup{C}}
which is used in the proof of the Theorem, are orthogonal to all terms in
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}-H_{\textup{C}}
up to additive contributions which vanish exponentially fast with increasing
d
.

A.3.3
Semi-classical states in
Theorem

3

For the case of
Theorem

3
, in
Section

IV
, the classical states on
\textup{C}_{2}
are initially (i.e. at time
t_{0,0}
) a pure product state with the rest of the computer. At later times (i.e.
t_{l,k}
,
l,k>0
), the state on
\textup{C}_{2}
becomes correlated with the state of the memory
M
.
For
t_{0,0}
, we define the semi-classical states analogously to the definition of the pure semi-classical state on
C
in
Theorem

2
, but on system
\textup{C}_{2}
rather than
C
. (Since the two have isomorphic Hilbert spaces, this is well defined). From
Section

A.2
, we thus have that these states are both minimum and equal uncertainty states. For later times (i.e.
t_{l,k}
,
l,k>0
), we define states to belong to the set of semi-classical states if they are both minimum and equal uncertainty states with respect to the same two observables which was used for the initial state.

More formally, one can define this set as follows. In
eq.

IV.10
, the Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{C}_{2}}
is defined identically to
H_{\textup{C}}
up to the change of Hilbert space
\textup{C}\to\textup{C}_{2}
. We can thus define the discrete Fourier transform basis
\{\ket{\theta_{k}}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\}_{k}
analogously to above but for
\textup{C}_{2}
. (The interaction terms
\{I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
are also diagonal in this basis.) The operator
t_{\textup{C}_{2}}
can thus be defined as
t_{\textup{C}_{2}}:=\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}2\pi kT_{0}\ket{\theta_{k}}\!\!\bra{\theta_{k}}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
, and the semi-classical (non-squeezed) pure states on
\textup{C}_{2}
, by solving
L_{\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}=E(\psi)\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}+\ket{\epsilon(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
for
|\lambda|=1
,
E(\psi)\in{\mathbbm{C}}
and where
\|\ket{\epsilon(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\|_{2}=0
as
d\to\infty
. We define
\rho_{\textup{C}_{2}}
as semi-classical (non-squeezed) if there exists
\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
such that

\Delta t_{\textup{C}_{2}}(\rho_{\textup{C}_{2}}):=\textup{tr}[t_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{2}\rho_{\textup{C}_{2}}]-(\textup{tr}[t_{\textup{C}_{2}}\rho_{\textup{C}_{2}}])^{2}
and
\Delta H_{\textup{C}_{2}}(\rho_{\textup{C}_{2}}):=\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{2}\rho_{\textup{C}_{2}}]-(\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{C}_{2}}\rho_{\textup{C}_{2}}])^{2}
both satisfy

\displaystyle\Delta t_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big(\rho_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big)\geq\Delta t_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big(\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big),\qquad\Delta H_{\textup{C}_{2}}(\rho_{\textup{C}_{2}})\geq\Delta H_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big(\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big),

(A.8)

where
\Delta t_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big(\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big):={\mathstrut}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\bra{\psi(d)}t_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{2}\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}-\left({\mathstrut}{}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\bra{\psi(d)}t_{\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\right)^{2}
and
H_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big(\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big):={\mathstrut}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\bra{\psi(d)}H_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{2}\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\\
-\left({\mathstrut}{}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\bra{\psi(d)}H_{\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{\psi(d)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\right)^{2}
. This is to say, the have uncertainties in the Hamiltonian and time operator which are above or equal to what is classically optimal.

We denote by
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{\textup{clas.}}
, the set of semi-classical states on
\textup{C}_{2}
according the above definition. In
Theorem

2
, we show that the state of the control is in this set at all times
\{t_{j,l}\}_{j,l}
.

Thus the Theorem uses a strict notion of semi-classicality, in the sense that it must apply at all relevant times, in contrast to a subset of times. This is important since
Theorem

2
characterises what is possible with solely classical resources. So while we could have included more semi-classical states in the definition of this set, this would not be beneficial for our purposes.

A.3.4
Semi-classical states in
Theorem

5

Since the self-oscillator can run in a non-equilibrium steady-state, we define a semi-classical state condition which applies to the states in one cycle, with implications for the achievable frequency in said cycle.

The relevant pair of observables are the Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
which generates the unitary part of the dynamics, and the observable
M^{(j,l)}_{\textup{L}}:=\sum_{k}t_{1}(1-\delta_{k,j})\ket{k,l}\!\!\bra{k,l}_{\textup{L}}
which distinguishes between being in state
\ket{j,l}_{\textup{L}}
and the other logical states of the computation. (Here
\{\ket{k,l}_{\textup{L}}\}_{k}
is the basis in
eq.

H.5
.) Their dimensionless counterparts are

\displaystyle\bar{M}^{(j,l)}_{\textup{L}}:=M^{(j,l)}_{\textup{L}}/T_{0},\qquad\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}:=H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}T_{0}.

(A.9)

We denote by
\mathcal{C}^{\text{clas.,\,}l}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
, the set of semi-classical self-oscillator states of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle. It is defined as follows:
A state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
is in
\mathcal{C}^{\text{clas.,\,}l}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
, if there exist a pair of states
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t_{j}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
,
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t_{j+1}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
(with
t_{j},t_{j+1}\in[0,t_{\max,l})
,
j\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
), such that
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t_{j}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\in\mathcal{C}(\bar{M}^{(j,l)}_{\textup{L}},\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}})
and
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t_{j+1}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\in\mathcal{C}(\bar{M}^{(j+1,l)}_{\textup{L}},\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}})
respectively. The requirement
t_{j},t_{j+1}\in[0,t_{\max,l})
guarantees that the states belong to the isentropic time interval of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle.

Recall that the states
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
are the normalised states of the dynamics during the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle, namely
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})=\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}/\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]
, where
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
is given by
eq.

F.5
.

Note that analogously to the definition in
Section

A.3.1
, the dynamics generated from states
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\in\mathcal{C}^{\text{clas.,\,}l}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
may turn out to be squeezed at a later time
t_{j}+\tau_{l}>\tau_{l}
, but since we are interested in upper bounds, said set will suffice, since a set where all the states are semi-classical is a subset and thus our definition only imposes stricter conditions.

Appendix B
Proof of
Theorem

1
: upper bounds on quantum and classical computers

See
1

Proof.

In the Fisher information approach to quantum metrology, the mean squared error of the signal
(
which in our case is
t\in[0,T_{0})
)
is given by
[
63
]

\displaystyle\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}P(\xi_{k}|t)(t_{\text{est}}(\xi_{k})-t)^{2},\qquad\sum_{k}P(\xi_{k}|t)=1

(B.1)

where
P(\xi_{k}|t)
is the probability of predicting measurement outcome
\xi_{k}
given that the signal takes on value
t
, and
t_{\text{est}}=t_{\text{est}}(\xi_{k})\in{\mathbbm{R}}
is our estimate for
t\in[0,T_{0})
which we make based on our measurement outcome
\xi_{k}
. By assumption, the logical space of our computer is initialised to
\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}
and then passes though a sequence
\big(\ket{l}_{\textup{L}}\big)_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
of states after the application of the first
l
gates: the logical state is
\ket{l}_{\textup{L}}
for
t=t_{l}=l\,T_{0}/N_{g}
for
l=0,1,2,\ldots,N_{g}-1
. The conditional probability
P(\xi_{k}|t)
can be written as
P(\xi_{k}|t)=\textup{tr}[M^{\prime}(\xi_{k})\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t)]
where
\{M^{\prime}(\xi_{k})\}_{\xi}
is a complete set of POVM elements and
\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t)
the state of the computation (which includes the logical space as a subspace) at time
t
.

For our estimate, we choose a projective POVM
\{\ket{l}\!\!\bra{l}_{\textup{L}}\}_{l}
corresponding to the part of the logical state space the computation passes through. The corresponding observable is given by

\displaystyle M^{\prime}=\sum_{l=0}^{N_{g}-1}t_{\text{est}}(\xi_{l})\ket{l}\!\!\bra{l}_{\textup{L}},

(B.2)

\displaystyle t_{\text{est}}(\xi_{l})=\left(l+\Delta_{0}\right)\frac{T_{0}}{N_{g}}=t_{l}+\Delta_{0}\frac{T_{0}}{N_{g}}.

(B.3)

Thus the root mean squared error in our measurement as a function of the signal
t
at times
t=t_{l}
is

\displaystyle\Delta t_{\text{est}}(t_{l}):=\sqrt{\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t_{l})^{2}}}=\Delta_{0}\frac{T_{0}}{N_{g}},

(B.4)

for
l=0,1,\ldots,N_{g}-1
. We start by proving
eq.

II.4
.
From the appendix of
[
28
]
it is stated that for any signal
x\in{\mathbbm{R}}
with a root mean squared error
\Delta X(x):=\sqrt{\braket{(X-x)^{2}}}
, for which there exist
x,x^{\prime}
such that:

\displaystyle\begin{split}1)&\qquad\Delta X(x)>0,\qquad\Delta X(x^{\prime})>0\\
2)&\qquad|x-x^{\prime}|=(\lambda+1)\big(\Delta X(x)+\Delta X(x^{\prime})\big)\end{split}

(B.5)

where
\lambda=4.64
, then

\displaystyle\Delta X(x)\geq\frac{\kappa}{\textup{tr}[H\rho_{0}]-E_{0}},

(B.6)

where
\kappa
is a numerical constant
\kappa\approx 0.091
,
\rho_{0}
the initial probe state,

\displaystyle\rho(x)=\mathrm{e}^{x\mathrm{i}H}\rho_{0}\mathrm{e}^{-x\mathrm{i}H}

(B.7)

is the probe state for signal value
x
, and
E_{0}
is the ground state of Hamiltonian
H
. (Here we have specialised to the case of a single copy of the probe state since this is sufficient for our purposes.)

In our case, we can choose the two values of the signal to be
t=t_{l+1}
and
t^{\prime}=t_{l}
, such that
|t-t^{\prime}|=T_{0}/N_{g}
, and
\Delta t_{\textup{est}}(t_{l})+\Delta t_{\textup{est}}(t_{l+1})=2\Delta_{0}T_{0}/N_{g}
. Therefore, by choosing
\Delta_{0}=1/\big(2(\lambda+1)\big)\approx 0.0887
it follows that

\displaystyle\Delta t_{\text{est}}(t_{l})\geq\frac{\kappa}{\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{Com}}\rho_{0}]-E^{0}_{\textup{Com}}},

(B.8)

where we have denoted the ground state of
H_{\textup{Com}}
by
E^{0}_{\textup{Com}}
. Therefore, by recalling that
f=N_{g}/T_{0}
with no restrictions of the probe state nor the Hamiltonian, from
eqs.

B.6
and
B.4
we arrive at
eq.

II.4
.

We now move on to the proof of
eq.

II.3
. Using techniques from quantum metrology, in
[
47
]
the quantum advantage for squeezed states under similar unitary encoding scheme via a signal-independent Hamiltonian was investigated. Our choice of estimator above is such that their results also apply to it, after changing to dimensionless variables.
In particular, we consider a dimensionless signal, defined by
t/T_{0}\in[0,1]
, and a corresponding dimensionless Hamiltonian
\bar{H}_{\textup{Com}}=H_{\textup{Com}}T_{0}
. Since the dynamics of
\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t)
depends on their product, the dynamics can be expressed solely in terms of these dimensionless quantities. The root mean squared of the dimensionless signal is thus

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}t_{\textup{est}}=\frac{\Delta t_{\textup{est}}}{T_{0}}=\frac{\Delta_{0}}{N_{g}}.

(B.9)

The authors of
[
47
]
consider the same unitary encoding of a signal described above (i.e.
eq.

B.7
), but now for the special case that said signal
\bar{x}\in{\mathbbm{R}}
and Hamiltonian
\bar{H}
are dimensionless. They show that when the probe state is not squeezed, and conditions
eq.

B.6
are satisfied, then

\displaystyle\Delta X(x)\geq\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\textup{tr}[\bar{H}\rho_{0}]-\bar{E}_{0}}},

(B.10)

where
\kappa^{\prime}
is a numerical constant. We provide the definition of non-squeezed states (specialised to our setup) in
Appendix

A
. Alternatively, see
[
47
]
for details.

In our case, we have for
l=0,1,2,\ldots,N_{g}-1
:

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}t_{\textup{est}}\geq\frac{c_{0}}{\sqrt{\textup{tr}[\bar{H}_{\textup{Com}}\rho_{0}]-\bar{E}^{0}_{\textup{Com}}}}

(B.11)

if
\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t_{l})
and
\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t_{l+1})
[where
\rho_{\textup{Com}}(t):=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{Com}}}\rho_{\textup{Com}}(0)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{Com}}}
] are non-squeezed, where the set of “non squeezed” states, denoted
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{Com}}^{\textup{clas.}}
, is defined in
Section

A.3.1
. Here
c_{0}
is a numerical constant from
[
47
]
. Thus since
f=N_{g}/T_{0}
,
eq.

II.3
follows from
eq.

B.11
.

Note that we have slightly changed the definition of the observable in the squeezed state criteria (rather than
M^{\prime}/T_{0}
in
eq.

B.2
, we have used
M/T_{0}
in
eq.

A.6
). These two observables only differ by an additive constant factor times the identity, and the eigenvectors of a matrix are invariant under the addition of a scalar times the identity matrix. Therefore, these different observables yield the same non-squeezed-state criteria.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of
Theorem

2
: Attaining the quantum limit

Before stating the proof of the main theorem, we prove several crucial lemmas. The proof of the main theorem is by construction. We will specialise definitions as we proceed and as becomes necessary to prove the desired results.

C.1
Main structural technical lemma

Let us introduce Hamiltonians of the form

\displaystyle H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}:=H_{\textup{C}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}.

(C.1)

In the following lemma, we assume that the states
\{\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{L}},\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\}_{j=0}^{N_{g}}
are normalised and
H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}
in
eq.

C.1
is finite-dimensional.
9
9

9

This last assumption is overkill; indeed under minimal assumptions it can be extended to the infinite-dimensional case. However, for our purposes this will suffice.

Lemma C.1
.

For
j=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}-\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{j}\big\|\ket{t_{k}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k}}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.2)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{j}\bigg(\big\|\ket{t_{k}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k}}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.3)

\displaystyle+t_{1}\max_{x\in[0,t_{1}]}\big\|\bar{H}_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}\bigg),

(C.4)

where

\displaystyle H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}:=H_{\textup{C}}+I_{\textup{L}}^{(k)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(k)},\qquad\bar{H}_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}:=H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}-H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}.

(C.5)

The Lemma is useful because it permits us to compute the error by only computing unitary evolution w.r.t.
H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}
rather than
H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}
. While the latter does not factorise into a product between system and control, it is readily apparent that the former does. This is of great utility as we will see in later proofs.

Proof.

The first inequality in
eq.

C.2
is a direct consequence of
Lemma

C.4
. To see this, in
Lemma

C.4
we choose
\ket{\Phi_{m}}=\ket{t_{m}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{m}}_{\textup{C}}
and
\Delta_{m}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}
and note
t_{j}=jt_{1}
. We can now add and subtract an appropriately-chosen term and apply the triangle inequality to achieve

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}-\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.6)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{j}\big\|\ket{t_{k}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k}}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.7)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{j}\Big(\big\|\ket{t_{k}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k}}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.8)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\,+\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}\Big).

(C.9)

The first term after the inequality is the first term after the inequality in
eq.

C.2
. We focus on upper bounding the
2{{}^{\text{nd}}}
term in
eq.

C.6
. For this, we start by applying
Lemma

C.4
again. This time, we make the association
\ket{\Phi_{m}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\delta_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{\Phi_{m-1}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\delta_{n}(m-1)H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{\Phi_{0}}
,
\Delta_{m}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\delta_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}
, with
\delta_{n}=t_{1}/n
. Thus applying
Lemma

C.4
, we have for all
n\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.10)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=1}^{n}\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}m\delta_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\delta_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(m-1)\delta_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.11)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{n}\big\|\Big(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\delta_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\delta_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\Big)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(m-1)\delta_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{j-1}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.12)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=1}^{n}\max_{x_{n}\in[0,t_{1}]}\big\|\Big(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\delta_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\delta_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\Big)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{j-1}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.13)

\displaystyle=n\max_{x_{n}\in[0,t_{1}]}\sqrt{{}_{\textup{L}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\!{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}x_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}A^{\dagger}(\delta_{n})A(\delta_{n})\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}},

(C.14)

where
A(y):=\Big(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}yH_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}yH_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\Big)
. Applying Taylor’s remainder theorem to the real function
f:{\mathbbm{R}}\to{\mathbbm{R}}

\displaystyle f(y):={}_{\textup{L}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\!{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}x_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}A^{\dagger}(y)A(y)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}

(C.15)

about the point
y=0
, we find

\displaystyle f(y)=\,{}_{\textup{L}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\!{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\big[\dot{A}^{\dagger}(y)\dot{A}(y)\big]_{y=0}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}\,y^{2}+R(y)\,y^{3},

(C.16)

where dots represent derivatives w.r.t.
y
and
R:\Re\to\Re
is a remainder function satisfying
\lim_{y\to 0}R(y)=0
. Calculating explicitly the derivatives
\dot{A}^{\dagger}(y)\dot{A}(y)
and plugging into
eq.

C.14
and recalling
\delta_{n}=t_{1}/n
we find

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.17)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x_{n}\in[0,t_{1}]}\sqrt{{}_{\textup{L}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\!{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}x_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\Big(\bar{H}_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}\Big)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}t_{1}^{2}+R(t_{1}/n)t_{1}^{3}/n},

(C.18)

for all
n\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
. Therefore, taking the limit
n\to\infty
and noting that the remainder term is uniformly bounded in
x_{n}\in[0,t_{1}]
, and that the only dependency on
n
in the non-remainder term in
eq.

C.16
is via its dependency on
x_{n}
, we find that

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.19)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\max_{x_{n}\in[0,t_{1}]}\sqrt{{}_{\textup{L}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\!{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}x_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\Big(\bar{H}_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}\Big)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}t_{1}^{2}+R(t_{1}/n)t_{1}^{3}/n},

(C.20)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\max_{x_{n}\in[0,t_{1}]}{}_{\textup{L}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\!{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}x_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\Big(\bar{H}_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}\Big)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x_{n}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}t_{1}^{2}\right)+}

(C.21)

\displaystyle\qquad\overline{+\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\max_{x_{n}\in[0,t_{1}]}R(t_{1}/n)\frac{t_{1}^{3}}{n}\right)},

(C.22)

\displaystyle=\max_{x\in[0,t_{1}]}t_{1}\sqrt{{}_{\textup{L}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\!{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{t_{k-1}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\Big(\bar{H}_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}\Big)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{C}}}.

(C.23)

Thus identifying the last line with the corresponding 2-norm, we conclude the proof.
∎

C.2
Some additional definitions required for the lemmas of
Section

C.3

Here we specialise the form of the terms in Hamiltonian
eq.

C.1
and the states of the control on
C
. There will still be some free parameters which will only be set at later stages as it becomes required in order to prove the desired results.

The free control term,
H_{\textup{C}}
, is chosen identically to that of
[
70
]
, namely:

\displaystyle{H}_{\textup{C}}=\sum_{n=0}^{d-1}n\omega_{0}\ket{E_{n}}\!\!\bra{E_{n}},

(C.24)

where
\{\ket{E_{n}}\}_{n=0}^{d-1}
forms an orthonormal basis for the Hilbert space of the control,
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{C}}
. The frequency
\omega_{0}
determines both the energy recurrence of the control when no interaction terms are present,
T_{0}=2\pi/\omega_{0}>0
, as
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\hat{H}_{\textup{C}}T_{0}}={\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{C}}
.

The states
\{\ket{\theta_{k}}\}
are the discrete Fourier Transform basis of the energy basis: For
k\in{\mathbbm{Z}}

\displaystyle\ket{\theta_{k}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{n=0}^{d-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}2\pi nk/d}\ket{E_{n}}.

(C.25)

Note that any subset of
d
consecutive terms forms an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{C}}
. We will make use of this redundancy below when defining the stats on
C
.

Let us now define the interaction terms: For
l=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}

\displaystyle I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}:=\frac{d}{T_{0}}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(k_{0})}I_{\textup{C},d}^{(l)}(k)\ket{\theta_{k}}\!\!\bra{\theta_{k}},\qquad I_{\textup{C},d}^{(l)}(x):=\frac{2\pi}{d}\bar{V}_{0}\left(\frac{2\pi}{d}x\right)\Bigg|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{(l)}},

(C.26)

with
\mathcal{S}_{d}(k_{0}):=\{k\,|\,k\in{\mathbbm{Z}}\text{ and }-d/2\leq k_{0}-k<d/2\}

and where
x_{0}^{(l)}:=2\pi(l-1/2)/N_{g}
. The function
\bar{V}_{0}:{\mathbbm{R}}\to{\mathbbm{R}}
is defined in
[
71
]
. It has
x_{0}\in{\mathbbm{R}}
as a parameter in its definition, which is
10
10

10

Technically,
\bar{V}_{0}
comes with the additional additive factor of
1/\delta d
is its definition in
[
71
]
, but in the current application it is not required and has thus been neglected for simplicity by setting
\delta=1
and mapping the
1/d
additive factor to zero. (Importantly, it is readily seen by following the proofs in
[
71
]
, that the lemmas from
[
71
]
which we will require hold equally well when it is omitted in the definition up to minor modifications which we will highlight as the become come up in the
manuscript
. We leave it as an exercise to re-do the derivations in
[
71
]
) under this minor modification. When we use a modified result in this
manuscript
, we will notify the reader of the modification.

\displaystyle\bar{V}_{0}(x)=nA_{0}\sum_{p=-\infty}^{+\infty}V_{B}(n(x-x_{0}+2\pi p)),

(C.27)

where
n>0
,
x_{0}\in{\mathbbm{R}}
and
A_{0}
is a normalization constant such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\bar{V}_{0}(x)dx=1,

(C.28)

and takes on the value (see F168 in
[
71
]
)

\displaystyle A_{0}=\frac{1}{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\textup{d}xV_{B}(x)},

(C.29)

where

\displaystyle V_{B}(\cdot)=\textup{sinc}^{2N}(\cdot)=(\sin(\pi\,\cdot)/(\pi\,\cdot))^{2N},\qquad N\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}.

(C.30)

Notice that
\bar{V}_{0}(\cdot)
is
2\pi
periodic and as such, the summation in
I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}
is independent of
k_{0}\in{\mathbbm{R}}
. We will later take advantage of this
k_{0}
independency and also show how to parametrise
n
in terms or
d
to achieve our desired result. As we will see,
N
on the other hand will be chosen such that it is
d
-independent. Therefore, from
eq.

C.29
it follows that
A_{0}
will also be
d
-independent.

One can use the Weierstrass M test (see Theorem 7.10 in
[
55
]
), to show that the sum in
eq.

C.27
converges uniformly. We thus have

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\mathrm{d}x\bar{V}_{0}(x)=nA_{0}\sum_{p=-\infty}^{+\infty}\int_{a}^{b}\mathrm{d}x\,V_{B}(n(x-x_{0}+2\pi p)),\qquad\forall a,b,x_{0}\in{\mathbbm{R}},n>0.

(C.31)

We will use this property extensively in proofs in this
manuscript
.

Let

\displaystyle\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{L}}:=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{L}}^{(j)}}\ket{t_{j-1}}_{\textup{L}},\quad j=1,2,\ldots,N_{g},

(C.32)

where we assume w.l.o.g. that the spectrum of
I_{\textup{L}}^{(j)}
lies in the interval
(0,2\pi]
. Its eigenvalues are arbitrary so that states
\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{L}}
and
\ket{t_{j-1}}_{\textup{L}}
can be related by any unitary transformation (we will later specify the spectrum in relation to the gate set
\mathcal{G}
). As mentioned in the main text,
\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}
is any pure state in
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{L}}
.

Since the memory is fixed in this section, the definition of
\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{l})
corresponds to the number of non-identical eigenvalues of
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}
and

\displaystyle t_{j}:=jT_{0}/N_{g},

(C.33)

for
j=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
.

As for the states of the control, we use so-called quasi-ideal clock states coming from
[
70
]
. In particular, we define
\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}:=\ket{\Psi(t_{j}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}
for
j=0,1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
, where for
t\in{\mathbbm{R}}
,
T_{0}>0
,
d\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
,

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi(td/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}:=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(td/T_{0})}\psi_{\textup{nor}}\big(td/T_{0},k\big)\ket{\theta_{k}},

(C.34)

where

\displaystyle\psi_{\textup{nor}}\big(k_{0};k\big):=A_{\textup{nor}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{\sigma^{2}}(k-k_{0})^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}2\pi n_{0}(k-k_{0})/d}.

(C.35)

with
\sigma>0
,
n_{0}\in(0,d-1)
. The amplitude
A_{\textup{nor}}
is defined such that
\ket{\Psi(td/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}
is normalised. Its large-
d
scaling is

\displaystyle|A_{\textup{nor}}|^{2}=\left(\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\right)+\epsilon(d),

(C.36)

where
\epsilon(d)\to 0
as
d\to\infty
(under reasonable assumptions about how
\sigma
depends on
d
which are satisfied in this
manuscript
; see
appendix
E in
[
70
]
for details).

C.3
Final technical lemmas

Lemma C.2
.

For all
k\in 1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
, the last terms in
Lemma

C.1
are upper bounded by

\displaystyle t_{1}\max_{x\in[0,t_{1}]}\big\|\bar{H}_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}\leq

(C.37)

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})4\pi^{2}nA_{0}}{T_{0}}\Biggl(3A_{\textup{nor}}d\,N_{g}\bigg(\mathrm{e}^{-{\pi}{\frac{d^{2}}{\sigma^{2}(4N_{g})^{2}}}}+\Big(\frac{2N_{g}}{\pi^{2}n}\Big)^{2N}\,\bigg)

(C.38)

\displaystyle\quad+A_{\textup{nor}}\left(\pi^{2}-\frac{79}{9}\right)N_{g}d\left(\frac{1}{2\pi n}\right)^{2N}+N_{g}d\,\left(1+\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\right)\varepsilon_{v}(t_{1},d)\Biggr),

(C.39)

where
n>0
,
N\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varepsilon_{v}(t,d)=&|t|\frac{d}{T_{0}}\left[\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\sigma^{3}}{\sigma d^{-\epsilon_{5}+1}}\right)^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{d^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+2C_{0}n\right)\right]\exp{\left(-\frac{\pi}{4}\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{(1+d^{\epsilon_{5}}/\sigma)^{2}}d^{2\epsilon_{5}}\right)}\\
&+\mathcal{O}\left(|t|\frac{d^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{4}\frac{d^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\sigma^{2}}\right)\text{ as }d\to\infty,\quad(0,d)\ni\sigma\to\infty\end{split}

(C.40)

where
C_{0}>0
,
\alpha_{0}>0
,
\epsilon_{5}>0
are fixed constants and

\displaystyle A_{\textup{nor}}\leq\left(\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{1/4}+\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\epsilon}_{1}+\bar{\epsilon}_{2}}{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}-\bar{\epsilon}_{1}-\bar{\epsilon}_{2}\right)}},

(C.41)

with

\displaystyle\bar{\epsilon}_{2}:=\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{2e^{-\frac{\pi\sigma^{2}}{2}}}{1-e^{-\pi\sigma^{2}}},\quad\bar{\epsilon}_{1}:=\frac{2e^{-\frac{\pi d^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}}{1-e^{-\frac{2\pi d}{\sigma^{2}}}}.

(C.42)

Proof.

For all
x\in[0,t_{1}]

\displaystyle\big\|\bar{H}_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.43)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\big\|I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.44)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\big|A_{j}^{(k-1)}\big|\big\|I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\ket{\theta_{j}^{(k-1)}}_{\textup{L}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}\big(\theta_{j}^{(k)}\big)}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.45)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\big|A_{j}^{(k-1)}\big|\big\|I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}\ket{\theta_{j}^{(k-1)}}_{\textup{L}}\big\|_{2}\big\|I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}\big(\theta_{j}^{(k)}\big)}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.46)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})}\big|A_{j}^{(k-1)}\big|\big\|I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}\big\|_{2}\max_{\vartheta\in[-\pi,\pi]}\big\|I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}(\vartheta)}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.47)

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\max_{\vartheta\in[-\pi,\pi]}\big\|I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}(\vartheta)}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.48)

where in line
C.44
we have used
eq.

C.5
together with the triangle inequality. For line
C.45
, we have used the decomposition

\displaystyle\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})}A_{j}^{(k-1)}\ket{\theta_{j}^{(k)}}_{\textup{L}},

(C.49)

where
\ket{\theta_{j}^{(k)}}_{\textup{L}}
belongs to the subspace spanned by the vectors of eigenvalue
\theta_{j}^{(k)}
for the operator
I_{\textup{L}}^{(k)}
. Thus
\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})\leq d_{\textup{L}}^{(k)}
, where recall that
\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})
is the number of non-identical eigenvalues of
I_{\textup{L}}^{(k)}
.
We have also defined

\displaystyle H_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}\big(\gamma\big):=H_{\textup{C}}+\gamma\,I_{\textup{C}}^{(k)},

(C.50)

\gamma\in{\mathbbm{R}}
(c.f. def. of
H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}
in
eq.

C.5
).
For line
C.46
, we have used the fact that the 2-norm of a tensor product is the product of the 2-norms. In line
C.47
, we have used
\|I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}\|_{2}
to denote the 2-norm-induced operator norm. In line
C.48
, we have used the assumption
\|I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}\|_{2}\leq 2\pi
for all
l=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
.

To continue, we will need the particular form of the interaction terms introduced in
eq.

C.26
and we will need to recall Theorem IX.1 (
Moving the clock through finite time with a potential
) from
[
70
]
, which states

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t(H_{\textup{C}}+\hat{V}_{d})}\ket{\bar{\Psi}_{\textup{nor}}(k_{0},\Delta)}_{\textup{C}}=\ket{\bar{\Psi}_{\textup{nor}}(k_{0}+t\,d/T_{0},\Delta+t\,d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}+\ket{\epsilon}_{\textup{C}},\quad\|\ket{\epsilon}_{\textup{C}}\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon_{v}(t,d),

(C.51)

where

\displaystyle\ket{\bar{\Psi}_{\textup{nor}}(k_{0},\Delta)}_{\textup{C}}:=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(k_{0})}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\int_{k-\Delta}^{k}\textup{d}yV_{d}(y)}\psi_{\textup{nor}}\big(k_{0};k\big)\ket{\theta_{k}}_{\textup{C}},

(C.52)

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{d}:=\frac{d}{T_{0}}\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}V_{d}(k)\ket{\theta_{k}}\!\!\bra{\theta_{k}}_{\textup{C}},\qquad V_{d}(x)=\frac{2\pi}{d}\bar{V}_{0}\left(\frac{2\pi}{d}x\right).

(C.53)

We can apply this theorem to approximate
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}(\vartheta)}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}
appearing in line
C.48
by identifying
x
with
t
, and
\hat{V}_{d}
with
\vartheta I_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}
. To do so, first note that by definition it follows that
\ket{\Psi_{\textup{nor}}(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}=\ket{\bar{\Psi}_{\textup{nor}}(t_{k-1}d/T_{0},0)}_{\textup{C}}
(recall definition
C.34
).

Continuing from line
C.48
, but now maximizing over
x
we thus find

\displaystyle\max_{x\in[0,t_{1}]}2\pi\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\max_{\vartheta\in[0,2\pi]}\big\|I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}(\vartheta)}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0}})_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.54)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in[0,t_{1}]}2\pi\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\max_{\vartheta\in[0,2\pi]}\Big\|\frac{d}{T_{0}}\sum_{q\in\mathcal{S}_{d}([t_{k-1}+x]d/T_{0})}I_{\textup{C},d}^{(l)}(q)\ket{\theta_{q}}\!\!\bra{\theta_{q}}\left(\ket{\bar{\Psi}_{\textup{nor}}([t_{k-1}+x]d/T_{0},xd/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}+\ket{\varepsilon_{C}(x,d)}\right)\Big\|_{1}

(C.55)

Lemma C.3
.

, the first term in

,

Proof.

\displaystyle\big\|\ket{t_{k}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{4\pi}{N_{g}}\int_{1/4}^{N_{g}/2}\bar{V}_{0}\left(\frac{2\pi}{N_{g}}y+x_{0}^{(k)}\right)\!\!\Bigg|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{(k)}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\textup{d}y\,\,\right)^{\!\!2}

(C.93)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{4\pi nA_{0}}{N_{g}}\right)^{2}\left(\int_{1/4}^{N_{g}/2}\left(\frac{N_{g}}{2n\pi^{2}y}\right)^{2N}\textup{d}y+\int_{1/4}^{N_{g}/2}\left(\frac{N_{g}}{2n\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{g}-y}\right)^{2N}\textup{d}y+\!\!\!\sum_{p\in{\mathbbm{Z}}\backslash\{0,-1\}}\int_{1/4}^{N_{g}/2}\!\!\left(2n\pi^{2}\left(\frac{y}{N_{g}}+p\right)\right)^{-2N}\!\!\!\textup{d}y\right)^{\!\!2}

(C.94)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{4\pi nA_{0}}{N_{g}}\right)^{2}\left(\left(\frac{2N_{g}}{\pi^{2}\,n}\right)^{2N}\frac{N_{g}}{2}+\left(\frac{N_{g}}{\pi^{2}\,n}\right)^{2N}\frac{N_{g}}{2}+\frac{N_{g}}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}n}\right)^{2N}\sum_{p\in{\mathbbm{Z}}\backslash\{0,-1\}}\left(\min_{y\in[1/4,N_{g}/2]}\left|\frac{y}{N_{g}}+p\right|\right)^{-2N}\right)^{\!\!2}

(C.95)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{4\pi nA_{0}}{N_{g}}\right)^{2}\left(2\left(\frac{2N_{g}}{\pi^{2}\,n}\right)^{2N}\frac{N_{g}}{2}+\frac{N_{g}}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}n}\right)^{2N}\left(\sum_{p\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}}p^{-2N}+\sum_{p\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}}\left|\frac{1}{2}-p-1\right|^{-2N}\right)\right)^{\!\!2}

(C.96)

\displaystyle\leq\left(2\pi nA_{0}\right)^{2}\left(2\left(\frac{2N_{g}}{\pi^{2}\,n}\right)^{2N}+\left(\frac{2\pi^{2}}{3}-5\right)\left(\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}n}\right)^{2N}\right)^{\!\!2}

(C.97)

\displaystyle\leq\left(4\pi nA_{0}\right)^{2}\left(\left(\frac{2N_{g}}{\pi^{2}\,n}\right)^{2N}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}n}\right)^{2N}\right)^{\!\!2}

(C.98)

where in line
C.92
we have taking into account the integrals with intervals of integration
[-q/4+1/2,N_{g}/2]
and
[q/4+1/2,N_{g}/2]
, map to one another under the transformation
q\to-q
. In line
C.93
we have used the non-negativity of
\bar{V}_{0}
. In line
C.94
we have substituted for
\bar{V}_{0}
using
eq.

C.27
and used the bound
V_{B}(x)\leq(\pi x)^{-2N}
for all
x\in{\mathbbm{R}}
. We have also exchanged the limits of summation and integration. This is justified via the Weierstrass M-test (see Theorem 7.10 in
[
55
]
). In line
C.97
, we have used the fact that the expression is upper bounded by the smallest value of
N
, i.e. one.

Thus using
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
for all
a,b>0
and plugging
eq.

C.98
into
eq.

C.88
, we finalise the proof.
∎

So far in the
appendix
we have considered a Hamiltonian of the form
eq.

C.1
while we are interested in ones of the form
eq.

III.5
since these are the Hamiltonians appearing in
Theorem

2
. We now introduce a Hamiltonian of the form
eq.

III.5
and relate it to the Hamiltonian appearing in
Lemma

C.1

\displaystyle H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}=H_{\textup{C}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)},

(C.99)

where
\{I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}}^{(l)}\}_{l}
are defined by

\displaystyle I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}}^{(l)}:=\sum_{\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}}\ket{\mathbb{m}}\!\!\bra{\mathbb{m}}_{\textup{M}_{0,l}}\otimes I_{\textup{L}}^{(l,\mathbb{m})},

(C.100)

l\in 1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
, and where
\ket{\mathbb{m}}_{\textup{M}_{0,l}}
is the memory state of cell
\textup{M}_{0,l}
corresponding to gate
\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}
, where recall
\mathcal{G}
is the gate set. Each term
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l,\mathbb{m})}
is defined as follows: it has spectrum which lies in the interval
(0,2\pi]
and the unitary
U(\mathbb{m})=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{L}}^{(l,\mathbb{m})}}\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{G}}
is the representation of gate
\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}
on
L
. While
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l,\mathbb{m})}
is
l
-independent, we keep the label to distinguish it from the term
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}
.

What is more, given our previous definitions, it is readily apparent that
H_{\textup{C}}\geq 0
and
\{I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\geq 0\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
. As such
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}\geq 0
. Therefore if
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}>0
we allow for an additional vector
\ket{\textup{ground}}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
in the Hilbert space of
\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}
which is orthogonal to the terms in
eq.

C.99
. This is the ground state of
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
. As such we always have that the ground state of
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
has zero energy. This is purely for convenience since later it will allow us to calculate the mean energy of state
\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
by simply taking its trace with
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
. Since none of the states nor operators discussed in this
manuscript
have support on
\ket{\textup{ground}}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
, we neglect its mention for now on for simplicity.

C.4
Proof of the theorem

Finally we are in a stage to prove
Theorem

2
. Below we state a more explicit version of it, which is what we will prove. In particular, we state more explicitly the form which the functions
{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(\cdot)
take (One can readily check that they belong to said function class). For this, we introduce 2 new functions:

g(\bar{\varepsilon})>0
is a
E_{0}
-independent function of
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
, while
\textup{poly}(E_{0})
is an
\bar{\varepsilon}
-independent polynomial in
E_{0}
. Both are independent of the elements in
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m})\}_{\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}}
.

Theorem
2

(
[
More explicit than main-text version.
]
Optimal conventional and quantum frequential computers exist)
.

For all gate sets
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{G}}
, initial memory states
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
and initial logical states
\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{L}})
, there exists triplets
\{\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\}_{j=0}^{N_{g}}
,
N_{g}
,
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
parametrised by the energy
E_{0}>0
and a dimensionless parameter
\bar{\epsilon}
, such that for all
j=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
and fixed
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
the large-
E_{0}
scaling is as follows

\displaystyle T\Big(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j}H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}},\,\,\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\Big)\leq\left(\sum_{k=1}^{j}\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})\right)g(\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}(E_{0})\,E_{0}^{-1/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}},

(C.101)

for the following two cases:

Case 1)

\displaystyle f=\frac{1}{T_{0}}\left({T_{0}}E_{0}\right)^{1/2-\bar{\varepsilon}}+\delta f,\qquad|\delta f|\leq\frac{1}{T_{0}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\textup{poly}(E_{0})E_{0}^{-1/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}\right)\text{ as }E_{0}\to\infty,

(C.102)

and
\big(\,\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\,\big)_{j=0}^{N_{g}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{clas.}}
.

Case 2)

\displaystyle f=\frac{1}{T_{0}}\left(T_{0}E_{0}\right)^{1-\bar{\varepsilon}}+\delta f^{\prime},\qquad|\delta f^{\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{T_{0}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\textup{poly}(E_{0})E_{0}^{-1/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}\right)\text{ as }E_{0}\to\infty.

(C.103)

Proof.

We will first show that this trivially reduces to a problem not involving the memory states. Then we will prove the scaling in
eq.

C.101
via
Lemmas

C.1
,
C.3
and
C.2
, but as a function of dimension rather than initial energy. We will then proceed to bound the dimension as a function of initial energy, and frequency as a function of initial energy.

From
eq.

C.99
, we observer that
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
is block-diagonal in the basis of the memory,
\big\{\ket{\mathbb{m}_{1}}_{\textup{M}_{0,1}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{2}}_{\textup{M}_{0,2}}

\ldots

\ket{\mathbb{m}_{N_{g}}}_{\textup{M}_{0,N_{g}}}\big\}_{\mathbb{m}_{l}\in\mathcal{G}}
. Since on the l.h.s. of
eq.

C.99
, the kets are a tensor product of an element of this set, the sole effect of commuting the memory state with the exponentiated Hamiltonian, is the mapping of
H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
in
eq.

C.99
to

\displaystyle H_{\textup{C}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}I_{\textup{L}}^{(l,\mathbb{m}_{l})}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}.

(C.104)

In the remainder of this proof, we will work with Hamiltonians of this form but using the shorthand notation
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}
rather than
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l,\mathbb{m}_{l})}
. Since our proof considers operators
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}
which can implement arbitrary gates, it can implement any corresponding gate
\mathbb{m}_{l}
, from any gate set.

Plugging the bounds from
Lemmas

C.3
and
C.2
into the bounds from
Lemma

C.1
, and simplifying the resultant expression we find, for
j=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}-\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{j}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.105)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{j}\bigg(\big\|\ket{t_{k}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(C.106)

\displaystyle+t_{1}\max_{x\in[0,t_{1}]}\big\|\bar{H}_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}\bigg)

(C.107)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{j}\left[\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{v}(t_{k-1},d)}+\pi\sqrt{2dA_{\textup{nor}}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{16}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma N_{g}}\right)^{2}}+4\pi nA_{0}\left(\left(\frac{2N_{g}}{\pi^{2}\,n}\right)^{2N}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}n}\right)^{2N}\right)\right)\right]+

(C.108)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{j}\Bigg[\frac{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})4\pi^{2}nA_{0}}{T_{0}}\Biggl(3A_{\textup{nor}}d\,N_{g}\bigg(\mathrm{e}^{-{\pi}{\frac{d^{2}}{\sigma^{2}(4N_{g})^{2}}}}+\Big(\frac{2N_{g}}{\pi^{2}n}\Big)^{2N}\,\bigg)

(C.109)

\displaystyle\quad+A_{\textup{nor}}\left(\pi^{2}-\frac{79}{9}\right)N_{g}d\left(\frac{1}{2\pi n}\right)^{2N}+N_{g}d\,\left(1+\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\right)\varepsilon_{v}(t_{k-1},d)\Biggr)\Bigg]

(C.110)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{j}(j-1)\frac{T_{0}}{N_{g}}\sqrt{2\varepsilon_{v}(1,d)}+j\pi\sqrt{2dA_{\textup{nor}}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{16}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma N_{g}}\right)^{2}}+4\pi nA_{0}\left(\left(\frac{2N_{g}}{\pi^{2}\,n}\right)^{2N}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}n}\right)^{2N}\right)\right)

(C.111)

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{j}\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})\right)\Bigg[\frac{4\pi^{2}nA_{0}}{T_{0}}\Biggl(3A_{\textup{nor}}d\,N_{g}\bigg(\mathrm{e}^{-{\pi}{\frac{d^{2}}{\sigma^{2}(4N_{g})^{2}}}}+\Big(\frac{2N_{g}}{\pi^{2}n}\Big)^{2N}\,\bigg)

(C.112)

\displaystyle+A_{\textup{nor}}\left(\pi^{2}-\frac{79}{9}\right)N_{g}d\left(\frac{1}{2\pi n}\right)^{2N}+d\,\left(1+\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\right)T_{0}(j-1)\,\varepsilon_{v}(1,d)\Biggr)\Bigg],

(C.113)

where in lines
C.111
and
C.113
, we have used the definition of
\varepsilon_{v}(t,d)
from
eq.

C.40
. Now observe that in order for lines
C.111
and
C.113
to be small, we need
d/(\sigma N_{g})
to tend to infinity as
d\to\infty
, and
[2N_{g}/(\pi^{2}n)]^{N}
to tend to zero as

sufficiently quickly.
We start by recalling the definitions of
N
and
n
used in
[

]

]
):

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=\left\lceil\frac{3-4\epsilon_{5}-\epsilon_{9}}{2(\epsilon_{7}-\epsilon_{5})}\right\rceil\geq\frac{3-4\epsilon_{5}-\epsilon_{9}}{2(\epsilon_{7}-\epsilon_{5})},

(C.114)

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=\frac{\ln(\pi\alpha_{0}\sigma^{2})}{2\pi C_{0}\alpha_{0}\kappa}\frac{d^{1-\epsilon_{5}}}{\sigma},

(C.115)

11
11

11

Note that
eqs.

C.114
and
C.115
differ slightly from the definitions cited from
[

]
. Namely an
\epsilon_{8}
(which was introduced via eq. F232 in

and

C.27
. See footnote

for explanation.

where
\kappa=0.792
, and

is solely a function of

, (e.g. independent of

,

(C.116)

and is uniformly bounded from below since we assume that

,
\tilde{n}_{0}\in(0,1)
a fixed numerical constant (i.e. independent of

).
The coefficients
\epsilon_{5},\epsilon_{7},\epsilon_{9},\eta
can be chosen to be any

-independent constants satisfying the relations

\displaystyle 0<\epsilon_{5}<\epsilon_{6}=\frac{\ln\sigma}{\ln d}<1,

(C.117)

\displaystyle 0<\epsilon_{7}<\eta/2,

(C.118)

\displaystyle\!\!\epsilon_{5}<\epsilon_{7},

(C.119)

\displaystyle 0<\epsilon_{9}<\eta,

(C.120)

\displaystyle 0<3-4\epsilon_{5}-\epsilon_{9},

(C.121)

(C.122)

Let us choose

\displaystyle\epsilon_{5}

\displaystyle=\eta\bar{\varepsilon},\quad\epsilon_{7}=2\eta\bar{\varepsilon},\quad\epsilon_{9}=\eta/2,\quad\sigma=d^{\eta/2}=d^{\epsilon_{6}}

(C.123)

We observe that for this choice of constants satisfies eqs. (

) to (

) for all

and

. It now follows

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2N_{g}}{\pi^{2}n}\right)^{2N}\leq\left(\frac{4C_{0}(N)\alpha_{0}\kappa}{\pi\ln(\pi\alpha_{0})}\right)^{\!2N}d^{[\eta(\bar{\varepsilon}+1/2)-\varepsilon_{g}]3/(\eta\varepsilon)},

(C.124)

where we have defined

\displaystyle N_{g}=\lfloor d^{1-\varepsilon_{g}}\rfloor\leq d^{1-\varepsilon_{g}},\quad\varepsilon_{g}>0,

(C.125)

and were it follows from the definitions and properties of

, that the prefactor

(C.126)

is independent of

. (It is however dependent on
\bar{\varepsilon}
and

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{g}=\eta(\bar{\varepsilon}+1/2)+\eta\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}

(C.127)

thus resulting in the bound

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2N_{g}}{\pi^{2}n}\right)^{2N}\leq\left(\frac{4C_{0}(N)\alpha_{0}\kappa}{\pi\ln(\pi\alpha_{0})}\right)^{\!2N}\,d^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}.

(C.128)

Since
\eta

are

. Therefore, for sufficiently large

, we have
N_{g}>1
and both terms

,

C.128
.
For
(d/(\sigma N_{g}))^{2}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d}{\sigma N_{g}}\right)^{2}=\frac{d^{2-\eta}}{\left(\lfloor d^{1-\eta(\bar{\varepsilon}+1/2)-\eta\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}\rfloor\right)^{2}}\sim d^{2\eta(\bar{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}})},

(C.129)

as

.

Finally, from

Therefore, taking into account that

, and

for sufficiently large

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}-\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{j}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}\leq\left(\sum_{k=1}^{j}\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})\right)f(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}(d)\,d^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}},

(C.130)

for all
\eta\in(0,1]
,
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
,

. The function
f\geq 0
is independent of

and the elements
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})\}_{k=1}^{j}
. Meanwhile,
\textup{poly}(d)\geq 0
is independent of,
\eta
,
\bar{\varepsilon}
, and the elements
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})\}_{k=1}^{j}
. Note that while the degree of the polynomial

is easily deducible from line

, it is not important for our purposes. We can lower bound the difference in kets appearing on the l.h.s. of

C.130
in terms of trace distance rather (than 2-norm) as per
