2
, via the use of
Lemma

C.5
.

We now turn our attention to calculating the mean energy of the initial state. Recall the discussion below
eq.

C.99
: the Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
has a ground-state energy of zero. As such the mean energy of the initial state is

\displaystyle{}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\!\!\bra{\vec{\mathbb{m}}}{}_{\textup{L}}\!\bra{0}{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{0}H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}\ket{\vec{\mathbb{m}}}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}

\displaystyle={}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{\Psi(0)}H_{\textup{C}}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}+\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}}{}_{\textup{L}}\!\bra{0}I_{\textup{L}}^{(l,\mathbb{m}_{l})}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\,{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{\Psi(0)}I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}.

(C.131)

We will now show that the terms
\{{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{\Psi(0)}I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
are zero in the large
d
limit. For all
l=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
we find

\displaystyle\left|{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{\Psi(0)}I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}{(k_{0})}}\left|\bar{V}_{0}\left(\frac{2\pi}{d}k\right){\bigg|}_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{(l)}}\right||\braket{\theta_{k}|\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}|^{2}

(C.132)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}A_{\textup{nor}}nA_{0}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}{(0)}}\sum_{p=-\infty}^{\infty}V_{B}\Big(n2\pi(kN_{g}/d-l+1/2+N_{g}p)/N_{g}\Big)\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\left(\frac{k}{\sigma}\right)^{2}}

(C.133)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}A_{\textup{nor}}nA_{0}\bigg(d\max_{k^{\prime}\in[-1/2,1/2]}\sum_{p\in\{0,\pm 1\}}V_{B}\Big(n2\pi(k^{\prime}N_{g}-l+1/2+N_{g}p)/N_{g}\Big)\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\left(\frac{k^{\prime}}{\sigma}\right)^{2}}

(C.134)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}(0)}\sum_{p\in{\mathbbm{Z}}\backslash\{0,\pm 1\}}\Big|n2\pi^{2}(kN_{g}/d-l+1/2+N_{g}p)/N_{g}\Big|^{-2N}\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\left(\frac{k}{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\bigg)

(C.135)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}A_{\textup{nor}}nA_{0}\bigg[2d\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\left(\frac{d}{4N_{g}\sigma}\right)^{2}}+d\max_{k^{\prime}\in[-1/(4N_{g}),1/(4N_{g})]}\sum_{p\in\{0,\pm 1\}}\!\!\!\!\!V_{B}\Big(\frac{n2\pi}{N_{g}}(k^{\prime}N_{g}-l+1/2+N_{g}p)\Big)

(C.136)

\displaystyle+d(n2\pi^{2})^{-2N}\!\!\!\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathcal{S}(0)\\
l\in\{1,2,\ldots,N_{g}\}\end{subarray}}\sum_{p=2}^{\infty}\left(\Big|\frac{k}{d}-(l-1/2)/N_{g}+p\Big|^{-2N}+\Big|\frac{k}{d}-(l-1/2)/N_{g}-p\Big|^{-2N}\right)\bigg]

(C.137)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}A_{\textup{nor}}nA_{0}\bigg[2d\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\left(\frac{d}{4N_{g}\sigma}\right)^{2}}+d\max_{q^{\prime}\in[-1/4,1/4]}\sum_{p\in\{0,\pm 1\}}\Bigg|\frac{n2\pi^{2}}{N_{g}}(q-l+1/2+N_{g}p)\Bigg|^{-2N}

(C.138)

\displaystyle+d(n2\pi^{2})^{-2N}\,\sum_{p=2}^{\infty}\left(\Big|-\frac{1}{2}-1+p\Big|^{-2N}+\Big|\frac{1}{2}-p\Big|^{-2N}\right)\bigg]

(C.139)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}A_{\textup{nor}}nA_{0}\bigg[2d\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\left(\frac{d}{4N_{g}\sigma}\right)^{2}}+3d\,\Bigg|\frac{n2\pi^{2}}{4N_{g}}\Bigg|^{-2N}

(C.140)

\displaystyle+d(n2\pi^{2})^{-2N}\sum_{p=2}^{\infty}\left(\Big|-\frac{1}{2}-1+p\Big|^{-2N}+\Big|\frac{1}{2}-p\Big|^{-2N}\right)\bigg]

(C.141)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}A_{\textup{nor}}nA_{0}d\Bigg[2\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\left(\frac{d}{4N_{g}\sigma}\right)^{2}}+3\Bigg(\frac{2N_{g}}{\pi^{2}n}\Bigg)^{2N}+\pi^{2}\left(\frac{1}{n2\pi^{2}}\right)^{2N}\Bigg],

(C.142)

where in line
C.132
we have used
eq.

C.26
. In line
C.133
we have set
k_{0}=0
(recall that
I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}
is independent of this parameter due to the periodicity of the summand). This choice means we can easily calculate the overlaps
\braket{\theta_{k}|\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}
. In line
C.136
we have used the fact that
k^{\prime}=k/d\in[-1/2,1/2]=[-1/2,-1/(4N_{g})]\cup[-1/(4N_{g}),1/(4N_{g})]\cup[1/(4N_{g}),1/2]
and the bound
V_{B}(x)\leq 1
for all
x\in{\mathbbm{R}}
. In line
C.139
we have used the fact that
k/d-(l-1/2)/N_{g}\in[-1/2-1,1/2]
for all
k\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(0)
,
l\in\{1,2,\ldots,N_{g}\}
,
N_{g}\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
. In line
C.140
we have used the fact that
-l+1/2+N_{g}p
is always a half integer. In line
C.142
we have used
N\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
.

Therefore, using
eq.

C.128
and the similar lines of reasoning to those used just after this equation, we conclude

\displaystyle\Bigg|\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}}{}_{\textup{L}}\!\bra{0}I_{\textup{L}}^{(l,\mathbb{m}_{l})}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\,{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{\Psi(0)}I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi d\max_{l=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}}\Bigg|{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{\Psi(0)}I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}\Bigg|

(C.143)

\displaystyle\leq f^{\prime}(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}^{\prime}(d)\,d^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}},

(C.144)

for all
\eta\in(0,1]
,
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
,
d\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
. In line
C.143
we used the fact that the spectrum of
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l,\mathbb{m}_{l})}
is in the interval
(0,2\pi]
. The function
f^{\prime}\geq 0
is independent of
d
and
\textup{poly}^{\prime}(d)\geq 0
is independent of
\eta
, and
\bar{\varepsilon}
.

In
[
70
]
, the mean energy of the initial state
\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}
for the Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{C}}
was calculated. It was found that

\displaystyle 0<{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{\Psi(0)}H_{\textup{C}}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}=\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}n_{0}+\epsilon_{E},

(C.145)

where

\displaystyle|\epsilon_{E}|\leq\textup{poly}^{\prime\prime}(d)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{4}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma}\right)^{2}}.

(C.146)

Recall that we assume in this manuscript that
n_{0}=\tilde{n}_{0}(d-1)
, with
\tilde{n}_{0}\in(0,1)
and
d
-independent. It is easily verified that
\textup{poly}^{\prime\prime}(d)
is independent of the parameters
\eta
,
\bar{\varepsilon}
introduced here. Since
\sigma=d^{\eta/2}
,
\eta\in(0,1]
here, we have using
eq.

C.131
that

\displaystyle E_{0}:={}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\!\!\bra{\vec{\mathbb{m}}}{}_{\textup{L}}\!\bra{0}{}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{0}H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}\ket{\vec{\mathbb{m}}}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}\tilde{n}_{0}(d-1)+\delta E^{\prime},

(C.147)

where

\displaystyle|\delta E^{\prime}|\leq f^{\prime\prime}(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}^{\prime\prime\prime}(d)\,d^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}},

(C.148)

with
f^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
independent of
d
and
\textup{poly}^{\prime\prime\prime}(d)\geq 0
independent of
\eta
, and
\bar{\varepsilon}
. Recall that
\tilde{n}_{0}\in(0,1)
a fixed numerical constant (i.e. independent of
d
). Therefore,

\displaystyle d=\frac{T_{0}}{2\pi\tilde{n}_{0}}E_{0}+\delta d,\qquad\delta d:=1-\frac{T_{0}}{2\pi\tilde{n}_{0}}\delta E^{\prime}.

(C.149)

This provides a parametrization of
d
in terms of
E_{0}
. We have to be cautions, because
\delta E^{\prime}
depends on
\eta
,
\bar{\varepsilon}
and
d
, and thus if we plug this relation into a function which depends on
d
but not on
\eta
, or
\bar{\varepsilon}
, we will not obtain a function which depends on
E_{0}
but not on
\eta
, or
\bar{\varepsilon}
. Nevertheless,
note that since
\delta d
converges to
1
in the large
d
limit for all
\eta\in(0,1]
and
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
, we have that for all
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
, here exists
E_{00}(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})>0
such that for all
\eta\in(0,1]
,
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
, and
E_{0}\geq E_{00}(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})
, the following holds

\displaystyle f(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}(d)\,d^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}\leq f(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})\left(\frac{T_{0}^{2}}{2\pi\tilde{n}_{0}}\right)^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,\textup{poly}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(E_{0})\,E_{0}^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}},

(C.150)

where
\textup{poly}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(E_{0})>0
for all
E_{0}\geq 0
, is a polynomial which is independent of
\eta
and
\bar{\varepsilon}
. Now let
R
denoted the ratio between the l.h.s and the r.h.s. of
eq.

C.150
and let
h>0
be defined by
h:=\max_{E_{0}\in[0,E_{00}(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})]}R(E_{0})
, where the l.h.s. of
eq.

C.150
is written as a function of
E_{0}
rather than
d
by virtue of
eq.

C.149
. Clearly
h
depends on
\eta
and
\bar{\varepsilon}
but not
E_{0}
. Therefore there exists a function
g(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})>0
which is
E_{0}
-independent such that

\displaystyle f(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}(d)\,d^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}\leq g(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(E_{0})\,E_{0}^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}},

(C.151)

holds for all
E_{0}\geq 0
obeying
eq.

C.149
. Plugging this relation into the r.h.s. of
eq.

C.130
, we obtain
eq.

C.101
after a re-labelling of
\textup{poly}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(E_{0})
by
\textup{poly}(E_{0})
, and defining
g(\bar{\varepsilon})=g(\eta(\bar{\varepsilon}),\bar{\varepsilon})
, where the parametrization
\eta(\bar{\varepsilon})
is chosen differently in cases 1) and 2) below.

We now turn our attention to calculating the gate frequency as a function of the mean energy
E_{0}
. Using
eq.

C.125

\displaystyle f=\frac{N_{g}}{T_{0}}=\frac{\lfloor d^{1-\varepsilon_{g}}\rfloor}{T_{0}}=\frac{\left(\frac{T_{0}}{2\pi\tilde{n}_{0}}E_{0}+\delta d\right)^{1-\varepsilon_{g}}+\delta_{1}}{T_{0}}=\frac{1}{T_{0}}{\left(\frac{T_{0}}{2\pi\tilde{n}_{0}}E_{0}\right)^{1-\varepsilon_{g}}}+\delta f,\qquad|\delta f|\leq\scalebox{1.0}{$\left|\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}\frac{\left(\frac{T_{0}}{2\pi\tilde{n}_{0}}E_{0}\right)^{-\varepsilon_{g}}}{T_{0}}\delta d+\frac{\delta_{1}}{T_{0}}\scalebox{1.0}{$\left|\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$},

(C.152)

where
\delta_{1}:=-d^{1-\varepsilon_{g}}+\lfloor d^{1-\varepsilon_{g}}\rfloor\in(-1,0]
.
For case 1) of
Theorem

2
, we choose
\eta
such that
\varepsilon_{g}=1/2+\bar{\varepsilon}
, which from
eq.

C.127
gives us

\displaystyle\eta(\bar{\varepsilon})=\frac{1/2+\bar{\varepsilon}}{1/2+\bar{\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}

(C.153)

which satisfies the condition
\eta\in(0,1]
for all
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
.
To finalise case 1) of the proof, we need to show that
\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{clas.}}
. Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{clas.}}
from
Section

A.3.2
, this consists in showing that
\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}
is an eigenstate of
L_{\textup{C}}=\lambda t_{\textup{C}}/T_{0}+\mathrm{i}H_{\textup{C}}T_{0}
with
|\lambda|=1
up to an additive vanishing term in the large
d
limit. Let
x
and
p
denoted canonically conjugate position and momentum operators. In
[
70
]
, it was shown that the action of
t_{\textup{C}}
and
H_{\textup{C}}
on the state
\ket{\Psi(td/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}
of width
\sigma
, is equal to the action of
\hat{x}
and
\hat{p}
on a Gaussian wave-packet state of the same width (up to additive exponentially small correction terms). In
[
47
, 4.1 Position measurements]
(also see
[
64
]
) the authors show that said Gaussian wave-packet states are eigenstates of
\lambda\hat{x}+\mathrm{i}\hat{p}
, for
\lambda=1
when the width of the Gaussian wave-packet is of equal width in both the position and momentum basis. These are the states
\ket{\Psi(td/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}
we have used in the classical case, as is manifest by the fact that they also have width
\sigma
which is equal in both these conjugate bases (i.e.
\sigma=d^{1/\eta}=\sqrt{d}
).

For case 2), we choose
\eta
such that
\varepsilon_{g}=\bar{\varepsilon}
which gives, using
eq.

C.127
,

\displaystyle\eta({\bar{\varepsilon}})=\frac{2\bar{\varepsilon}}{2\bar{\varepsilon}+2\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}+1}

(C.154)

and tends to zero from above as
\bar{\varepsilon}
tends to zero from above.
We can therefore make
\varepsilon_{g}
arbitrarily small by choosing
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small.
Now, from
eq.

C.152
it follows

\displaystyle f=\frac{1}{T_{0}}\left(\frac{T_{0}}{2\pi\tilde{n}_{0}}E_{0}\right)^{1-\bar{\varepsilon}}+\delta f^{\prime},\qquad|\delta f^{\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{T_{0}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\textup{poly}(E_{0})E_{0}^{-1/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}\right)\text{ as }E_{0}\to\infty.

(C.155)

To achieve
eq.

III.12
, we choose
\tilde{n}_{0}=1/(2\pi)
. This is consistent with the parameter regime of
\tilde{n}_{0}
[see text below
eq.

C.116
]. Note that we cannot make
f
arbitrarily large by choosing
\tilde{n}_{0}
arbitrarily small because in said limit
E_{0}
also becomes arbitrarily small [recall
eq.

C.147
].
∎

C.5
Generic known useful technical lemmas

The following two lemmas are rather trivial but crucial for this work.

Lemma C.4

(Unitary errors add linearly: Lemma C.0.2. in
[
70
]
or
[
49
]
)
.

Let
\{\ket{\Phi_{m}}\}_{m=1}^{n}
be a set of states
12
12

12

In Lemma C.0.2. in
[
70
]
these states were normalised by definition. Here we remove this assumption since it is not necessary and we will use it in the proof of
Theorem

4
in the case of sub-normalized states.
in a 2-normed vector space satisfying
\|\ket{\Phi_{m}}-\Delta_{m}\ket{\Phi_{m-1}}\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon_{m}
,
\|\Delta_{m}\|_{2}\leq 1
. Then

\displaystyle\|\ket{\Phi_{n}}-\Delta_{n}\Delta_{n-1}\ldots\Delta_{1}\ket{\Phi_{0}}\|_{2}\leq\sum_{m=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{m}.

(C.156)

Proof.

By induction. The theorem is true by definition for
n=1
, and if the theorem is true for all
n
up to
k
, then for
n=k+1
,

\displaystyle\|\left|\Phi_{k+1}\right\rangle-\Delta_{k+1}\Delta_{k}\ldots\Delta_{1}\left|\Phi_{0}\right\rangle\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|\left|\Phi_{k+1}\right\rangle-\Delta_{k+1}\left|\Phi_{k}\right\rangle+\Delta_{k+1}\left(\left|\Phi_{k}\right\rangle-\Delta_{k}\ldots\Delta_{1}\left|\Phi_{0}\right\rangle\right)\|_{2}

(C.157)

\displaystyle\leq\|\left|\Phi_{k+1}\right\rangle-\Delta_{k+1}\left|\Phi_{k}\right\rangle\left\|{}_{2}+\right\|\Delta_{k+1}\left(\left|\Phi_{k}\right\rangle-\Delta_{k}\ldots\Delta_{1}\left|\Phi_{0}\right\rangle\right)\|_{2}

(C.158)

\displaystyle\leq\|\left|\Phi_{k+1}\right\rangle-\Delta_{k+1}\left|\Phi_{k}\right\rangle\left\|{}_{2}+\right\|\Delta_{k+1}\left\|{}_{2}\right\|\left(\left|\Phi_{k}\right\rangle-\Delta_{k}\ldots\Delta_{1}\left|\Phi_{0}\right\rangle\right)\|_{2}

(C.159)

\displaystyle\leq\|\left|\Phi_{k+1}\right\rangle-\Delta_{k+1}\left|\Phi_{k}\right\rangle\left\|{}_{2}+\right\|\left|\Phi_{k}\right\rangle-\Delta_{k}\ldots\Delta_{1}\left|\Phi_{0}\right\rangle\|_{2}

(C.160)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{k+1}+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\epsilon_{m}=\sum_{m=1}^{k+1}\epsilon_{m}

(C.161)

where we used the Minkowski vector norm inequality and the equivalence between the induced
l_{2}
operator norm and the property
\left\|\Delta_{m}\right\|_{2}\leq 1
in line
C.159
.
∎

Lemma C.5

(Upper bounding trace distance by Euclidean distance for pure states)
.

The trace distance and Euclidean distance between two normalised pure states
\ket{A}
,
\ket{B}
is

\displaystyle T\big(\ket{A},\ket{B}\big)=\sqrt{1-|\braket{A|B}|^{2}}\,,\qquad\|\ket{A}-\ket{B}\|_{2}=\sqrt{1-\Re\big[\!\braket{A|B}\!\big]},

(C.162)

respectively. They are related by

\displaystyle T\big(\ket{A},\ket{B}\big)\leq\sqrt{2}\,\|\ket{A}-\ket{B}\|_{2}.

(C.163)

Proof.

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-|\braket{A|B}|^{2}}=\sqrt{1-\Re(\braket{A|B})^{2}-\Im(\braket{A|B})^{2}}\leq\sqrt{1-\Re(\braket{A|B})^{2}}=\|\ket{A}-\ket{B}\|_{2}\sqrt{1+\|\ket{A}-\ket{B}\|_{2}^{2}/4}

(C.164)

\displaystyle\leq\|\ket{A}-\ket{B}\|_{2}\sqrt{2}

(C.165)

∎

Appendix D
Interaction strengths in the semi-classical and fully quantum control-state cases

Here we show that the interaction strength is not stronger in the case that achieves the linear scaling of gate frequency
f
with mean energy
E_{0}
, compared with the semi-classical state limit where it depends on
\sqrt{E_{0}}
. For a longer discussion on the importance of these observations, see
Section

VII.1
. We will then compare this with the interaction strength ubiquitously found in nature, namely a linear (in the creation and annihilation operators) interaction. We will show that we do not require a stronger interaction term for our results. For concreteness, we will focus on the interactions used in the proof of
Theorem

, but one can readily check that we have used the same interaction terms in the later theorems, and hence the conclusions we arrive at here hold generally.

To start with, recall that the total interaction responsible for implementing the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
gate is
I^{(l)}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}}\otimes I^{(l)}_{\textup{C}}
. We will characterise its “strength” by upper bounding how its norm grows with
d
(the Hilbert space dimension of
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{C}}
). This is the relevant parameter, since we have seen that its is proportional (up to decaying additive terms) to the total mean energy of the total system,
E_{0}
(recall
eq.

C.147
). We will also multiply said interaction terms by
T_{0}
, since (as we have seen), the quantum advantage of the quantum frequential computer is independent of the value of
T_{0}>0
.

The term
I^{(l)}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}}
solely depends on the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
gate to be implemented (namely
U(\mathbb{m}_{l})
), and (as we have seen) it is independent of
d
(and
E_{0}
) in both cases (i.e. in both the semi-classical states and squeezed state case,
eqs.

C.101
and
III.11
respectively). It thus constitutes a constant factor when computing the strength of
I^{(l)}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}}\otimes I^{(l)}_{\textup{C}}
, and we can neglect it. We thus focus on the terms
I^{(l)}_{\textup{C}}
: From
eq.

C.26
, we have
Hilbert–Schmidt norm

\displaystyle\|I^{(l)}_{\textup{C}}\|_{2}=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi d}}{T_{0}}\sqrt{\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(k_{0})}\frac{2\pi}{d}{\bar{V}_{0}}^{2}\left(\frac{2\pi}{d}k\right)\Bigg|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{(l)}}}.

(D.1)

Therefore, in the large
d
limit,

\displaystyle\frac{T_{0}}{\sqrt{2\pi d}}\|I^{(l)}_{\textup{C}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\frac{2\pi}{d}{\bar{V}_{0}}^{2}\left(\frac{2\pi}{d}k+\frac{2\pi}{d}\lceil-d/2+k_{0}\rceil\right)\Bigg|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{(l)}}}=\sqrt{\int_{0}^{2\pi}dx\,{\bar{V}_{0}}^{2}\left(x+\frac{2\pi}{d}\lceil-d/2+k_{0}\rceil\right)\Bigg|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{(l)}}}

(D.2)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\int_{0}^{2\pi}dx\,{\bar{V}_{0}}^{2}\left(x\right)\bigg|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{(l)}}}=\|\bar{V}_{0}\|_{2}\leq\|\bar{V}_{0}\|_{1}=1,

(D.3)

where we have defined

\displaystyle\|\bar{V}_{0}\|_{p}:=\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi}dx\,{\bar{V}_{0}}^{p}\left(x\right)\Big|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{(l)}}\right)^{1/p},

(D.4)

and where in line
D.2
we have used the Riemannian integral, while in line
D.3
we have used the fact that the smooth function
\bar{V}_{0}
is
2\pi
periodic and integrating periodic functions over their period is invariant under a shift of the function’s argument. For the last equality, we have used fact that the 1-norm upper bounds the 2-norm and recalled that
\bar{V}_{0}\geq 0
and used
eq.

C.28
(which holds independently of the value of real parameter
x_{0}
).

Thus we see that the interaction strength grows at most linearly in
\sqrt{d}
regardless of if we are in the case of semi-classical control states only or any state of the control. Moreover, we can compare this growth with that of the free evolution of the control,
H_{\textup{C}}
. Using definition
C.24
, we have, in the large
d
limit

\displaystyle\frac{T_{0}}{\sqrt{2\pi d}}\|H_{\textup{C}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{d}\sum_{n=0}^{d-1}n^{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{3}}\sqrt{2d^{2}-3d+1}=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{3}}d.

(D.5)

Thus the interaction term grows more slowly in Hilbert–Schmidt norm than the interaction term responsible for implementing the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
gate.

Finally, recall that the term in the total Hamiltonian responsible for free evolution of the control, namely
H_{\textup{C}}
, is that of the
1{{}^{\text{st}}}

d
levels of a quantum harmonic oscillator with frequency
\omega_{0}=2\pi/T_{0}
. We can alternatively think of this Hamiltonian on
C
as being that of the quantum harmonic oscillator and restricting the control state to states without support on energy levels greater than
\omega_{0}d
(or, more generally, it could have support on higher levels but with said support is such tiny magnitude that it has negligible effect on the dynamics). In this scenario, a common interaction term found in many systems is that of a linear coupling in creation-annihilation operators to the systems the quantum harmonic oscillator interacts with. If we assume this, a meaningful comparison of interaction strength is to consider the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of the creation or annihilation operator when truncated to the 1st
d
levels in energy, which we denote
a_{d}^{\dagger}
,
a_{d}
respectively. Thus using the expression
a^{\dagger}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{j}\ket{E_{j+1}}\!\!\bra{E_{j}}
, (where
\ket{E_{j}}
is the
j{{}^{\text{th}}}
energy level of the harmonic oscillator), we find in the large
d
limit

\displaystyle\|a_{d}^{\dagger}\|_{2}=\|a_{d}\|_{2}=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}j}=\frac{\sqrt{(d-1)d}}{\sqrt{2}}=\frac{d}{2}.

(D.6)

Thus we see from
eqs.

D.5
and
D.6
that the Hilbert–Schmidt norm of the free Hamiltonian,
\|H_{\textup{C}}\|_{2}
, grows faster with
d
than that of creation-annihilation couplings,
\|a_{d}^{\dagger}\|_{2}
,
\|a_{d}\|_{2}
by a multiplicative factor of
\sqrt{d}
. However, the Hilbert–Schmidt norm interaction terms
I^{(l)}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}}\otimes I^{(l)}_{\textup{C}}
grow slower than
\|H_{\textup{C}}\|_{2}
by at least a multiplicative factor of
d
and slower than
\|a_{d}^{\dagger}\|_{2}
or
\|a_{d}\|_{2}
by a at least a
\sqrt{d}
multiplicative factor.

Appendix E
Proof of
Theorem

3
: Attaining the quantum limit with only a semi-classical bus

The proof will rely heavily on material from
Appendix

C
. We first prove a theorem which allows us to decouple the errors originating from the control of the gates and the control of the memory.

For this we will need to introduce a few definitions (eqs. (
E.1
) are reproduced from the main text for convenience).

\displaystyle H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}

\displaystyle:=H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}+H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}},\quad H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}:=H_{\textup{C}_{2}}+\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}}I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)},\quad H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}:=H_{\textup{C}}+\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}}I_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{L}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)},

(E.1)

\displaystyle H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}

\displaystyle:=H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}+\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)},

(E.2)

k=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
, where we define
H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}
via

\displaystyle H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}

\displaystyle:=H_{\textup{C}}+I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}}^{(k)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(k)},

(E.3)

\displaystyle I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}}^{(k)}

\displaystyle:=I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}}^{(k)}+\ket{{\bf 0}}\!\!\bra{{\bf 0}}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}}\otimes I^{(k)}_{\textup{W}_{k}},

(E.4)

with
I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}}^{(k)}
defined in
eq.

C.100
and we restrict the spectrum of
I_{\textup{W}_{k}}^{(k)}
to lie in the interval
(0,2\pi]
. The term
\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}
is defined via

\displaystyle\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}:=H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}-I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(k)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}=H_{\textup{C}_{2}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)},

(E.5)

where
H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}
is defined in
eq.

IV.10
to be
H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}=H_{\textup{C}_{2}}+\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}}I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)}
. Here we additionally define the structure

\displaystyle I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(l)}:=I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}\otimes\ket{\textup{on}}\!\!\bra{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{l}}.

(E.6)

We will define the terms
\{I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)},I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)}\}_{l}
and
H_{\textup{C}_{2}}
in the proofs when the need for their definitions arises. The terms
\{I_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}}^{(l)},I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\}_{l}
and
H_{\textup{C}}
have been defined in
Appendix

C
.

E.1
Main technical lemma: A decoupling of error contributions

The following lemma permits one to decouple the errors resulting from the application of the gates under Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
and the errors due to shuttling memory around via Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}
(Indeed, in the following, lines
E.8
and
E.10
correspond to errors associated to dynamics under
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
while lines
E.9
and
E.11
, to errors associated to dynamics under
H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}
).

Unless stated otherwise, the lemmas and theorems in this section hold for all states
\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{clas.}}
. The states
\{\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\}_{k,r}
are assumed to obey
eq.

IV.8
throughout. They will later be further specialised in
Section

E.3
and all proceeding lemmas and theorems will apply to these specialised versions. Likewise the states of the control on
C
,
\{\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\}_{k,r}
, will be general at first and then specialised. The same is true for the Hamiltonians. The only generic assumption is that they act on a finite-dimensional Hilbert-space and are Hermitian (the former assumption could be easily relaxed for a lot of the lemmas in this section, but for our purposes this will be irrelevant and thus we have this assumption for simplicity).

The states
\{\ket{t_{r,k}}_{\textup{W}}\}_{r,k}
and
\{\ket{t_{k,l}}_{\textup{L}}\}_{k,l}
are given by
eqs.

IV.5
,
IV.6
and
IV.2
respectively throughout. All lemmas and theorems hold for any gate set
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Lemma E.1

(Quantum-control-and-bus error decoupling)
.

For
j=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
and
l=0,1,2,\ldots,L
we have

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j,l}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\ket{0}_{\textup{W}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}-\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.7)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\Bigg(\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{r,k})}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.8)

\displaystyle\quad+\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,k}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{W}}_{k}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,k}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{W}}_{k}}\big\|_{2}

(E.9)

\displaystyle\quad+t_{1}\max_{x\in[0,t_{1}]}\max_{\,\,\,\big\{\mathbb{m}_{l}\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{{\bf 0}\}\big\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}}\big\|\bar{H}_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\vec{\mathbb{m}})}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.10)

\displaystyle\quad+t_{1}\max_{y\in[0,t_{1}]}\big\|\left(I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(k)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}y\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\big\|_{2}\Bigg)

(E.11)

where

\displaystyle H_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}

\displaystyle:={}_{\textup{M}_{0,\scriptscriptstyle{\#}}}\!\!\braket{\vec{\mathbb{m}}|H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}|\vec{\mathbb{m}}}_{\textup{M}_{0,\scriptscriptstyle{\#}}}=H_{\textup{C}}+I_{\textup{L}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}+I_{\textup{W}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}

(E.12)

\displaystyle H_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{r,k})}

\displaystyle:=H_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}{\big|}_{\mathbb{m}_{k}\,\mapsto\,\mathbb{m}_{r,k}}

(E.13)

\displaystyle\bar{H}_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\vec{\mathbb{m}})}

\displaystyle:={}_{\textup{M}_{0,\scriptscriptstyle{\#}}}\!\!\braket{\vec{\mathbb{m}}|\left(H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}-H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}\right)|\vec{\mathbb{m}}}_{\textup{M}_{0,\scriptscriptstyle{\#}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=1\\
q\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\left(I_{\textup{L}}^{(q,\mathbb{m}_{q})}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(q)}+I_{\textup{W}}^{(q,\mathbb{m}_{q})}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(q)}\right),

(E.14)

where
\vec{\mathbb{m}}=(\mathbb{m}_{1},\mathbb{m}_{2},\ldots,\mathbb{m}_{N_{g}})
,
\ket{\vec{\mathbb{m}}}_{\textup{M}_{0,\scriptscriptstyle{\#}}}:=\ket{\mathbb{m}_{1}}_{\textup{M}_{0,1}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{2}}_{\textup{M}_{0,2}}\ldots\ket{\mathbb{m}_{N_{g}}}_{\textup{M}_{0,{N_{g}}}}
with
\mathbb{m}_{l}\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{{\bf 0}\}
and recall
I_{\textup{L}}^{(q,\mathbb{m}_{q})}
is defined in
eq.

C.100
for
\mathbb{m}_{l}\in\mathcal{G}
. For
\mathbb{m}_{l}={\bf 0}
we define
I_{\textup{L}}^{(k,{\bf 0})}:=\hat{0}
(with
\hat{0}
the zero operator) and
I_{\textup{W}}^{(k,{\bf 0})}:=I_{\textup{W}_{k}}^{(k)}
and
I_{\textup{W}_{k}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}:=\hat{0}
for all
\mathbb{m}_{l}\in\mathcal{G}
.

Note that while in the above 4 lines on the r.h.s. of the inequality, the kets belong to different tensor-product subspaces, these subspaces are consistent with the spaces upon which the distinct Hamiltonians act non-trivially upon.

Proof.

For
j=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
,
l=0,1,2,\ldots,L

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j,l}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\ket{0}_{\textup{W}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}-\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.15)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{C}}

(E.16)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.17)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\bigg(\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{C}}

(E.18)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.19)

\displaystyle\quad+t_{1}\max_{x\in[0,t_{1}]}\big\|\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}\bigg),

(E.20)

where
H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}
is given by
eq.

E.2
and
\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}
by

\displaystyle\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}:=H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}-H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}=H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}-H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}+I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(k)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}=\bar{H}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}+I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(k)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)},

(E.21)

where in the last line we substituted using
eq.

C.99
. Lines
E.16
to
E.20
follow analogously to the proof of
Lemma

C.1
. Now recall that
I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(l)}
acts trivially on memory cells
\textup{M}_{0,1},\textup{M}_{0,2},\ldots,\textup{M}_{0,l-1},\textup{M}_{0,l+1},\ldots,\textup{M}_{0,N_{g}}
and switches
\textup{W}_{1},\textup{W}_{2},\ldots,\textup{W}_{l-1},\textup{W}_{l+1},\ldots,\textup{W}_{N_{g}}
(see
eq.

IV.10
). Therefore, it follows from the definitions of
H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}
and
\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}
that these terms commute since they only act non-trivially on different Hilbert spaces. Therefore
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}
. Now recall the following identities for operators
O_{\textup{A}}
,
O_{\textup{B}}
and kets
\ket{A}_{\textup{A}}
,
\ket{A^{\prime}}_{\textup{A}}
and
\ket{B}_{\textup{B}}
,
\ket{B^{\prime}}_{\textup{B}}
, on Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{A}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{B}}
respectively:
\|\ket{A}_{\textup{A}}\ket{B}_{\textup{B}}-\ket{A^{\prime}}_{\textup{A}}\ket{B^{\prime}}_{\textup{B}}\|_{2}\leq\|\ket{A}_{\textup{A}}-\ket{A^{\prime}}_{\textup{A}}\|_{2}+\|\ket{B}_{\textup{B}}-\ket{B^{\prime}}_{\textup{B}}\|_{2}
, which follows from the triangle inequality, and
\|(O_{\textup{A}}\otimes O_{\textup{B}})\ket{A}_{\textup{A}}\ket{B}_{\textup{B}}\|_{2}=\|O_{\textup{A}}\ket{A}_{\textup{A}}\|_{2}\,\|O_{\textup{B}}\ket{B}_{\textup{B}}\|_{2}
.

For
j=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
,
l\in 0,1,2,\ldots,L

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j,l}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\ket{0}_{\textup{W}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}-\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.22)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\Bigg(

(E.23)

\displaystyle\quad+\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}}\!\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\!\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.24)

\displaystyle\quad+\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,k}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{W}}_{k}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,k}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{W}}_{k}}\big\|_{2}

(E.25)

\displaystyle\quad+t_{1}\max_{x\in[0,t_{1}]}\bigg(\big\|\bar{H}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\big\|_{2}

(E.26)

\displaystyle\quad+\big\|\left(I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(k)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\big\|_{2}\bigg)\Bigg),

(E.27)

where in line
E.27
we have used the fact that
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}
is block-diagonal in the
\{\ket{\mathbb{m}}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}}\}_{\mathbb{m}\in{\mathcal{G}}\cup\{{\bf 0}\}}
basis. To complete the proof for line
E.24
, we first recall
eq.

IV.8
(which asserts that
\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}}=\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}}=\ket{\mathbb{m}_{k,r}}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}}
). Now taking into account the block diagonality of
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}
, we conclude line
E.8
. For line
E.26
, first note that
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}=\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k,\vec{w})}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}
where
\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k,\vec{w})}

:=

{}_{\textup{W}}\!\braket{t_{k-1,r}|\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}|t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}
, since
H_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}
is block-diagonal in the basis of the switches:
\{\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{l}},\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{l}}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
. Second, we expand the normalised vector
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}x\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k,\vec{w})}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}
in the orthonormal basis of the memory
\big\{\Pi_{k,l}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{k,l}}_{\textup{M}_{k,l}}\,|\,\mathbb{m}_{k,l}\in\mathcal{G}\text{ or }\mathbb{m}_{0,l}={\bf 0}\big\}
and an arbitrary orthonormal basis for the state of
\textup{C}_{2}
. Third, we now note that
\bar{H}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}
acts trivially on
\textup{C}_{2}
and is block-diagonal in the above orthonormal basis of the memory. By applying the definition of the two-norm, line
E.10
follows.

∎

E.2
Lemmas bounding error contributions from control on
C

We will now state and prove a lemma which will bound lines
E.8
and
E.10
in
eq.

E.7
. We use the same specialised control states as in
Appendix

C
, namely
\{\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{C}}=\ket{\Psi(t_{k,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\}_{k,r}
where
\ket{\Psi(td/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}
is defined in
eq.

C.34
. These states satisfy the cyclicity condition,
eq.

IV.1
, as shown in the proof to the following lemma.

Lemma E.2

(Bound on quantum-control-like terms)
.

There exists parametrizations of the control states
\{\ket{\Psi(t_{k,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\}_{k,r}
(
k=0,1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
;
r=0,1,2,\ldots,L
) and Hamiltonian
H_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}
in terms of
\bar{\varepsilon}
such that the following holds for all
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
and
j=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
;
l\in 0,1,\ldots,L
,

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\bigg(\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{r,k})}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.28)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad+t_{1}\max_{x\in[0,t_{1}]}\max_{\,\,\,\{\mathbb{m}_{l}\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{{\bf 0}\}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}}\big\|\bar{H}_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\vec{\mathbb{m}})}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}\bigg)

(E.29)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})\right)h(\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}(d)\,d^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}},

(E.30)

and

\displaystyle N_{g}=\lfloor d^{1-\bar{\varepsilon}}\rfloor,

(E.31)

where the function
h\geq 0
is independent of
d
and the elements
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})\}_{k=1}^{j}
. Meanwhile,
\textup{poly}(d)\geq 0
is independent of
\bar{\varepsilon}
and the elements
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})\}_{k=1}^{j}
.

Proof.

In
Theorem

2
, the clock on
C
ran over one oscillation of the oscillator over a total time
T_{0}=2\pi/\omega_{0}
. In the current setup, we are running the computer over multiple
l
runs of the oscillator. The proof consists in relating terms from the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
run of the oscillator to the
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
run, and then using the results from
Appendix

C
to bound them.

We start by recalling that
t_{l,r}=t_{l+rN_{g}}=t_{l}+rT_{0}
. Thus, recalling the definition of
\ket{\Psi(td/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}
in
eq.

C.34
, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{d}(t_{l,r}d/T_{0})

\displaystyle=\{k-rd\,|\,k\in{\mathbbm{Z}}\text{ and }-d/2\leq t_{l}d/T_{0}-k<d/2\},

(E.32)

\displaystyle\psi_{\textup{nor}}(t_{l,r}d/T_{0})

\displaystyle=A_{\textup{nor}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{\sigma^{2}}\left([k-rd]-t_{l}d/T_{0}\right)^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}2\pi([k-rd]-t_{l}d/T_{0})/d},

(E.33)

from which it follows

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi(t_{l,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}=\ket{\Psi(t_{l}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}

(E.34)

for all
j=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
, and
l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
.

A state of the logical computational space
\ket{t_{p,r}}_{\textup{L}}
is generated by applying
p
gates to the state of the logical computational space after
rN_{g}
gates to it, i.e.
\ket{t_{p,r}}_{\textup{L}}=\Pi_{l=1}^{p}U(\mathbb{m}_{r,l})\ket{t_{0,r}}_{\textup{L}}
, for
r=1,2,\ldots,L
.
For
r=0
, recall that we are applying unitaries to the switches to turn them on sequentially, rather than applying gates to the computation: at times
t_{p,0}
, we apply
U({\bf 0})\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{p}}=\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{p}}
. Let us start by evaluating the terms in
eqs.

E.28
and
E.29
for
r=0
and
r>0
separately:

\displaystyle\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{r,k})}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.35)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\big\|\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{W}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}&\mbox{ if }r=0\\
\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}&\mbox{ if }r=1,2,\ldots,L\end{cases}

(E.36)

where

\displaystyle H_{\textup{W}\textup{C}}^{(k)}:=

\displaystyle H_{\textup{C}}+I_{\textup{W}}^{(k)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(k)},

(E.37)

\displaystyle H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}:=

\displaystyle H_{\textup{C}}+I_{\textup{L}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(k)},\quad\mathbb{m}_{k}\in\mathcal{G}

(E.38)

Similarly,

\displaystyle\big\|\bar{H}_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\vec{\mathbb{m}})}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.39)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\big\|\bar{H}_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\vec{\mathbb{m}})}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{W}\textup{C}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}&\mbox{ if }\mathbb{m}_{k}={\bf 0}\\
\big\|\bar{H}_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\vec{\mathbb{m}})}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}&\mbox{ if }\mathbb{m}_{k}\in\mathcal{G},\end{cases}

(E.40)

where we can write
\bar{H}_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\vec{\mathbb{m}})}
in the form
\bar{H}_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\vec{\mathbb{m}})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=1\\
q\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\left(I_{\mathrm{\gamma_{1}}(\mathbb{m}_{q})}^{(q,\mathbb{m}_{q})}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(q)}\right)
, where
\mathrm{\gamma_{1}}(\mathbb{m}_{q})=\textup{L}
if
\mathbb{m}_{q}\in\mathcal{G}
, and
\mathrm{\gamma_{1}}(\mathbb{m}_{q})=\textup{W}
if
\mathbb{m}_{q}={\bf 0}.
(This follows from its definition;
eq.

E.14
.)
In
Appendix

C
, we dealt with states of this form in
eqs.

E.36
and
E.40
(i.e. in
Lemmas

C.2
and
C.3
respectively).

Using the above identities we can now apply the proof of
Theorem

2
from lines
C.106
and
C.107
onwards. Importantly, the only difference is that we have
H_{\textup{W}\textup{C}}^{(k)}
and
\bar{H}_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\vec{\mathbb{m}})}
(or
H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}
and
\bar{H}_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\vec{\mathbb{m}})}
), rather than
H_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}
and
\bar{H}_{\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k)}
. These only differ by the fact that the latter have interaction terms
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}
while the former has terms
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l,\mathbb{m}_{l})}
or
I_{\textup{W}}^{(l)}
. Recall that
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}
is a generic term responsible of implementing any unitary on
L
, while
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l,\mathbb{m}_{l})}
is responsible for implementing the gate
\mathbb{m}_{l}\in\mathcal{G}
on
L
, and
I_{\textup{W}}^{(l)}
is responsible for implementing the gate
{\bf 0}
on
W
. Since the proof of
Theorem

2
was for all generic terms
I_{\textup{L}}^{(l)}
, it also applies in the case at hand (once one identifies the subsystems
W
and
L
in the current proof with
L
in the original proof) and the maximization over gates,
\max_{\,\,\,\{\mathbb{m}_{l}\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{{\bf 0}\}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}}
, vanishes.

Thus from
eq.

C.130
, we conclude that there exists an initial clock state
\ket{\Psi{(0)}}_{\textup{C}}
(the same clock state used in case 2) of
Theorem

2
) such that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{j}\bigg(\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}H_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{r,k})}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.41)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad+t_{1}\max_{x\in[0,t_{1}]}\max_{\,\,\,\{\mathbb{m}_{l}\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{{\bf 0}\}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}}\big\|\bar{H}_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\vec{\mathbb{m}})}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}xH_{\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}}^{(k,\mathbb{m}_{k})}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{k-1,r}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}\bigg)

(E.42)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{k=1}^{j}\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})\right)f(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}(d)\,d^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}},

(E.43)

where the parameters are defined as per
eq.

C.130
; the only difference between line
E.43
and the r.h.s. of
eq.

C.130
is the replacement of
\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{k})
with
\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})
. Thus we have that
eq.

E.43
holds for all
\eta\in(0,1]
,
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
,
d\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
. The function
f\geq 0
is independent of
d
and the elements
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})\}_{k=1}^{j}
. Meanwhile,
\textup{poly}(d)\geq 0
is independent of,
\eta
,
\bar{\varepsilon}
, and the elements
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})\}_{k=1}^{j}
.

To finalise the proof, we simply need to specialise to case 2) by choosing
\eta
such that
\varepsilon_{g}=\bar{\varepsilon}
, which provides that parametrization of
\eta
as a function of
\eta
according to
eq.

C.154
. We then define
h(\bar{\varepsilon}):=f(\bar{\varepsilon},\bar{\varepsilon})
and recall the definition of
N_{g}
in terms of
\varepsilon_{g}
in
eq.

C.125
.
∎

E.3
Description of the control on
\textup{C}_{2}

In order to bound the terms associated with the dynamics of Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}
, in
eq.

E.7
(namely lines
E.9
and
E.11
), we have to specialise further the Hamiltonian. We do this here. In particular, the free Hamiltonian of the clock
H_{\textup{C}_{2}}
will be a copy the free Hamiltonian of the clock controlling the gates,
H_{\textup{C}}
, i.e.
H_{\textup{C}_{2}}=\sum_{n=0}^{d-1}\omega_{0}n\ket{E_{n}}\!\!\bra{E_{n}}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
, where
\omega_{0}=2\pi/T_{0}
and
\{\ket{E_{n}}\}_{n}
is an orthonormal basis for the Hilbert space associated with
\textup{C}_{2}
. Also similar to before, the clock interaction terms,
\{I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
, will be chosen to be diagonal in the discrete Fourier Transform basis associated with
\{\ket{E_{n}}_{\textup{C}}\}_{n}
, namely

\displaystyle I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)}:=\frac{d}{T_{0}}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(k_{0})}I_{{\textup{C}_{2}},d}^{(l)}(k)\ket{\theta_{k}}\!\!\bra{\theta_{k}}_{\textup{C}_{2}},\qquad I_{{\textup{C}_{2}},d}^{(l)}(x):=\frac{2\pi}{d}\bar{V}_{0}\left(\frac{2\pi}{d}x\right)\Bigg|_{{x_{0}}=x_{0}^{\prime(l)}},

(E.44)

where
I_{{\textup{C}_{2}},d}(k)
is chosen such that
I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)}
is independent of
k_{0}\in{\mathbbm{R}}
(as was the case with the interaction terms for the clock on
C
.) and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{V}_{0}(x)&=n_{2}A_{0}\sum_{p=-\infty}^{+\infty}V_{B}(n_{2}(x-x_{0}+2\pi p)),\\
V_{B}(\cdot)&=\textup{sinc}^{2N_{2}}(\cdot)=(\sin(\pi\,\cdot)/(\pi\,\cdot))^{2N_{2}},\qquad N_{2}\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}.\end{split},

(E.45)

which is the same as before (c.f.
eq.

C.27
), except for exchanging the free parameters
n>0
for
n_{2}>0
and
N
for
N_{2}
. This is necessary since we will parametrise
n_{2}
with
d
differently for the interactions terms of
C
(
A_{0}
above is the same as in
eq.

C.27
) but with
N
replaced with
N_{2}
). Likewise, the parameter
x_{0}
(appearing in the definition of
\bar{V}_{0}(\cdot)
), will not take on the same value as for the clock on
C
, moreover, it will take on a value
x_{0}^{\prime(l)}
which will be chosen differently in this case. This is necessary, in order to avoid a “read-write issue”: we cannot write to a memory cell which is simultaneously read without incurring a large error. To avoid such issues we want the unitary on the memory corresponding to the interaction
I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)}
to be performed out of phase in time with the average time at which the
I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)}
logical gate is performed. Since
x_{0}^{(l)}
encodes this time for the
l^{\text{th}}
logical gate as angle in cycle
R
, we choose

\displaystyle x_{0}^{\prime(l)}=x_{0}^{(l)}+\pi=\frac{2\pi}{N_{g}}\left(l-\frac{1}{2}\right)+\pi.

(E.46)

The justification of this choice (beyond physical intuition) will become apparent in the subsequent proofs. Finally, the parameters
n
and
N
in the definition of
\bar{V}(\cdot)
will be denoted by
n_{2}
and
N_{2}
respectively, in order to distinguish them from those coming from the interaction terms
\{I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
.

Now that we have justified the actual timing, we should choose the interaction terms
\{I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
in
H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}
appropriately. First recall the initial configuration of the memory:
The total memory consists in
N_{g}(L+1)
cells, arranged in a grid. Each cell stores in an orthogonal state a gate to be implemented. The initial memory state is thus of the form
\ket{\vec{\mathbb{m}}}_{\textup{M}}:=\ket{\vec{\mathbb{m}}_{0}}_{\textup{M}_{0}}\ket{\vec{\mathbb{m}}_{1}}_{\textup{M}_{1}}\ldots\ket{\vec{\mathbb{m}}_{L}}_{\textup{M}_{L}}
with
\ket{\vec{\mathbb{m}}_{j}}_{\textup{M}_{j}}:=\ket{\mathbb{m}_{j,1}}_{\textup{M}_{j,1}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{j,2}}_{\textup{M}_{j,2}}\ldots\ket{\mathbb{m}_{j,N_{g}}}_{\textup{M}_{j,N_{g}}}
. The clock on
C
can only read sequentially the memory cells in the first row, namely
\ket{\vec{\mathbb{m}}_{0}}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
. Therefore the clock on
\textup{C}_{2}
is responsible for updating the memory cells on
\ket{\vec{\mathbb{m}}_{0}}_{\textup{M}_{0}}
so that the entire gate sequence can be implemented. A consistent way to achieve is to have
\{I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
in
H_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}
satisfy the following conditions (for all
l=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{0,l}}_{\textup{M}_{0,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{2,l}}_{\textup{M}_{2,l}}\ldots\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L-1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L,l}}\\
&\;\,\quad=\ket{\mathbb{m}_{1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{0,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{2,l}}_{\textup{M}_{1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{3,l}}_{\textup{M}_{2,l}}\ldots\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{0,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L,l}},\\
&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{0,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{2,l}}_{\textup{M}_{1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{3,l}}_{\textup{M}_{2,l}}\ldots\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L-1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-2,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{0,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L,l}}\\
&\;\,\quad=\ket{\mathbb{m}_{2,l}}_{\textup{M}_{0,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{3,l}}_{\textup{M}_{1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{4,l}}_{\textup{M}_{2,l}}\ldots\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-2,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{0,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L,l}},\\
&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{2,l}}_{\textup{M}_{0,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{3,l}}_{\textup{M}_{1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{4,l}}_{\textup{M}_{2,l}}\ldots\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L-1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-3,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-2,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{0,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L,l}}\\
&\;\,\quad=\ket{\mathbb{m}_{3,l}}_{\textup{M}_{0,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{4,l}}_{\textup{M}_{1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{5,l}}_{\textup{M}_{2,l}}\ldots\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-3,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{0,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-2,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{2,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L,l}},\\
&\qquad\vdots\\
&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L-1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{0,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L,l}}_{\textup{M}_{1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{0,l}}_{\textup{M}_{2,l}}\ldots\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L-1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L-2,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L,l}}\\
&\;\,\quad=\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L,l}}_{\textup{M}_{0,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{0,l}}_{\textup{M}_{1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{2,l}}\ldots\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L-2,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L-1,l}}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{L-1,l}}_{\textup{M}_{L,l}},\end{split}

(E.47)

for all
\mathbb{m}_{j,l}\in\mathcal{G}
,
j\in 1,\ldots,L
, such that
\mathbb{m}_{0,l}={\bf 0}
for all
l=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
. Thus since the action of
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}}
maps from one orthonormal basis set to another, it is guaranteed that a unitary representation of
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}}
exists. What is more, from its construction it readily follows that it is independent of the initial memory state
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}}
which encodes the to-be-implemented gate sequence.

Note that the memory states
{\bf 0}
play an additional role here beyond their primary role of controlling the turning on of the switches. Namely, they ensure that the states in
eq.

E.47
before and after the application of
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}}
are indeed orthogonal. To see this, imagine the fictitious scenario in which
\mathbb{m}_{0,l}\neq{\bf 0}
,
\mathbb{m}_{0,l}\in\mathcal{G}
,
{\bf 0}\notin\mathcal{G}
and all initial memory cells in
M
have the same value
m\in\mathcal{G}
. This is highly degenerate, and the states in
eq.

E.47
before and after the application of
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}}
are indistinguishable and hence cannot be orthogonal. Since however,
{\bf 0}\notin\mathcal{G}
by definition, even if the initial memory state is highly degenerate in the sense which has just been described, the states before and after the application of
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}}
are always mutually orthogonal.

The definition of
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}}
[
eq.

E.47
] is also consistent with our prior assumption on
I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}
, namely that it acts non-trivially only on memory block
\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}
. What is more, if
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}}
is applied once in the time intervals
(t_{l,0},t_{l,1})
,…,
(t_{l,L-1},t_{l,L})
, then condition
eq.

IV.8
is met. We will see that our idealised solution—the one we wish to approximate—will be consistent with this.

Let the set of pairs
\{(\Omega_{i_{l}},\,\ket{\Omega_{i_{l}}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}})\}_{i_{l}}
be the eigenvalues and vectors of
I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}
respectively:

\displaystyle I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}\ket{\Omega_{i_{l}}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}=\Omega_{i_{l}}\ket{\Omega_{i_{l}}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}},\qquad\Omega_{i_{l}}\in(0,2\pi],

(E.48)

l=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
.

We can now specify the idealised state on
\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}
at times
t_{k,r}
.
13
13

13

In this
manuscript
, we use notation
\prod_{i=1}^{n}[a_{i}]:=a_{1}a_{2}...a_{n}
and where the multiplication operation takes preference over summation, i.e.

For

;

,

\displaystyle\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{M}\backslash\{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k},\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k+1}\}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k+1}},

] and where recall the definition of the short-hand notation:
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},p}}:=

\ket{\mathbb{m}_{0,p}}_{\textup{M}_{0,p}}

\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{\textup{Cntl},p}}
.
For the boundary values i.e.

;

since their is no time before

. Namely, we define

\displaystyle\ket{t_{0,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\ket{t_{0,r}}_{\textup{M}\backslash\{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},1}\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},N_{g}}\}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{0,r}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},1}}\ket{t_{0,r-1}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},N_{g}}},

(E.52)

\displaystyle\ket{t_{0,r}}_{\textup{M}\backslash\{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},1}\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},N_{g}}\}\textup{C}_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\not\in\{1,N_{g}\}\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\left[\sum_{i_{l}}\,\,{\vphantom{\big[{k}\big]}}{}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}\!\!\braket{\Omega_{i_{l}}|0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}\ket{\Omega_{i_{l}}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}\right]\ket{\Psi_{\bar{i}_{1},\bar{i}_{N_{g}}}(t_{k,r})}_{\textup{C}_{2}},

(E.53)

\displaystyle\ket{t_{0,r}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},1}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}rI^{(1)}_{\textup{M}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},1}},\qquad\ket{t_{0,r-1}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},N_{g}}}:=\begin{cases}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},N_{g}}}&\mbox{ for }r=0\\
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(r-1)I^{(1)}_{\textup{M}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},1}}&\mbox{ for }r=1,2,\ldots,L.\end{cases}

(E.54)

The state on
\textup{C}_{2}
is defined (in slightly more generality) as

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{\bar{i}_{n_{1}},\bar{i}_{n_{2}},\ldots,\bar{i}_{n_{s}}}(t)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}:=\sum_{q\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(td/T_{0})}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\not\in\{n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{s}\}\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\,\Omega_{i_{l}}^{(l)}\left(\int_{q-\Theta_{l}(t)d/T_{0}}^{q}\textup{d}y\,I_{\textup{C}_{2},d}^{(l)}(y)\right)}\right]\psi_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}(td/T_{0},q)\ket{\theta_{q}}_{\textup{C}_{2}}

(E.55)

for
n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{s}\in\{1,2,\ldots,N_{g}\}
, and where

\displaystyle\Theta_{l}(t):=\begin{cases}0\mbox{ if }t\leq t_{l}\\
t-t_{l}\mbox{ if }t>t_{l}.\end{cases}

(E.56)

It readily satisfies the initial condition at
t_{0,0}=0
, namely
\ket{t_{0,0}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}=(\Pi_{l,k}\ket{\mathbb{m}_{l,k}}_{\textup{M}_{l,k}})\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
. What is more, one can easily verify that this idealised memory state at different times
t_{k,r}
satisfies the necessary condition in
eq.

IV.8
.
\psi_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}(\cdot,\cdot)
is defined analogously to
\psi_{\textup{nor}}(\cdot,\cdot)
for the state on
C
(see
eq.

C.35
). We denote these normalised amplitudes with a superscript simply not to confuse them with the state on
C
which has a different standard deviation:

\displaystyle\psi_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}\big(k_{0};k\big):=A_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{\sigma_{2}^{2}}(k-k_{0})^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}2\pi n_{0,2}(k-k_{0})/d}.

(E.57)

Here the terms are defined the same as in case 1) of section
Appendix

C
. In particular, this means that they depend on the dimension
14
14

14

We have chosen the Hilbert space dimensions of both clocks to be the same, namely
d
.

d
as follows

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}=\sqrt{d},\qquad n_{0,2}=\tilde{n}_{0,2}\,(d-1),

(E.58)

where
\tilde{n}_{0,2}\in(0,1)
is

-independent by definition. Analogous to the definition of

in

, we define

for the clock on

to be

\displaystyle\alpha_{0,2}=1-\left|1-n_{0,2}\left(\frac{2}{d-1}\right)\right|\in(0,1].

(E.59)

Analogously, to
|A_{\textup{nor}}|
,
|A_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}|
satisfies the upper bound

\displaystyle|A_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}|^{2}=\left(\frac{2}{\sigma_{2}^{2}}\right)+\epsilon(d),\text{ as }d\to\infty,

(E.60)

where
\epsilon(d)\to 0
as

(see
