]
for details).

Note that if the
l^{\text{th}}
factor
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\,\Omega_{i_{l}}^{(l)}\int_{q-\Theta_{l}(t)d/T_{0}}^{q}\textup{d}y\,I_{\textup{C}_{2},d}^{(l)}(y)}
in
\ket{\Psi_{\bar{i}_{n_{1}},\bar{i}_{n_{2}},\ldots,\bar{i}_{n_{s}}}(t)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
is equal to
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\,\Omega_{i_{l}}^{(l)}r}
, at time
t_{k,r}
, then state
\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}
becomes a bi-partite product state between the state on
\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}
and the rest of
\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}
. What is more the state on
\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}
is equal to
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}rI_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}
, which in turn is a product state over memory cells, by virtue of eqs. (
E.47
). This insight will be required in the following two lemmas in the next sections.

E.4
Generalization of Theorem 9.1 in
[
70
]

In the proof of
Theorem

2
we used the main theorem from
[
70
]
(Theorem 9.1) several times. In order to continue in the proof of
Theorem

3
, we now need to prove a generalization of Theorem 9.1. In order to keep notation close to that used in
[
70
]
and to avoid conflicts of notation used in other sections of this
manuscript
, the definitions and notation used in this section do not apply to other sections of this
manuscript
.

We start with a definition of a new clock state:

\displaystyle\ket{\bar{\Psi}_{\textup{nor}}(k_{0},\vec{\Delta})}:=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(k_{0})}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\,\sum_{j=1}^{D}\int_{k-\Delta_{j}}^{k}\mathrm{d}x\,V_{d}^{(j)}(x)}\psi_{\textup{nor}}(k_{0},k)\ket{\theta_{k}},

(E.61)

with
\vec{\Delta}:=(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\ldots,\Delta_{D})

\in{\mathbbm{R}}^{D}
,
D\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
,
k_{0}\in{\mathbbm{R}}
and
\psi_{\textup{nor}}(\cdot,\cdot)
defined in
eq.

C.35
. The functions
\{V_{d}^{(j)}(\cdot)\}_{j}
are all defined in the same way that
V_{d}(\cdot)
is defined in
[
70
]
, namely

\displaystyle{V}_{d}^{(j)}(\cdot):=\frac{2\pi}{d}V_{0}^{(j)}\left(\frac{2\pi}{d}(\cdot)\right),\qquad j=1,2,\ldots,D

(E.62)

where
V_{0}^{(j)}:{\mathbbm{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbbm{R}}\cup{\mathbbm{H}}^{-}
(where
{\mathbbm{H}}^{-}:=\left\{a_{0}+\mathrm{i}b_{0}:a_{0}\in{\mathbbm{R}},b_{0}<0\right\}
denotes the lower-half complex plane) is an infinitely differentiable function of period
2\pi
.

For the following theorem, let us define the following terms. We start with the interaction potentials,

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{d}^{(j)}:=\frac{d}{T_{0}}\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(k_{0})}V_{d}^{(j)}(k)\ket{\theta_{k}}\!\!\bra{\theta_{k}},

(E.63)

where
k_{0}\in{\mathbbm{R}}
and is
k_{0}
-independent due to the periodic nature of
V_{0}
and the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{d}
below
C.35
.
The definition of
b
from
[
70
, eq. 83]
is updated as follows.
15
15

15

Note that the definition of
b
from
[
70
]
has a maximization over the function itself (in addition to over its derivatives). In the proof, since the bound for
b
is resultant from bound on a phase, it can readily be seen that the maximization of the function is at most
2\pi
due to the invariance of a phase under modulo
2\pi
arithmetic.

b
is any real number satisfying:

\displaystyle b\geq\max\left\{2\pi\,,\,\,\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\left(\max_{x\in[0,2\pi]}\left|\sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{d^{k}}{dx^{k}}V_{0}^{(j)}(x)\right|+\max_{y\in[0,2\pi]}\left|\sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{d^{k}}{dy^{k}}V_{0}^{(j)}\left(y-\frac{2\pi\Delta_{j}}{d}\right)\right|\,\right)^{\!\!1/(k+1)}\right\}.

(E.64)

Observe that, due to the
2\pi
-periodicity of the functions
\{V_{0}^{(j)}\}_{j=1}^{D}
, the r.h.s. is invariant under the mapping
\{\Delta_{j}\}_{j=1}^{D}

\to

\{\Delta_{j}+a\}_{j=1}^{D}
, for all
a\in{\mathbbm{R}}
.
We further define the rate parameter as

\bar{v}=\frac{\pi\alpha_{0}\kappa}{\ln\left(\pi\alpha_{0}\sigma^{2}\right)}b,

where
\kappa=0.792
and

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2}{d-1}\right)\min\left\{n_{0},(d-1)-n_{0}\right\}=1-\left|1-n_{0}\left(\frac{2}{d-1}\right)\right|\in(0,1],

(E.65)

where recall
n_{0}\in{\mathbbm{R}}
is a parameter which appears in the definition of
\psi_{\textup{nor}}(\cdot,\cdot)
(
eq.

C.35
). (These definitions are analogous to eqs. 85 and 27 in
[
70
]
.)

In the following Theorem, the free clock Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{C}}
, is defined analogously to in the rest of this manuscript (see
eq.

C.24
). In this
manuscript
, we will only use the case
\{V_{0}^{(j)}:{\mathbbm{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbbm{R}}\}_{j=1}^{D}
but we write the general case for generality and to be in-keeping with the original theorem.

Theorem E.1

(Moving the clock through finite time with a generalised potential)
.

Let
k_{0}\in{\mathbbm{R}},\vec{\Delta}\in{\mathbbm{R}}^{D}
,
D\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
, and
t\in{\mathbbm{R}}
if
\{V_{0}^{(j)}:{\mathbbm{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbbm{R}}\}_{j=1}^{D}
while
t\geq 0
otherwise. Then the effect of the generator
{H}_{\textup{C}}+\sum_{j=1}^{D}\hat{V}_{d}^{(j)}
for time
t
on
\left|\bar{\Psi}_{\textup{nor}}\left(k_{0},\vec{\Delta}\right)\right\rangle
is approximated by

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\left({H}_{\textup{C}}+\sum_{j=1}^{D}\hat{V}_{d}^{(j)}\right)}\left|\bar{\Psi}_{\textup{nor}}\left(k_{0},\vec{\Delta}\right)\right\rangle=\left|\bar{\Psi}_{\textup{nor}}\left(k_{0}+\frac{d}{T_{0}}t,\vec{\Delta}+\frac{d}{T_{0}}t\right)\right\rangle+|\epsilon\rangle,\quad\||\epsilon\rangle\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon_{v}(t,d)

where
\vec{\Delta}+\frac{d}{T_{0}}t:=(\Delta_{1}+\frac{d}{T_{0}}t,\Delta_{2}+\frac{d}{T_{0}}t,\ldots,\Delta_{D}+\frac{d}{T_{0}}t)
, and in the limits
d\rightarrow\infty,(0,d)\ni\sigma\rightarrow\infty
, we have that

\varepsilon_{v}(t,d)=\begin{cases}|t|\frac{d}{T_{0}}\left(\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{d^{3/2}}{\bar{v}+1}\right)^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}(d)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\pi}{4}\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{(1+\bar{v})^{2}}d\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{2}d}\right)&\text{ if }\sigma=\sqrt{d}\\
|t|\frac{d}{T_{0}}\left(\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\sigma^{3}}{\bar{v}\sigma^{2}/d+1}\right)^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{d^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\pi}{4}\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\left(\frac{d}{\sigma^{2}}+\bar{v}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma}\right)^{2}\right)&\\
\quad+\mathcal{O}\left(|t|\frac{d^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{4}\frac{d^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\sigma^{2}}\right)&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

It can readily be seen that
Theorem

E.1
reduces to Theorem 9.1 in
[
70
]
, in a number of special cases. For example, when
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=\ldots=\Delta_{D}
, and we identify
\hat{V}_{d}
in
[
70
]
with
\sum_{j=1}^{D}\hat{V}_{d}^{(j)}
for any
D\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
.

Proof.

The proof follows analogously to the proof of Theorem 9.1 in
[
70
]
, (which used Lemmas IX.0.1., IX.0.2., IX.0.3., IX.0.4., IX.0.5., as input lemmas to the proof of Theorem 9.1.).
One needs to exchange definition 80 in
[
70
]
, namely

\displaystyle\Theta(\Delta;x)=\int_{x-\Delta}^{x}dyV_{d}(y),

(E.66)

with the new definition

\displaystyle\Theta(\Delta;x):=\sum_{j=1}^{D}\Theta_{j}(\Delta_{j};x),\qquad\Theta_{j}(\Delta_{j};x):=\int_{x-\Delta_{j}}^{x}dyV_{d}^{(j)}(y).

(E.67)

After this change, it is easy to go through the above-mentioned Lemmas line-by-line and the proof of the theorem itself, to verify
Theorem

E.1
.
∎

E.5
Lemmas bounding error contributions from control on
\textup{C}_{2}

In this section we will state and prove a lemma which will bound lines
E.9
and
E.11
in
eq.

E.7
. However, before doing so, we will need to introduce the following definition and lemma.

\displaystyle\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{[k,r]}:=H_{\textup{C}_{2}}+\sum_{q\in{\bf W}(k,r)}I_{\textup{M}}^{(q)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(q)},

(E.68)

with

\displaystyle{\bf W}(k,r):=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{ if }r=0\text{ and }k=1.\\
\{1,2,3,\ldots,k-1\}&\mbox{ if }r=0\text{ and }k=2,3,4,\ldots,N_{g}.\\
\{2,3,4,\ldots,N_{g}\}&\mbox{ if }r=1,2,\ldots,L,\text{ and }k=1.\\
\{1,2,3,\ldots,k-1,k+1,\ldots,N_{g}\}&\mbox{ if }r=1,2,\ldots,L,\text{ and }k=2,3,4,\ldots,N_{g}.\\
\end{cases}

(E.69)

where
\emptyset
is the empty set.

In the following lemma, is a technical lemma which will be used in the proofs of
Lemmas

E.5
and
E.4
.

Lemma E.3

(Bounding the dynamics on
\textup{C}_{2}
)
.

Consider the following definition.

\displaystyle\ket{\epsilon(t)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}:=\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t\,\!\left(\!H_{\textup{C}_{2}}+\sum_{l\in{\bf W}(k,r)}\Omega_{i_{l}}^{(l)}I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)}\right)}\ket{\Psi_{\bar{i}_{k}}(t_{k-1,r})}_{\textup{C}_{2}}-\ket{\Psi_{\bar{i}_{k}}(t_{k,r})}_{\textup{C}_{2}},

(E.70)

where
k=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
;
r=0,1,2,\ldots,L
;
t\in{\mathbbm{R}}
and
\{\Omega_{i_{l}}^{(l)}\in(0,2\pi]\}_{i_{l}}
. Assuming
n_{2}
to be monotonically increasing with
d
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\|\ket{\epsilon(t)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\|_{2}=:\varepsilon_{v}^{(2)}(t,d)\leq|t|\frac{d}{T_{0}}\left(\mathcal{O}\left(d^{1/2+\varepsilon_{g}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(d)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}\kappa^{2}C_{0}^{2}(N_{2})}\frac{d}{N_{g}n_{2}^{2}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{2}d}\right),

(E.71)

as
d\to\infty
.

Proof.

Given the definitions, we can readily apply
Theorem

E.1
with
\vec{\Delta}=(t_{k-2}d/T_{0},t_{k-3}d/T_{0},t_{k-4}d/T_{0},\ldots,t_{1}d/T_{0},0)
for
r=0
and
\vec{\Delta}=(rd+t_{k-2}d/T_{0},rd+t_{k-3}d/T_{0},rd+t_{k-4}d/T_{0},\ldots,rd,rd+t_{-2}d/T_{0},rd+t_{-3}d/T_{0},\ldots,rd+t_{k-1-N_{g}}d/T_{0})
for
r=1,2,\ldots,L
. (This amounts to
\vec{\Delta}
being of dimension
k-1=|{\bf W}(k,0)|
and
N_{g}-1=|{\bf W}(k,r)|
respectively). In both cases, the Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{C}}+\sum_{j=1}^{D}\hat{V}_{d}^{(j)}
is chosen to be
H_{\textup{C}_{2}}+\sum_{q\in{\bf W}(k,r)}\Omega_{i_{q}}^{(q)}I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(q)}
.

Thus, out task is to bound
eq.

E.64
for this setting. Thus the summation
\sum_{j=1}^{D}V_{0}^{(j)}(x)
in
eq.

E.64
amounts to

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{D}V_{0}^{(j)}(x)=\sum_{q\in{\bf W}(k,r)}\Omega_{i_{q}}^{(q)}\bar{V}_{0}(x)\Big|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{\prime(q)}}.

(E.72)

where recall
\bar{V}_{0}(\cdot)
is given by
eq.

E.45
. Therefore for
k=1,2,3,\ldots
we have

\displaystyle\max_{x\in[0,2\pi]}\left|\sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{d^{k}}{dx^{k}}\bar{V}_{0}\left(x\right)\right|\leq\pi N_{g}\max_{x\in[0,2\pi]}\left|\frac{d^{k}}{dx^{k}}V_{0}\left(x\right)\right|\leq\pi N_{g}n_{2}^{k+1}C_{0}(N_{2})^{k+1}

(E.73)

where we have used
eq.

E.48
in the penultimate inequality and Lemma 28 from
[
71
, page 29]
in the last inequality. Recall
C_{0}^{(2)}(N_{2})
is solely a function on
N_{2}
, i.e. independent of
k
,
d
and
n_{2}
(it is denoted
C_{0}(N)
in Lemma 28 from
[
71
, page 29]
. Here we use the superscript to distinguish it from
C_{0}(N)
used for the control on
C
in the proof of
Theorem

2
).

For the second term in
eq.

E.64
we have for
p=1,2,3,\ldots

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{d^{p}}{dy^{p}}V_{0}^{(j)}\left(y-\frac{2\pi\Delta_{j}}{d}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{q\in{\bf W}(k,r)}\Omega_{i_{q}}^{(q)}\frac{d^{p}}{dy^{p}}\bar{V}_{0}\left(y-\frac{2\pi t_{k-1-q,r}}{T_{0}}\right)\!\bigg|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{\prime(q)}}\right|

(E.74)

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{q\in{\bf W}(k,r)}\Omega_{i_{q}}^{(q)}\frac{d^{p}}{dy^{p}}\bar{V}_{0}\left(y-\frac{2\pi}{N_{g}}(k-1-q)-x_{0}^{\prime(q)}\right)\!\bigg|_{x_{0}=0}\right|

(E.75)

\displaystyle=\left|\left(\sum_{q\in{\bf W}(k,r)}\Omega_{i_{q}}^{(q)}\right)\frac{d^{p}}{dy^{p}}\bar{V}_{0}\left(y-\frac{2\pi}{N_{g}}(k-3/2)-\pi\right)\!\bigg|_{x_{0}=0}\right|

(E.76)

\displaystyle\leq\pi N_{g}\left|\frac{d^{p}}{dy^{p}}\bar{V}_{0}\left(y-\frac{2\pi}{N_{g}}(k-3/2)-\pi\right)\!\bigg|_{x_{0}=0}\right|

(E.77)

In line
E.75
, we have used the
2\pi
periodicity of
\bar{V}_{0}
. In line
E.76
we have used definition
E.46
. In line
E.77
we have used definition
E.48
.Therefore, using Lemma 28 from
[
71
, page 29]
we find

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{d^{p}}{dy^{p}}V_{0}^{(j)}\left(y-\frac{2\pi\Delta_{j}}{d}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\pi N_{g}n_{2}^{p+1}C_{0}(N_{2})^{p+1}.

(E.78)

Therefore, from definition
E.64
it follows that we can choose
b
such that

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\geq\max\left\{2\pi\,,\,\,\sup_{p\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\left((2\pi N_{g})^{1/(p+1)}n_{2}C_{0}(N_{2})\right)\right\}.

(E.79)

\displaystyle=\max\left\{2\pi\,,\,\,(2\pi N_{g})^{1/2}n_{2}C_{0}(N_{2})\right\}.

(E.80)

Therefore, applying
Theorem

E.1
, we conclude
eq.

E.71
.
∎

We will now state and prove a lemma which will bound line
E.9
in
eq.

E.7

We will also require the definition of how the switch states change overtime. This was discussed in the main text but we reproduce it here in a more compact form for ease of readability. For
l=0,1,2,\ldots,N_{g}
;
r=0,1,2,\ldots,L
:

\displaystyle\ket{t_{l,r}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}=\begin{cases}\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}&\mbox{ if }r=1,2,3,\ldots,L\\
\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}&\mbox{ if }r=0\text{ and }l\geq k\\
\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}&\mbox{ if }r=0\text{ and }l<k\end{cases}

(E.81)

Lemma E.4

(Bound for
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
bus-related term)
.

Consider the control states
\{\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\}_{k,r}
and Hamiltonian interaction terms described in
Section

E.3
. The following holds for all
N_{2}-2,N_{g}-2\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
,
n_{2}>0
,
k=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
and
r\in 0,1,2,\ldots,L
.

\displaystyle\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,k}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{W}}_{k}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,k}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{W}}_{k}}\big\|_{2}

(E.82)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\delta}_{r,0}\,A_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}4\pi^{2}\sqrt{2d\scalebox{1.0}{$\left[\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}\frac{5}{(2N_{2}-1)^{2}\,8^{2}}\left(\frac{\pi^{2}n_{2}}{4}\right)^{-4N_{2}}+4\pi^{2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi d/8^{2}}\scalebox{1.0}{$\left]\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}}+\varepsilon_{v}^{(2)}(t_{1},d),

(E.83)

where

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{r,0}=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }r=0\\
1&\mbox{ if }r=1,2,\ldots,L\end{cases}

(E.84)

and
\varepsilon_{v}^{(2)}(t_{1},d)
is upper bounded in
Lemma

E.3
.

Proof.

Due to boundary conditions which would require a change of notation, we only consider
k=2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
here. The case
k=1
follows analogously with slight changes in the notation [e.g.
eq.

E.87
requires some modification as is readily apparent].
Note that by definition,
\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}
acts trivially on all memory cells
\textup{M}_{0,k}
,
\textup{M}_{1,k}
,
\textup{M}_{2,k}
, …,
\textup{M}_{L,k}
. What is more, by definition
E.49
,
\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}}=\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}}
, where
{\bar{\textup{M}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}}
denotes all of
M
except
{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}}
.

\displaystyle\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,k}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{W}}_{k}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,k}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{W}}_{k}}\big\|_{2}

(E.85)

\displaystyle=\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{W}}_{k}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{W}}_{k}}\big\|_{2}.

(E.86)

What is more,
\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}
is diagonal in the on/off basis of the switch and only has support on the on-switch states
\big\{\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{l}}\big\}_{l=1}^{N_{g}}
. What is more, from the definition of
\ket{t_{l,m}}_{\textup{W}}
[
eq.

E.81
] it follows

\displaystyle\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}=\begin{cases}\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{1}}\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{2}}\ldots\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{k-1}}\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{k+1}}\ldots\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{N_{g}}}&\mbox{ if }r=0\\
\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{1}}\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{2}}\ldots\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{N_{g}}}&\mbox{ if }r=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}\end{cases}

(E.87)

\displaystyle\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{W}}=\begin{cases}\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{1}}\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{2}}\ldots\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{k}}\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{k+1}}\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{k+2}}\ldots\ket{\textup{off}}_{\textup{W}_{N_{g}}}&\mbox{ if }r=0\\
\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{1}}\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{2}}\ldots\ket{\textup{on}}_{\textup{W}_{N_{g}}}&\mbox{ if }r=1,2,\ldots,N_{g}\end{cases}

(E.88)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,k}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{W}}_{k}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,k}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{W}}_{k}}\big\|_{2}

(E.89)

\displaystyle=\big\|\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}\textup{C}_{2}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{[k,r]}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}\textup{C}_{2}}\big\|_{2},

(E.90)

where
\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{[k,r]}
is defined in
eq.

E.68
.
Let us now compute
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{[k,r]}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}\textup{C}_{2}}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{[k,r]}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}\textup{C}_{2}}

(E.91)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{[k,r]}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\backslash\{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k-1}\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}\}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k-1}}

(E.92)

\displaystyle=\!\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\not\in\{k,k-1\}\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\left[\sum_{i_{l}}\,\,{\vphantom{\big[{k}\big]}}{}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}\!\!\braket{\Omega_{i_{l}}|0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}\ket{\Omega_{i_{l}}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}\right]\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}\!\left(\!H_{\textup{C}_{2}}+\sum_{q\in{\bf W}(k,r)}\Omega_{i_{q}}^{(q)}I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(q)}\right)}\!\!\ket{\Psi_{\bar{i}_{k-1},\bar{i}_{k}}(t_{k-1,r})}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k-1}}

(E.93)

\displaystyle=\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\

Lemma E.5

(Bound for
2{{}^{\text{nd}}}
bus-related term)
.

Consider the control states
\{\ket{t_{k,r}}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\}_{k,r}
and Hamiltonian interaction terms described in
Section

E.3
. The following holds for all
N_{2}-2,N_{g}-2\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
,
d-4\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
,
j=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
and
r\in 0,1,2,\ldots,L
.

\displaystyle\max_{y\in[0,t_{1}]}\big\|\left(I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(k)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}y\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\big\|_{2}

(E.119)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\delta}_{r,0}\frac{4\pi^{2}}{T_{0}}n_{2}A_{0}\!\scalebox{1.0}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}A_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}\sqrt{d}\Bigg(3\left(\frac{\pi^{2}n_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{-2N_{2}}+2\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{8^{2}}d}\Bigg)+2\varepsilon_{v}^{(2)}(t_{1},d)\scalebox{1.0}{$\left)\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}

(E.120)

where

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{r,0}=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }r=0\\
1&\mbox{ if }r=1,2,\ldots,L\end{cases}

(E.121)

and
\varepsilon_{v}^{(2)}(t_{1},d)
is upper bounded in
Lemma

E.3
.

Proof.

Here we prove the result for

. While the case

follows analogously, it is best treated separately due to the periodic boundary conditions.

\displaystyle\max_{y\in[0,t_{1}]}\big\|\left(I_{\textup{M}\textup{W}}^{(k)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}y\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{W}}\big\|_{2}

(E.122)

\displaystyle=\bar{\delta}_{r,0}\max_{y\in[0,t_{1}]}\big\|\left(I_{\textup{M}}^{(k)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}y\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{[k,1]}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\big\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\bar{\delta}_{r,0}\max_{y\in[0,1]}\big\|I_{\textup{M}}^{(k)}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}}\big\|_{2}\,\,\big\|I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{y}\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{[k,1]}}\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},k}\textup{C}_{2}}\big\|_{2}

(E.124)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\delta}_{r,0}\big\|I_{\textup{M}}^{(k)}\big\|_{2}\max_{y\in[0,1]}\!\!\left(\!\big\|I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\not=k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}\left[\sum_{i_{l}}\,\,{\vphantom{\big[{k}\big]}}{}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}\!\!\braket{\Omega_{i_{l}}|0}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}\ket{\Omega_{i_{l}}}_{\textup{M}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\#},l}}\right]\ket{\Psi_{\bar{i}_{k}}(t_{k-1+y,r})}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big\|_{2}+\big\|I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(k)}\ket{\epsilon}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big\|_{2}\!\!\right)

(E.125)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\delta}_{r,0}\,2\pi\max_{y\in[0,1]}\!\scalebox{1.0}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}\frac{d}{T_{0}}\sqrt{\sum_{q\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(t_{k-1+y,r}d/T_{0})}\left(I_{\textup{C}_{2},d}^{(k)}(q)\right)^{2}\left|\psi_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}(t_{k-1+y,r}d/T_{0},q)\right|^{2}}

(E.126)

\displaystyle+\frac{d}{T_{0}}\sqrt{\sum_{q\in 0,1,\ldots,d-1}\left|I_{\textup{C}_{2},d}^{(k)}(q)\,\,{}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\braket{\theta_{q}|\epsilon}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\right|^{2}}\scalebox{1.0}{$\left)\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}

(E.127)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\delta}_{r,0}\,2\pi\max_{y\in[0,1]}\!\scalebox{1.0}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}\sqrt{\sum_{q\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(t_{k-1+y,r}d/T_{0})}\left(\bar{V}_{0}\Big(\frac{2\pi}{d}q\Big)\bigg|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{\prime(k)}}\right)^{2}\left|\psi_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}(t_{k-1+y,r}d/T_{0},q)\right|^{2}}

(E.129)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\delta}_{r,0}\,2\pi\max_{y\in[0,1]}\!\scalebox{1.0}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}A_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}\sqrt{d\max_{q\in[-d/2+[k-1+y]d/N_{g}+rd,\,d/2+[k-1+y]d/N_{g}+rd]}\left(\bar{V}_{0}\Big(\frac{2\pi}{d}q\Big)\bigg|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{\prime(k)}}\right)^{2}\left|\psi_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}(t_{k-1+y,r}d/T_{0},q)\right|^{2}}

(E.133)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\leq\bar{\delta}_{r,0}\,2\pi\max_{y\in[0,1]}\!\scalebox{1.0}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}n_{2}A_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}A_{0}\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}\sqrt{d}\scalebox{1.0}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}\!\!\left(\frac{2\pi^{2}n_{2}}{\sqrt{8}}\right)^{-2N_{2}}\left[\max_{q^{\prime}\in[-1/8,\,1/8]}\,\sum_{p=-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\sqrt{8}(q^{\prime}+[1/2+y-1]/N_{g}+1/2+p)\right)^{-2N_{2}}\right]\\

(E.134)

\displaystyle+\frac{4\pi}{T_{0}}\varepsilon_{v}^{(2)}(t_{k,r},d)n_{2}A_{0}\scalebox{1.0}{$\left)\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}

(E.135)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\delta}_{r,0}\,2\pi\max_{y\in[0,1]}\!\scalebox{1.0}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}n_{2}A_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}A_{0}\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}\sqrt{d}\scalebox{1.0}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}3\left(\frac{\pi^{2}n_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{-2N_{2}}+\left(5\left(\frac{3\pi^{2}n_{2}}{2}\right)^{-2N_{2}}+1\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\left(\frac{d}{\sigma_{2}}\right)^{2}{\left(1/8\right)}^{2}}\Bigg)

(E.136)

\displaystyle+\frac{4\pi}{T_{0}}n_{2}A_{0}\,\varepsilon_{v}^{(2)}(t_{k,r},d)\scalebox{1.0}{$\left)\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}

(E.137)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\delta}_{r,0}\frac{4\pi^{2}}{T_{0}}n_{2}A_{0}\!\scalebox{1.0}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}A_{\textup{nor}}^{(2)}\sqrt{d}\scalebox{1.0}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}3\left(\frac{\pi^{2}n_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{-2N_{2}}+2\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\left(\frac{d}{\sigma_{2}}\right)^{2}{\left(1/8\right)}^{2}}\Bigg)+2\,\varepsilon_{v}^{(2)}(t_{k,r},d)\scalebox{1.0}{$\left)\rule{0.0pt}{19.91684pt}\right.$}

(E.138)

Where in line

we have used the definitions of

and

together with

\displaystyle\bar{H}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}^{[k,1]}=H_{\textup{C}_{2}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq k\end{subarray}}^{N_{g}}I_{\textup{M}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{(l)}.

(E.139)

In line

,

; used
\ket{t_{k-1,r}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}

Finally we are in a stage to prove
Theorem

3
. Below we state a more explicit version of it, which is what we will prove. In particular, we state more explicitly the form which the functions
{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(\cdot)
take (One can readily check that they belong to said function class). For this, we introduce a new function
h
: In the following,
h(\bar{\varepsilon})>0
(for
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
) is independent of
E_{0}^{\prime}
and
L
, while
\textup{poly}\big(E_{0}^{\prime}\big)
is a polynomial in
E_{0}^{\prime}
and independent of
\bar{\varepsilon}
.
Both are independent from the elements in the set
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m})\}_{\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{{\bf 0}\}}
.

Theorem
3

(
[
More explicit than main-text version.
]
Optimal quantum frequential computers only require a classical internal bus)
.

For all gate sets
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{G}}
, initial memory states
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{M}}
and initial logical states
\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{L}})
, there exists
\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}
,
\ket{0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
,
\{\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{C}},\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\}_{j=1,2,\ldots,N_{g};\,l=0,1,\ldots,L}
,
N_{g}
,
H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{C}}
parametrised by the energy
E_{0}^{\prime}>0
and a dimensionless parameter
\bar{\varepsilon}
(where elements
\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{C}}
,
\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}
satisfy
eqs.

IV.1
and
IV.8
respectively), such that for all
j=1,2,3,\ldots,N_{g}
;
l=0,1,2,\ldots,L
and fixed
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
, the large-
E_{0}^{\prime}
scaling is as follows

\displaystyle T\Big(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j,l}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{0}_{\textup{W}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}},\,\,\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{C}}\Big)

(E.140)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})\right)h(\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}\big(E_{0}^{\prime}\big)\,\,\big(E_{0}^{\prime}\big)^{-1/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}},

(E.141)

where
\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
,
\textup{tr}_{\textup{M}}\big[\ket{t_{j,l}}\!\!\bra{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\big]\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{\textup{clas.}}
, and

\displaystyle f=\frac{1}{T_{0}}\left(T_{0}E_{0}^{\prime}\right)^{1-\bar{\varepsilon}}+\delta f^{\prime\prime},\qquad|\delta f^{\prime\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{T_{0}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\textup{poly}\big(E_{0}^{\prime}\big)\big(E_{0}^{\prime}\big)^{-1/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,\right)\text{ as }E_{0}^{\prime}\to\infty.

(E.142)

Proof.

Recall
eq.

C.125
:
N_{g}=\lfloor d^{1-\varepsilon_{g}}\rfloor
. Let
\varepsilon_{g}=\bar{\varepsilon}
so that we are in the regime where
lemma

E.2
holds, and
N_{g}\leq d^{1-\bar{\varepsilon}}
. Choosing

\displaystyle n_{2}=d^{\bar{\varepsilon}/4}

(E.143)

we find

\displaystyle\frac{d}{N_{g}n_{2}^{2}}\geq d^{\bar{\varepsilon}/2}.

(E.144)

Therefore, so long as
N_{2}
is
d
-independent, from
Lemma

E.3
it follows that
\varepsilon_{v}^{(2)}(t_{1},d)
decays faster than any polynomial in
d
for all fixed
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
. (As a side comment, note that we would not have been able to calculate its decay rate if
N_{2}
were
d
-dependent. This is because the function
C_{0}(N_{2})
is unknown. This will limit the rate at which
n_{2}^{-2N_{2}}
can decay as we will now see.) We now choose the parameter
N_{2}
,

\displaystyle N_{2}=\lceil 2/\bar{\varepsilon}^{1+1/2}\rceil\geq 2/\bar{\varepsilon}^{1+1/2}.

(E.145)

Therefore

\displaystyle n_{2}^{-2N_{2}}\leq d^{-1/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}

(E.146)

for all
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
.

Therefore, using the above upper bounds for
\varepsilon_{v}^{(2)}(t_{1},d)
and
n_{2}^{-2N_{2}}
, we can plug the bounds of
lemmas

E.2
,
E.4
and
E.5
into the r.h.s. of
Lemma

E.1
to achieve

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j,l}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\ket{0}_{\textup{W}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}-\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.147)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})\right)h(\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}(d)\,d^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}+\textup{poly}^{\prime}(d)\,d^{-1/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}},

(E.148)

where
\textup{poly}(d)
,
\textup{poly}^{\prime}(d)
are
\bar{\varepsilon}
-independent polynomials and
h(\bar{\varepsilon})\geq 0
is
d
-independent. All
\textup{poly}(d)
,
\textup{poly}^{\prime}(d)
, and
h(\bar{\varepsilon})
are independent from the elements of
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})\}_{l,j}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\big\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{j,l}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\ket{0}_{\textup{W}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}-\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{W}}\ket{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}}\big\|_{2}

(E.149)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{k=1}^{j}\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{r,k})\right)h(\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}^{\prime\prime}(d)\,d^{-1/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}},

(E.150)

where
\textup{poly}^{\prime\prime}(d)
is independent from the elements of
\{\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{l,j})\}_{l,j}
and
\bar{\varepsilon}
.

We will now express
d
in terms of the power
P^{\prime}
. For this, we start by calculating the mean energy of the initial state. Defining
\rho^{0}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}:=\ket{0}_{\textup{M}}\ket{0}_{\textup{W}}\ket{0}_{\textup{L}}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}\ket{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\big(\,{\mathstrut}_{\textup{M}}\!\bra{0}{\mathstrut}_{\textup{W}}\!\bra{0}{\mathstrut}_{\textup{L}}\!\bra{0}{\mathstrut}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{\Psi(0)}{\mathstrut}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\bra{\Psi(0)}\big)
, we find by direct calculation,

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\prime}:\!

\displaystyle=\textup{tr}[\rho^{0}_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}H_{\textup{M}\textup{W}\textup{L}\textup{C}\textup{C}_{2}}]={\mathstrut}_{\textup{C}}\!\braket{0|H_{\textup{C}}|0}_{\textup{C}}+{\mathstrut}_{\textup{W}}\!\bra{0}{\mathstrut}_{\textup{C}}\!\bra{0}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}}I_{\textup{W}_{l}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\right)\ket{0}_{\textup{W}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}+{\mathstrut}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\braket{0|H_{\textup{C}_{2}}|0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}

(E.151)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}\tilde{n}_{0}(d+1)+\delta E^{\prime}+{\mathstrut}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\braket{0|H_{\textup{C}_{2}}|0}_{\textup{C}_{2}},

(E.152)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}(\tilde{n}_{0}+\tilde{n}_{0,2})(d+1)+\delta E^{\prime\prime},

(E.153)

where in line
E.152
we have used
eqs.

C.145
and
C.147
and where

satisfies the same bound as
\delta E^{\prime}
, namely
eq.

C.148
. To achieve the last line, we have used that
{\mathstrut}{}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\braket{0|H_{\textup{C}}|0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
is the same as
{\mathstrut}{}_{\textup{C}}\!\braket{0|H_{\textup{C}}|0}_{\textup{C}}
after mapping the pair
\{\tilde{n}_{0},\sigma\}
to
\{\tilde{n}_{0,2},\sigma_{2}\}
, as is readily verifiable from their definitions.

5Using the relation

eq.

E.153
,

\displaystyle d=\frac{T_{0}E_{0}^{\prime}}{2\pi(\tilde{n}_{0}+\tilde{n}_{0,2})}+\delta d^{\prime},\qquad\qquad\delta d^{\prime}:=1-\frac{T_{0}(L+1)}{2\pi(\tilde{n}_{0}+\tilde{n}_{0,2})}\delta E^{\prime\prime}.

(E.154)

therefore, up to an additive vanishing term,
\delta d^{\prime}-1
, we have that
d
scales linearly with
E_{0}^{\prime}
, thus using
Lemma

C.5
to lower bound the l.h.s. of

E.150
in term of trace distance, we find

E.140
.

To achieve the scaling of the gate frequency

with energy
E_{0}^{\prime}
, i.e.

E.142
, we can proceed analogously to

C.152
for
\varepsilon_{g}=\bar{\varepsilon}
. This leads to

\displaystyle f=\frac{1}{T_{0}}\left(\frac{T_{0}}{2\pi(\tilde{n}_{0}+\tilde{n}_{0,2})}E_{0}^{\prime}\right)^{1-\bar{\varepsilon}}+\delta f^{\prime\prime},\qquad|\delta f^{\prime\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{T_{0}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\textup{poly}\big(E_{0}^{\prime}\big)\big(E_{0}^{\prime}\big)^{-1/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,\right)\text{ as }E_{0}^{\prime}\to\infty.

(E.155)

Hence

E.142
is achieved by choosing
\tilde{n}_{0}=\tilde{n}_{0,2}=1/\pi
[which is permitted since
\tilde{n}_{0},\tilde{n}_{0,2}\in(0,1)
].

To finalise the proof, using the definition of the set of non-squeezed states
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{\textup{clas.}}
from
Section

A.3.3
, we need to show that
\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
,
\textup{tr}_{\textup{M}}\big[\ket{t_{j,l}}\!\!\bra{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\big]\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{\textup{clas.}}
. We show this property now. For
\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
this follows immediately from the fact that
\sigma_{2}=\sqrt{d}
[from

E.58
], the definitions from
Section

A.3.3
and the proof that
\ket{t_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{clas.}}
(which can be found in the paragraph after

C.153
). From the definition
E.49
a direct calculation of
\Delta t_{\textup{C}_{2}}\left(\textup{tr}_{\textup{M}}\big[\ket{t_{j,l}}\!\!\bra{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\big]\right)
yields

\displaystyle\Delta t_{\textup{C}_{2}}\left(\textup{tr}_{\textup{M}}\big[\ket{t_{j,l}}\!\!\bra{t_{j,l}}_{\textup{M}\textup{C}_{2}}\big]\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}{\mathstrut}{}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\bra{\theta_{k}}t_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{2}|\psi^{(2)}_{\textup{nor}}(t_{j,l}d/T_{0},k)|^{2}\ket{\theta_{k}}_{\textup{C}_{2}}-\left({\mathstrut}{}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\bra{\theta_{k}}t_{\textup{C}_{2}}|\psi^{(2)}_{\textup{nor}}(t_{j,l}d/T_{0},k)|^{2}\ket{\theta_{k}}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\right)^{2}

(E.156)

\displaystyle={\mathstrut}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\bra{\Psi(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}t_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{2}\ket{\Psi(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}_{2}}-\left({\mathstrut}{}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\bra{\Psi(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}t_{\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{\Psi(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\right)^{2},

(E.157)

for

,

and where we have defined
\ket{\Psi(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}_{2}}
analogously to

rather than

, i.e.

(E.158)

where

is defined in

. Similarly

\displaystyle={\mathstrut}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\bra{\Psi(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}H_{\textup{C}_{2}}^{2}\ket{\Psi(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}_{2}}-\left({\mathstrut}{}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\!\!\bra{\Psi(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}H_{\textup{C}_{2}}\ket{\Psi(t_{j,l}d/T_{0})}_{\textup{C}_{2}}\right)^{2}.

(E.159)

for

,

. Therefore, from the definition of

it follows that

for

Appendix F
Generic computer self-oscillator models considered in this
manuscript

F.1
Setup and definitions for generic computer self-oscillator models considered in this
manuscript

In this section, we detail the setup of the self-oscillator and define the important quantities associated with it. Since we are interested in the generic setup, in this section, we will model the computer as a bipartite system
S
C
, where the
C
system is a self-oscillator controlling system
S
. For the reader interested in the special case pertinent to
Theorem

4
, they can set
\textup{S}=\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}
, and
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}=H^{\prime}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}
in this section. Moreover, such specialisation will be applied in
Appendix

G
, but not in
Appendix

H
where
Theorem

5
is proven.

F.1.1
Dynamics and its partially-unravelled description

As motivated in the main text, we model the self-oscillator as follows. A generic dynamical semigroup is of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}{(\cdot)}=-\mathrm{i}[H,(\cdot)]+\mathcal{D}(\cdot),\quad\mathcal{D}(\cdot)=\sum_{j}J^{j}(\cdot){J^{j}}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{{J^{j}}^{\dagger}J^{j},(\cdot)\},

(F.1)

where
H
is self-adjoint and the dissipative term,
\mathcal{D}(\cdot)
, is formed by a set
\{J^{j}\}_{j}
of arbitrary linear operators
[
39
,
24
]
. Terms of the form
J^{j}(\cdot){J^{j}}^{\dagger}
correspond to completely positive maps while those of the form
-\frac{1}{2}\{{J^{j}}^{\dagger}J^{j},(\cdot)\}
correspond to the back-reaction of the environment onto the system caused by the action of
J^{j}(\cdot){J^{j}}^{\dagger}
. The evolution operator for a time
\tau\geq 0
is
\mathrm{e}^{\tau\mathcal{L}}(\cdot)
.

In our case of a self-oscillator,
H
is of the form
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
, where
S
is the system which the control on
C
controls, while the dissipater part only acts non-trivially on the system it needs to correct—the control on
C
. Furthermore, it is bi-partitioned into the form
\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)

=

\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}(\cdot)+\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{re}}(\cdot)
. This gives rise to the total Lindbladian of our system

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}=-\mathrm{i}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}},(\cdot)]+\mathcal{D}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}.

(F.2)

For simplicity, we assume that
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{S}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{\textup{C}}}
are finite-dimensional. We make the definition

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot):=\sum_{j}J^{j}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot){J^{j}_{\textup{C}}}^{\dagger},

(F.3)

and impose the constraint

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{re}}(\rho_{\textup{C}})\propto\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0},\quad\forall\,\rho_{\textup{C}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{C}}),

(F.4)

where
\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{C}})
is a fixed state. W.l.o.g., we assume that
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
has ground-state energy zero (if it does not, we can subtract
{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}E_{0}
, where
E_{0}
is the ground-state energy without affecting the dynamics).
Let us denote by
P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})\,\textup{d}t
the probability that the
l+1
renewal event occurs during the infinitesimal time interval
[t+\tau_{l},t+\tau_{l}+\textup{d}t]
, given that the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
renewal event occurred at time
\tau_{l}
. In the following, it is useful to have the mental picture that this distribution is highly peaked around the time of approximately one period
t\approx T_{0}
, otherwise, the self-oscillator will be of bad quality since the purpose of the self-oscillator is to correct for small errors in each oscillation which would otherwise build up over time—not to significantly alter its dynamics in each cycle. We will see that this condition is indeed met in the special case of
Theorem

4
.

In order to describe our results, we are interested in considering the state of the self-oscillator during its
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle. Since the renewal of a self-oscillator is statistical in nature, we need to condition on the dynamics of the dynamical semigroup to obtain the relevant quantities. This conditioning on the dynamics of the dynamical semigroup is generically known as unravelling in the literature.
In our case, these conditional states can be defined recursively. Namely for
t\geq 0
, the state of the self-oscillator at time
t+\tau_{l}
, given that
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
renewal occurred at time
\tau_{l}
(and no more renewals have occurred after this time) is

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}:=\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\right),\quad\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{D}^{\text{re}}_{\textup{C}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}\tau_{l}|\tau_{l-1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\right)}{\textup{tr}\left[\mathcal{D}^{\text{re}}_{\textup{C}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}\tau_{l}|\tau_{l-1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\right)\right]}&\mbox{ if }l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}\\
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(0|0):=\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(0):=\rho_{\textup{S}}(0)\otimes\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}&\mbox{ if }l=0.\end{cases},

(F.5)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}(\cdot):=-\mathrm{i}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}},(\cdot)]+\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}(\cdot),\qquad\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}(\cdot)=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\{J^{j{\dagger}}_{\textup{C}}J^{j}_{\textup{C}},(\cdot)\}=-\{V_{\textup{C}},(\cdot)\},

(F.6)

and we define
0=:\tau_{0}<\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\ldots<\tau_{l}
, and
V_{\textup{C}}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}J^{j{\dagger}}_{\textup{C}}J^{j}_{\textup{C}}
. Note that the state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}\tau_{l}|\tau_{l-1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}
on the r.h.s. in
eq.

F.5
is defined by setting
\tau_{l}
to
\tau_{l-1}
and
t=\tau_{l}-\tau_{l-1}
in the l.h.s. of
eq.

F.5
. What is more, while we are only explicitly labelling the conditioning of the state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}
on having renewed at time
\tau_{l}
; in fact, due to the recursive nature of the definition, it is conditioned on having renewed at times
\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots,\tau_{l}
.

Note that
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}
is not necessarily normalised for
t>0
. The trace of
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}
corresponds to the probability of this event occurring. In general, we will use left and right bold font brackets
\bm{(\!\!\!(}
and
\bm{)\!\!\!)}
(or
\bm{[\!\![\!\![\!\![}
and
\bm{]\!\!]\!\!]\!\!]}
) to indicate that the states (or kets and bras) are not necessarily normalised and to distinguish themselves from their normalised counterparts which will use normal brackets
(
and
)
(or
[
and
]
).
We denote the normalised counterpart of
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}
by

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}):=\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}/\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}].

(F.7)

The probability that the
(l+1){{}^{\text{th}}}
renewal occurs at a time
t+\tau_{l}
, given that the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
renewal occurred at time
\tau_{l}
, is thus

\displaystyle P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l}):=\textup{tr}\left[\mathcal{D}^{\text{re}}_{\textup{C}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\right)\right]=2\,\textup{tr}[V_{\textup{C}}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}],\quad l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}.

(F.8)

F.1.2
Generation of a classical signal when each renewal process occurs

The aim of this section is to provide extra justification of the previous one. In particular, a justification of the partial-unravelling of the dynamical semigroup. Those familiar with dynamical semigroups, may wish to skip this section.

Let us construct a new Lindbladian over subsystems
S
C
R
denoted
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{R}}
. We do so by mapping
\{J^{j}_{\textup{C}}\}_{j}\to\{\tilde{J}^{j}_{\textup{C}\textup{R}}\}_{j}
, in the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
, where
\tilde{J}^{j}_{\textup{C}}:=J^{j}_{\textup{C}}\otimes J_{\textup{R}}
,
J_{\textup{R}}:=\ket{1}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{R}}+\ket{2}\!\!\bra{1}_{\textup{R}}+\ldots+\ket{N_{T}}\!\!\bra{N_{T}-1}_{\textup{R}}+\ket{0}\!\!\bra{N_{T}}_{\textup{R}}

and
\{J^{j}_{\textup{C}}\}_{j}
are defined in
Section

F.1.1
. It can readily be seen from the dynamical semigroup that if the register on
R
is initiated to
\ket{0}_{\textup{R}}
—a product state with the rest of the system—then the dynamics leads to a probabilistic mixture over product states with the register and the rest of the system with the register in one of the states
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{R}}:=\{\ket{l}_{\textup{R}}\}_{l}
. Moreover, the state of the register keeps track of the partitioning of the ensemble into the number of renewals which have occurred at time
t
. In other words, it serves as a classical counter for the number of renewal event which updates itself in real time as new events occur.

To see this, consider an arbitrary state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
which is in a tensor-product state with the counter in state
\ket{k}\!\!\bra{k}_{\textup{R}}
and consider an infinitesimal time step
\delta t
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{R}}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\otimes\ket{k}\!\!\bra{k}_{\textup{R}}\right)=\Big(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}+\delta t\,\mathcal{L}^{\textup{no re}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}})\Big)\otimes\ket{k}\!\!\bra{k}_{\textup{R}}+\delta t\,\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}})\otimes\ket{k+1}\!\!\bra{k+1}_{\textup{R}}+\mathcal{O}(\delta t^{2}).

(F.9)

Intuitively, this can be understood as saying that in every infinitesimal time step, there are two possibilities: either no renewal occurs in said time step (and the counter’s value remains unchanged), or there is a renewal event which occurs (and the counter increases its value by one).

Thus tacking into account the divisibility of dynamical semigroups, if we start out in a product state
\rho^{0}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\otimes\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{R}}
, the state of the entire computer and counter at time
t>0
will be of the form

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{R}}}\left(\rho^{0}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\otimes\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{R}}\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(k)}(t)\otimes\ket{k}\!\!\bra{k}_{\textup{R}},

(F.10)

where the (un-normalised) state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(k)}(t)
, has passed through
k
renewal events.

Finally, it is important to note that the introduction of the counter register, has not modified the dynamics of the computer in anyway, i.e. it is straightforward to show

\displaystyle\textup{tr}_{\textup{R}}\!\left[\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{R}}}\big((\cdot)\otimes\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{R}}\big)\right]=\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}}(\cdot),

(F.11)

for all
t\geq 0
. Intuitively, one can see this as a consequence of the counter register never acquiring coherence, and hence it can be measured via non-demolition measurements.

Since the renewal events occur at non-deterministic times, the state of the computer and counter system in
eq.

F.10
, at any given time, there is some possibility that the renewal process has occurred an arbitrary number of times. Of course, in reality, we need these non-deterministic times to be very close to deterministic in order to not incur large operation errors. That such a scenario is indeed possible, is proven in
Theorem

4
.

If one were to actually realise
eq.

F.10
, and then watched the counter in real time, they would never see “a mixture of all register values” at any given time. Moreover, what they would experience is the register starting off in
\ket{0}_{\textup{R}}
, and then, after a short time period, changing to
\ket{1}_{\textup{R}}
, then a bit later to
\ket{2}_{\textup{R}}
, etc. This is because
eq.

F.10
represents the experience averaged over many runs.
Equation

F.5
represents the dynamics one would actually see and experience in any one run of the computer.

What is more, one can easily reproduce the conditional dynamical state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
in
eq.

F.7
, by measuring the counter register in
eq.

F.9
from time
t=0
to the present time
t+\tau_{l}
, and conditioning on observing the following: the counter register transition from state
\ket{0}_{\textup{R}}
to
\ket{1}_{\textup{R}}
at time
\tau_{1}
,
from
\ket{1}_{\textup{R}}
to
\ket{2}_{\textup{R}}
at time
\tau_{2}
, from
\ket{2}_{\textup{R}}
to
\ket{3}_{\textup{R}}
at time
\tau_{3}
, etc, until observing it transition from
\ket{l-1}_{\textup{R}}
to
\ket{l}_{\textup{R}}
at time
\tau_{l}
; and no more changes until (and including) the present time
t+\tau_{l}
. On the other hand, the probability
\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]
is the answer to: give that said counter register transitions occurred at said times, what is the probability that no new register transitions occur in the time interval
[\tau_{l},t+\tau_{l})
.

F.2
(Non)-isentropic time interval, power consumption and dissipation

Here we will accomplish three things: First explain why the states in the so-called isentropic time interval are isentropic. Second, derive the states which
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)
has to correct. Third, derive an expression for the power consumed per cycle and power dissipated.

By definition, the states in the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
isentropic time interval, are the states on
S
C
during the time interval starting just after the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
application of
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)
at time
\tau_{l}
and finishing at time
\tau_{l}+t_{\max,l}
, where
t_{\max,l}>0
is the largest real value such that

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}:=\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}}\approx\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}}

(F.12)

holds for all
t\in[0,t_{\max,l}]
, where the
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
equality is simply the definition
F.5
. This is an imprecise definition of
t_{\max,l}
since we have not characterised what the approximation
\approx
is. In order not to break the flow or ideas, we leave its rigorous definition to
section

H.1
. Clearly, the r.h.s. of
eq.

F.12
is normalised and undergoing unitary dynamics and hence the state’s entropy is constant throughout this time interval and the process is reversible (at least to a good approximation).

The state at the end of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle which the channel
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)
acts on is

\displaystyle\lim_{t\,\uparrow\,\tau_{l+1}-\tau_{l}}\textup{tr}_{\textup{S}}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})]=\frac{\textup{tr}_{\textup{S}}\!\left[\mathrm{e}^{(\tau_{l+1}-\tau_{l})\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\right)\right]}{\textup{tr}\!\left[\mathrm{e}^{(\tau_{l+1}-\tau_{l})\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\right)\right]}.

(F.13)

Recalling the
2{{}^{\text{nd}}}
equation in
eq.

F.5
, it follows from the property
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)\propto\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}
, that the state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
itself is a product state of the form
\sigma_{\textup{S}}^{(l)}\otimes\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}
, for some system state
\sigma_{\textup{S}}
. (This is because
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)=2\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}[V_{\textup{C}}\,\cdot]\,\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}
, as we show later in the proof of
Lemma

H.3
). Moreover, unless
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
does not contain interaction terms coupling the control with the
S
system—something necessary for the control to do its job, (control the application so unitary gates on
S
)—the state on
C
in
eq.

F.13
, is a mixed state where the weights of the mixture depend on
\sigma_{\textup{S}}^{(l)}
. (In
fig.

5
, the three lines correspond to the different states in the mixture as the evolve through their distinct orbits). In the analogy with Landauer erasure, the states
eq.

F.13
generated by choosing different input states
\sigma_{\textup{S}}^{(l)}
, is analogous to the set of memory states which have to be erasable via the Landauer erasure process.
The possible states
\sigma_{\textup{S}}^{(l)}
typically, among other things, will depend on all possible logical states and permissible memory states, e.g. states in
L
and
\textup{M}_{0}
respectively in the context of
Section

V
. Note also that the channel generating the output states are not fully deterministic since the times
\tau_{l}
,
l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
are the outcomes of random variables. This feature means that these different states also have to be erasable by
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}
at multiple times in their trajectories.

We now turn to the question of the required power consumed to perform the renewal event (c.f. Landauer erasure).

The derivation will be analogous to the standard derivation of energy transfer between system and environment in dynamical semigroups (see, e.g.
[
54
,
66
]
), with the minor difference that we will conditional on the dynamics, so that we can find the contribution associated with the application of the irreversible renewal operator,
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)
per cycle, as opposed as the ensemble averages over all possible events which can occur at time
t
, i.e. a probabilistic ensemble of zero to infinite number of renewals occurring.

First, we calculate the infinitesimal change in the state, given that the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
renewal occurred in the time interval
[t+\tau_{l-1},t+\tau_{l-1}+\mathrm{d}t)
: this is

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l-1}|\tau_{l-1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\right)\mathrm{d}t,

(F.14)

where we have intentionally not normalised the state since we wish to keep the associated probability of said event occurring. One can alternatively derive this from the framework where we included the counter register in
section

F.1.2
, i.e. one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}

\displaystyle\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l-1}|\tau_{l-1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\right)\otimes\ket{l}\!\!\bra{l}_{\textup{R}}\,\delta t+\mathcal{O}(\delta t^{2})

(F.15)

\displaystyle=\ket{l}\!\!\bra{l}_{\textup{R}}\Big(\mathrm{e}^{\delta t\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{R}}}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l-1}|\tau_{l-1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\otimes\ket{l-1}\!\!\bra{l-1}_{\textup{R}}-\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l-1}|\tau_{l-1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\otimes\ket{l-1}\!\!\bra{l-1}_{\textup{R}}\Big)\ket{l}\!\!\bra{l}_{\textup{R}}.

(F.16)

The associated change in internal energy during this infinitesimal time step is

\displaystyle\textup{tr}\left[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l-1}|\tau_{l-1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\right)\right]\mathrm{d}t.

(F.17)

Thus, the total energy exchanges associated with the implementation of the irreversible operator
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}
in the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle is

\displaystyle P^{\textup{in}}_{l}:=\frac{\braket{E^{\text{re}}}}{T_{0}};\quad\braket{E^{\text{re}}}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\,\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\text{re}}(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)})].

(F.18)

(Here we have used the convention that the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle corresponds to the interval
[\tau_{l},\tau_{l+1})
as opposed to
[\tau_{l-1},\tau_{l})
. Hence
eq.

F.18
is a function of
\tau_{l}
rather than
\tau_{l-1}
.)

Note that since by definition
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\geq 0
and
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)
is completely positive, it follows that the integrand in
eq.

F.18
is non-negative. Thus, it corresponds to energy flowing
into
the system from the environment (if non-zero). Taking into account the form of the operator
\mathcal{D}^{\textup{re}}_{\textup{C}}
, it would be zero if the renewed state
\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}
, has zero mean energy (i.e. its the ground state) since probability
\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]
is non-negative.

However, if
\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}
is in the ground state (i.e. has mean energy zero), then, since the initial state is
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(0|0)=\rho_{\textup{S}}(0)\otimes\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}
, (recall
eq.

F.5
), no free dynamics of the oscillator due to
H_{\textup{C}}
would have occurred, and the only energy acquired by the control on
C
, would have need internally from
S
via the
S
-
C
interaction terms in the
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
Hamiltonian. This wound most likely result in a terrible computer with huge errors, and the energy required for renewal could come solely from
S
. In the case of
Theorem

4
, the special case used in the proof which achieves the optimal scaling has
\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{C}}\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}]
which is large (proportional ot the dimension which is itself proportional to the power consumed), while in the case of
Theorem

5
, it is effectively eliminated by assumption (the to-be-defined instantaneous initial-cycle-state parameter error,
\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}
is small by assumption).

By analogous reasoning, we find that, if in the interval
(\tau_{l},t+\tau_{l}]
no renewal event has occurred, then the net flow of energy
into

S
C
from the environment is

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\text{no re}}(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)})].

(F.19)

Thus, to find the average flow of energy into
S
C
during one cycle, one needs to integrate up until the end of the cycle, at time
\tau_{l+1}
. This happens at time
t+\tau_{l}
with probability
P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})
, thus taking the averaged time, we find that the energy flowing
into
the system on cycle average is

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,P({t+\tau_{l}},+1|\tau_{l})\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\text{no re}}(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)})].

(F.20)

This is negative, since it has to balance out the positive flow of energy into the system during the renewal even itself. If not, there would be a build-up of energy in
S
C
, yet the internal state of the computer and control is zero.
Thus the energy flowing
out
of the computer into the environment is

\displaystyle\braket{E^{\text{no re}}}:=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,P({t+\tau_{l}},+1|\tau_{l})\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\text{no re}}(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)})].

(F.21)

Thus we define the dissipated energy per cycle into the environment as

\displaystyle P^{\textup{diss}}_{l}:=\frac{\braket{E^{\text{no re}}}}{T_{0}}.

(F.22)

Finally, it is worth pointing out that these definitions of
P^{\textup{in}}
and
P^{\textup{diss}}
are perfectly consistent with the existence of the isentropic time intervals discussed at the beginning of this section. The existence of an isentropic interval in the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle simply implies that the integrands in
eqs.

F.18
and
F.22
are close to zero for a significant interval over which they are being integrated, where said interval corresponds to the isentropic interval.

F.3
Dependency on
T_{0}

This section can be skipped on a
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
read. It only derives how certain quantities scale with the cycle time
T_{0}
.

Recall that in the cases where we considered computational models with Hamiltonian dynamics, the dependency on gate frequency
f
on
T_{0}
was the same in both the quantum and classical cases, and thus there was no quantum advantage deliverable by changing
T_{0}
. Here, we will show that the same is true in the open quantum systems case considers here, where the dynamics is given by a dynamical semigroup as opposed to a Hamiltonian. This was discussed in a paragraph shortly after
Theorem

1
.

We will show that the dependency of
P^{\textup{in}}
on
T_{0}
is that it is proportional to
1/T_{0}^{2}
, so that the theorems quantifying the gate frequency
f
in terms of
P^{\textup{in}}
, namely
Theorems

4
,
5
and
H.1
, all convey the same dependency of the gate frequency with cycle time:
f
is directly proportional to
1/T_{0}
. This is to say, its dependency is identical to the dependency of the computers which were modelled via Hamiltonian dynamics.

To start with, recall
eqs.

F.5
and
F.6
. The initial state on
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(0|0)
is
T_{0}
-independent. We can re-write the dynamics in terms of the dimensionless parameters,
\bar{t}:=t/T_{0}
, and
\bar{\tau}_{l}:=\tau_{l}/T_{0}
as follows:

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}\bar{t}+\bar{\tau}_{l}|\bar{\tau}_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}:=\mathrm{e}^{\bar{t}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}}\left(\bar{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\bar{\tau}_{l}|\bar{\tau}_{l})\right),\quad\bar{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\bar{\tau}_{l}|\bar{\tau}_{l}):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{re}}_{\textup{C}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}\bar{\tau}_{l}|\bar{\tau}_{l-1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\right)}{\textup{tr}\left[\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{re}}_{\textup{C}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}\bar{\tau}_{l}|\bar{\tau}_{l-1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\right)\right]}&\mbox{ if }l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}\\
\bar{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(0|0):=\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(0|0):=\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(0):=\rho_{\textup{S}}(0)\otimes\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}&\mbox{ if }l=0.\end{cases},

(F.23)

where

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}(\cdot):=-\mathrm{i}[\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}},(\cdot)]+\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}(\cdot),\qquad\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}(\cdot)=-\{\bar{V}_{\textup{C}},(\cdot)\},

(F.24)

with

\displaystyle\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}=H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}T_{0},\qquad\bar{V}_{\textup{C}}=:V_{\textup{C}}T_{0}.

(F.25)

Here, we define the dimensionless Hamiltonian and interaction terms,
\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
and
\bar{V}_{\textup{C}}
, to be
T_{0}
-independent. Or equivalently, that
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
and
V_{\textup{C}}
are directly proportional to
1/T_{0}
. This definition can be readily motivated as follows: Recall that
T_{0}
is the cycle time, it thus follows that the dynamics of the initial control cycle state, namely
\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}
, under the free Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{C}}
, is
T_{0}
-periodic. Thus
H_{\textup{C}}
should be directly proportional to
T_{0}
such that a change in
T_{0}
does not break the periodicity of the dynamics. Likewise, the full dynamics including the interaction terms, needs to be finely tuned to the periodicity of the free dynamics of
H_{\textup{C}}
, because the same dynamics on
S
C
needs to be achieved, regardless of the cycle time
T_{0}
. Thus one needs all interaction strengths to be proportional to
1/T_{0}
in order for
1/T_{0}
to be a common factor in the dynamics so that a change in
T_{0}
, merely re-scales the speed at which the full dynamics unfolds. Hence we arrive at
\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
and
\bar{V}_{\textup{C}}
being
T_{0}
-independent. The fact that they are dimensionless follows from the fact that we are using natural units (i.e.“units such that
\hbar=1
”), so energy has units of inverse time. Similarly, we have that the quantity
\bar{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)T_{0}
is dimensionless and
T_{0}
-independent.

Finally, note that the particular choice of
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
and
V_{\textup{C}}
which were used in the proof of
Theorem

4
are exactly directly proportional to
1/T_{0}
, in agreement to the above definition.

From these definitions, it thus follows that
\bar{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}{\bm{(\!\!\!(}}(\cdot)+\bar{\tau}_{l}|\bar{\tau}_{l}{\bm{)\!\!\!)}}:{\mathbbm{R}_{\geq 0}}\to\mathcal{\hat{S}}({\mathcal{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}})
is a
T_{0}
-independent function whose real input is dimensionless. By comparing
eqs.

F.5
and
F.6
with
eqs.

F.23
and
F.24
, we find the relation

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}\bar{t}\,T_{0}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}=\bar{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}\bar{t}+\bar{\tau}_{l}|\bar{\tau}_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)},\qquad\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})=\bar{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\bar{\tau}_{l}|\bar{\tau}_{l}),\qquad l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}.

(F.26)

It thus follows from
eq.

F.18
, that

\displaystyle\braket{E^{\text{re}}}:=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\,\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\text{re}}(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)})]=\frac{1}{T_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\bar{s}\,\textup{tr}[\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\mathcal{\bar{D}}_{\textup{C}}^{\text{re}}(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}\bar{s}T_{0}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)})]

(F.27)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\bar{s}\,\textup{tr}[\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\mathcal{\bar{D}}_{\textup{C}}^{\text{re}}(\bar{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}\bar{s}+\bar{\tau}_{l}|\bar{\tau}_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)})],

(F.28)

where we performed the change of integration variable,
\bar{s}:=s/T_{0}
after the
2{{}^{\text{nd}}}
equality. Now note that the integral in line
F.28
is written entirely in terms of
T_{0}
-independent quantities. It thus follows that

\displaystyle\braket{\bar{E}^{\text{re}}}:=\braket{E^{\text{re}}}T_{0}

(F.29)

is
T_{0}
-independent. Hence

\displaystyle P^{\textup{in}}_{l}:=\frac{\braket{E^{\text{re}}}}{T_{0}}=\frac{\braket{\bar{E}^{\text{re}}}}{T_{0}^{2}}

(F.30)

and the dimensionless quantity

\displaystyle T_{0}^{2}P^{\textup{in}}_{l}

(F.31)

is
T_{0}
-independent. Therefore, the gate frequency
f
in
eq.

V.11
in
Theorem

4
is directly proportional to
1/T_{0}
, analogously to the dependency in
eqs.

II.3
and
II.4
.

For completeness, we will now show that the instantaneous initial-cycle-state parameter error,
\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}
can be chosen to be
T_{0}
-independent w.l.o.g. (this is only relevant for
Section

VI
.)
For this, we start by noting that the
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
moment of the probability density
P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})
can be written in the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{l}(1):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})=T_{0}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\bar{t}\,\bar{t}\,2\textup{tr}[\bar{V}_{\textup{C}}\bar{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}\bar{t}+\bar{\tau}_{l}|\bar{\tau}_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]=:T_{0}\,\bar{\mathbb{M}}_{l}(1),

(F.32)

where
\bar{\mathbb{M}}_{l}(1)
is manifestly
T_{0}
-independent.

From the definition of the instantaneous initial-cycle-state parameter,
\varepsilon^{0}_{\textup{H}}
in
eq.

H.13
, and the above definitions, one has that
\|\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}[\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}]\|_{F}
, and
\|\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\|_{F}
are directly proportional to
1/T_{0}
. Then using
eq.

F.32
, we conclude that
\varepsilon^{0}_{\textup{H}}
is an upper bound to a
T_{0}
-independent quantity, and hence can itself be chosen to be
T_{0}
-independent.

Appendix G
Achieving the lower bound for a quantum frequential computer in a non-equilibrium steady state

Here we will now specialise the description of a computer with a self-oscillator from
Section

F.1
to the case relevant for
Theorem

4
and describe how the memory on
\textup{M}_{0}
is updated in accordance with how this was previously achieved via the bus. We finish with the proof of
Theorem

4
.

G.1
Specialization of the generator of dynamics and
\textup{M}_{0}
memory updating rule

We start by speciating the terms in the dynamical semigroup
eq.

F.2
to the relative terms. Some of the parameter choices will be left to the proofs in the subsequent section.

In our case, we choose
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}=H^{\prime}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}
where

\displaystyle H^{\prime}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}=H_{\textup{C}}+\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}-1}I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)},

(G.1)

and where the terms on the r.h.s. are as in
Appendix

E
. E.g. for
I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)}
see definition in
eq.

E.4
.
Recall that the dissipative part is fully determined by specifying the operators
\{J_{\textup{C}}^{j}\}_{j}
. In our case, we set

\displaystyle J_{\textup{C}}^{j}=\sqrt{2v_{j}}\,{}_{\textup{C}}\!\ket{0}\!\!\bra{\theta_{j}}_{\textup{C}},\qquad v_{j}>0,

(G.2)

j=0,1,\ldots,d-1
where
\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}
is the unperturbed initial state of the oscillator and
\{\ket{\theta_{j}}_{\textup{C}}\}_{j=0}^{d-1}
is the discrete Fourier transform basis of the energy eigenbasis of
H_{\textup{C}}
defined in
eq.

C.25
.

One can check that this definition readily satisfies
eq.

F.4
. This is to say, the renewal process therefore maps all input states to the state of the oscillator to its initial state:
\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{re}}(\rho_{\textup{C}})\propto\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{C}}
for all
\rho_{\textup{C}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{C}})
. Moreover, in this case the output state
\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{C}}
is exactly the state after each cycle when it evolves according to its free dynamics
without
any perturbations due to it controlling the implementation of gates, in other words, according to
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}jT_{0}H_{\textup{C}}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}=\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}
,
j\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
. Therefore, if the renewal process occurs periodically with period
T_{0}
it will correct for the small perturbative errors incurred by the oscillator due to its implementation of the logical gates required for the computation.

We can also evaluate the generator of dynamics under the scenario of no renewal. Recall that if a renewal operation does not occur in the infinitesimal time interval
[\tau,\tau+\mathrm{d}\tau]
, then the state is mapped from
\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\tau)
to
\mathcal{L}^{\textup{no re}}(\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\tau))
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}(\cdot):=-\mathrm{i}[H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}},(\cdot)]+\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}(\cdot),\qquad\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}(\cdot)=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\{J^{j{\dagger}}_{\textup{C}}J^{j}_{\textup{C}},(\cdot)\}.

(G.3)

Thus from the form of
eq.

G.3
one can solve the dynamics to find a significant simplification. Namely

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\tau\mathcal{L}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}}(\cdot)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\tau G_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}}(\cdot)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\tau G_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}^{\dagger}}

(G.4)

where

\displaystyle G_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}:=H_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}-\mathrm{i}V_{\textup{C}},\qquad V_{\textup{C}}:=\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}v_{j}\ket{\theta_{j}}\!\!\bra{\theta_{j}}_{\textup{C}}>0.

(G.5)

We choose

\displaystyle V_{\textup{C}}=\gamma_{0}\,I_{\textup{C}}^{(N_{g})}+\frac{\varepsilon_{b}}{2T_{0}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{C}},

(G.6)

where
I_{\textup{C}}^{(N_{g})}
is the positive semidefinite interaction term used in the proofs of
Theorems

2
and
3
defined in
eq.

C.26
,
\varepsilon_{b}>0
will be specified later in
eq.

G.74
is vanishing for large
E_{0}
for all fixed
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
. The parameter
\gamma_{0}>0
is a scale factor (specified later in
eq.

G.16
) and
{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{C}}
the identity operator.

The states
\{\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\}_{l}
have just undergone the renewal process and are thus the output of channel
eq.

F.3
. As a consequence, they are of the form

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})=\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}[\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})]\otimes\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{C}}.

(G.7)

We will now discuss how the implicit updating on the memory cells in
\textup{M}_{0}
via the bus is modelled. To this end, we start by noting that
eq.

G.7
is a product state with memory cell
\textup{M}_{0,1}
due to
eq.

V.3
. Moreover, it is convenient for the following proofs to work with pure states. We will therefore purify the state on
\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}
via ancillary systems which we denote
P
, leading to a state

\displaystyle\ket{\rho(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}\textup{P}}:=\ket{\mathbb{m}_{l,1}}_{\textup{M}_{0,1}\textup{P}_{1}}\ket{(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,1}\textup{W}\textup{L}\bar{\textup{P}}_{1}}\ket{0}_{\textup{C}}

(G.8)

which satisfies
\textup{tr}_{\textup{P}}\big[\ket{\rho(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}\!\!\bra{\rho(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big]=\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
.
In this
appendix
we used the convention
\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,k}=\textup{M}_{0}\backslash\textup{M}_{0,k}
16
16

16

This is the convention used in all of
Appendix

G
. Note that it differs from the convention used in
Appendix

E
where
\bar{\textup{M}}_{0,k}=\textup{M}\backslash\textup{M}_{0,k}
.

and each memory cell
\textup{M}_{0,k}
we associate with its own ancilla
\textup{P}_{k}
and additional ancillae for
W
L
. Here
\bar{\textup{P}}_{1}
denotes the total ancilla system after the removal of
\textup{P}_{1}
. We will use the notation
\ket{\mathbb{m}}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}\textup{P}_{k}}=\ket{\mathbb{m}}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}}\ket{\mathbb{m}}_{\textup{P}_{k}}
for all
\mathbb{m}\in\mathcal{G}\cup\{{\bf 0}\}
since said states on
\textup{M}_{0,k}
are already pure.

The state
\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
can be calculated from the state
\ket{\rho(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}
by evolving it according to the dynamical semigroup while conditioning on a renewal event not occurring. Had this been the only process involved, the dynamics would have been given by
\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})=\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}}(\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}))/P(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
where
P(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})=\textup{tr}\left[\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}}(\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}))\right]
in accordance with
eq.

F.23
. However, recall that the bus is updating the memory cells
\textup{M}_{0}
in a similar fashion to the autonomous and explicit formulation in
Theorem

3
, although here modelled implicitly for convenience. We will thus model said memory updates as the application of unitaries at discrete times to the memory cells in
\textup{M}_{0}
which the bus updates as said times.

In particular, we have assumed that
eq.

V.3
holds and complete ignorance on the other memory cells contained in
\textup{M}_{0}
. We thus implement this assumption via the unitary transformation denoted
U_{\textup{M}_{0,k}\textup{P}_{k}\textup{M}_{0,k-1}\textup{P}_{k-1}}
on the states in
\textup{M}_{0}
which have been purified. Therefore, noting that the channel
eq.

G.4
preserves purity, the states
\{\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\}_{l=0}^{\infty}
for
t\in[t_{k-1},t_{k})
and
k\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
are given by
17
17

17

As in the previous sections, kets and bras containing left and right bold font brackets
\bm{(\!\!\!(}
and
\bm{)\!\!\!)}
(or
\bm{[\!\![\!\![\!\![}
and
\bm{]\!\!]\!\!]\!\!]}
) indicate that the states are not necessarily normalised and to distinguish them from their normalised counterparts which will use normal brackets
(
and
)
or
[
and
]
.

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})P(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})

\displaystyle=\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)},

(G.9)

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}

\displaystyle=\textup{tr}_{\textup{P}}\big[\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}\!\!\bra{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big],

(G.10)

\displaystyle\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}\textup{P}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(t-t_{k-1})G_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}}\,U^{(l,k)}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}\textup{P}_{k}\textup{M}_{0,k-1}\textup{P}_{k-1}}\ket{\bm{(\!\!\!(}k,l\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}\textup{P}},

(G.11)

where
18
18

18

In the following we have denoted by
U^{(l,k)}_{\textup{M}_{0,k}\textup{P}_{k}\textup{M}_{0,k-1}\textup{P}_{k-1}}
the unitary denoted by
U^{(l,k)}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{P}}
in the main text. This is to avoid notational clutter in the main text while permitting more expression in the

. Furthermore, note that memory cell and ancilla
\textup{M}_{0,0}\textup{P}_{0}
have not been defined. Such systems are not required, and only appear here. Since this operator is proportional to the identity, such systems are only introduced for notational convenience.

\displaystyle\ket{\bm{(\!\!\!(}k,l\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}\textup{P}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{1}G_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}}\,U^{(l,k-1)}_{\textup{M}_{0,k-1}\textup{P}_{k-1}\textup{M}_{0,k-2}\textup{P}_{k-2}}\ket{\bm{(\!\!\!(}k-1,l\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}\textup{P}},\qquad k-1\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}

(G.12)

\displaystyle\ket{(1,l)}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}\textup{P}}

\displaystyle:=\ket{(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}\textup{P}},\qquad U^{(l,1)}_{\textup{M}_{0,1}\textup{P}_{k}\textup{M}_{0,0}\textup{P}_{0}}={\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{M}_{0,1}\textup{P}_{1}\textup{M}_{0,0}\textup{P}_{0}},

(G.13)

and the conditional probability
P(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
is defined by taking the trace on both sides of
