Appendix H
Upper bounds on gate frequency for self-oscillators

In the following, we assume the description laid-out in
Appendix

F
. Thus, as in said section, here
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
and
H_{\textup{C}}
are arbitrary Hamiltonians on finite dimensional Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{C}}
respectively. (Finite dimensions are only assumed for simplicity, to avoid the usual complications which can arise in infinite dimensions. Since the actual dimensions do not appear in any of the results, generalizations to the infinitive-dimensional case should be possible via conventional techniques.) In this section, we also assume that
S
contains a logical system
L
as a subsystem, and denote the remaining system after removing
L
from
S
by
[S
\backslash
L]
. (Note the consistency with the special case assumed in the context of
Theorem

4
, where
\textup{S}=\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}
.)

H.1
Quantities pertaining to the quality of computer’s self-oscillator and some lemmas about them

Since
V_{\textup{C}}
is positive semi-definite, we can write it in the form
V_{\textup{C}}=\sum_{j}v_{j}\ket{v_{j}}\!\!\bra{v_{j}}
, with
v_{j}\geq 0
and
\{\ket{v_{j}}\}_{j}
an orthonormal basis. This in turn allows one to write
P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})
in terms of a weighted 1-norm where the weights are time dependent:

\displaystyle P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})

\displaystyle=2\,\|\vec{V}_{\textup{C}}\|_{w,1}(t,l),\quad\vec{V}_{\textup{C}}:=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},\ldots),

(H.1)

\displaystyle\|\vec{h}\|_{w,q}(t,l)

\displaystyle:={\left(\sum_{j}\left|h_{j}\right|^{q}p_{j}(t,l)\!\right)\!}^{1/q},\quad p_{j}(t,l):=\braket{v_{j}|\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}|v_{j}}.

(H.2)

As we have discussed in the main text, self-oscillators have a notion of an isentropic time interval. We will soon use these above identities to define when the isentropic interval of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle ends. The isentropic time interval is the time interval in each cycle in which the self-oscillator is not being corrected and can implement gates on the logical space. The influence of the restoring force on the oscillator does not dynamically turned off and on abruptly, but gradually ramped up and down over the cycle. As such, the time at which the isentropic time interval of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle ends is only approximately defined. We define it here, by imposing a hard cut-off, beyond which the effects of the restoring force on the dynamics are considered to be not-neglectable and before which they are neglectable. With this in mind, we denote the isentropic time interval of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle as

\displaystyle[\tau_{l},t_{\max,l}+\tau_{l}).

(H.3)

The value of
t_{\max,l}
depends on 2 factors: 1) the quality of the self-oscillators ability to only apply the restoring force towards the end of the cycle while still being strong enough to correct for errors, and 2) our willingness to tolerate small deviations from unitary dynamics over
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
during the time-interval
[\tau_{l},t_{\max,l}+\tau_{l})
. We now define these two quantities followed by quantifying their values.

The
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
can be characterised by integrating the weighted 2-norm of
\vec{V}_{\textup{C}}
, over interval
[0,\tau]
for some
\tau\in{\mathbbm{R}}
, namely

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(\tau):=\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathrm{d}t\,\|\vec{V}_{\textup{C}}\|_{w,2}(t,l).

(H.4)

Note that if
V_{\textup{C}}
and
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
are orthogonal for
t
in some interval, then the integrand on the r.h.s. of
eq.

H.4
is also zero over said interval. It is also a non-decreasing function of its argument
\tau
. Thus
eq.

H.4
is a measure of isentropicity in its own right.
For the
2{{}^{\text{nd}}}
, we can consider how much gate implementation error we are willing to tolerate. For that, we can devise a figure of merit based on the performance in the special case in which, in the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle, we wish to implement a sequence of gates, which map the logical state to a sequence of orthonormal states, denoted

\displaystyle\ket{1,l}_{\textup{L}},\ket{2,l}_{\textup{L}},\ket{3,l}_{\textup{L}},\ldots

(H.5)

with equal time
t_{1}
between them (i.e., the
j{{}^{\text{th}}}
gate maps state
\ket{j,l}_{\textup{L}}
to
\ket{j+1,l}_{\textup{L}}
). For simplicity of notation, we assume that the sequence spans
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{S}}
. We will implement as many as possible within the time interval
[\tau_{l},t_{\max,l}+\tau_{l})
, and the rest in later cycles: specifically the first
h_{l}
gates in the sequence, where
h_{l}=\max\{j\in{\mathbbm{Z}_{>0}}\,|\,t_{\max,l}-jt_{1}>0\}
. As with the upper bounds in
Section

II
, for simplicity, we assume that the gate frequency of our computer is the same for all gates it can implement. We quantify our tolerance to error in terms of trace distance
T
be requiring that the following holds

\displaystyle T\big(\rho_{\textup{L}}(t_{j}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}),\ket{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}\in[0,1/2),

(H.6)

where
t_{j}=jt_{1}\in(0,t_{\max,l})
,
j\in{\mathbbm{Z}_{>0}}
and

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{L}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})=\textup{tr}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})],

(H.7)

(where
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
defined in
eq.

F.7
), are the normalised states conditioned on the cycle in question. The
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
condition in
eq.

H.6
quantifies how close the actual dynamics is to the desired gate, while the
2{{}^{\text{nd}}}
condition quantifies how close this is to orthogonality with the next gate. For a good implementation, we would demand that the parameter
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}
is small.

Together, the parameters
\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}
,
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}
allow us to quantitatively define length of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
isentropic time interval
[\tau_{l},t_{\max,l}+\tau_{l})
: For a given
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}
, the quantity
t_{\max,l}\geq 0
is the largest constant such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4(1+\lambda)^{2}}=\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}(2-{\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}})+2\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(t_{\max,l})\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}},

(H.8)

where
\lambda=4.64
. Note that since
\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}
increases with
t_{\max,l}
, the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
isentropic time interval will be largest when
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}
is small.

Before continuing with the relevant definitions. We make a small side-step with the following lemma.

Lemma H.1

(Bounds for
\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(\cdot)
)
.

For all
\tau\geq 0
,
l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}

:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathrm{d}t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})=\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathrm{d}t\,\|\vec{V}_{\textup{C}}\|_{w,1}(t,l)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(\tau),

(H.9)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(\tau)\leq\big(\|V_{\textup{C}}\|\,\tau\big)^{3/4}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathrm{d}t\,\|\vec{V}_{\textup{C}}\|_{w,1}(t,l)\right)^{1/4}=\frac{\left(\|V_{\textup{C}}\|\,\tau\right)^{3/4}}{2^{1/4}}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathrm{d}t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})\right)^{1/4},

(H.10)

where
\|\cdot\|
is the operator norm.

Proof is in
Section

H.4
. Due to monotonicity with respect to
\tau
of all terms in
eq.

H.10
,
Lemma

H.1
allows one to generate lower and upper bounds on
t_{\max,l}
when the conditional probability distribution
P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})
is known. What is more, it also shows that if
P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})
is sufficiently small when integrated over
t\in[0,t^{*}]
, then
t_{\max,l}\geq t^{*}
, while if said probability becomes sufficiently large when integrated over
t\in[t^{*},t^{**}]
, then
t_{\max,l}\leq t^{**}
. This makes sense, because
P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})
is the probability that the
(l+1){{}^{\text{th}}}
renewal event occurs, and can only become non-zero when the system
S
C
starts to interact with the environment and subsequently takes it out of the isentropic time interval. In
Section

H.5
we apply
Lemma

H.1
to find a bound for
t_{\max,l}
in the context of
Theorem

4
.

We aim to bound the gate frequency

\displaystyle f=1/t_{1}

(H.11)

with which the gates during the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
isentropic time interval,
[\tau_{l},t_{\max,l}+\tau_{l})
are applied up to error
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}
.

•

1) [Universally bounded
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
moments]: there exists
\mathbb{M}(1)<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{l}(1):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})\leq\mathbb{M}(1)

(H.12)

for all
l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
. This is to say, we assume that the
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
moment of the conditional distributions
\{P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})\}_{l}
are universally upper bounded. As we show in
section

F.3
,
\mathbb{M}_{l}(1)
is always directly proportional to
T_{0}
. Moreover, when the oscillator is of good quality, we expect
\mathbb{M}_{l}(1)/T_{0}\approx 1
because in such cases the probability of renewing the oscillator should be peaked around the cycle time.

•

2) [Instantaneous Initial-cycle-state control of
S
]:
The interval of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
isentropic time interval initiates immediately after the oscillator state on
C
is corrected to
\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}
. We want the control on
S
to start gradually after the state has been renewed to
\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}
. For this, we need the state of the control on
C
to initially be non-interacting with the system
S
, otherwise, among other things, the interactions would interfere with the renewal process. As such we want
\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}
to be orthogonal to all terms in
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
which act non-trivially on both
S
and
C
, while allowing it to only act non-trivially on the terms which solely act on
C
. This way the state of the oscillator can only start to interact with
S
after dynamically evolving due to its free dynamics. For generality, rather than demanding that
\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}
is
exactly
orthogonal to said terms, we allow for a small overlap of
\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}
with the non-free terms. Explicitly, for any Hamiltonian
H_{\textup{C}}
,
\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}
is defined as any quantity to satisfy the following bound

\displaystyle{\left\|\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}[\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}]\right\|_{F}}{\|\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\|_{F}}2\mathbb{M}(1)T_{0}\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0},\quad\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}:=H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}-H_{\textup{C}},

(H.13)

where
\|\cdot\|_{F}
is the Frobenius norm. We will be interested in the cases in which
\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}
is small relative to the power
T_{0}^{2}P^{\textup{in}}_{l}
.

We call
\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}
the
instantaneous initial-cycle-state parameter
. In order for the l.h.s. of
eq.

H.13
to be small, there must be no free terms of the form
H_{\textup{S}}
in
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
, otherwise, it would appear to not be possible to satisfy
eq.

H.13
. Therefore, all the control and dynamics on
S
is generated via interactions with the oscillator. This is in accordance with all previously-defined hamiltonians in this
manuscript
. In addition to
\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}
being dimensionless, it is
T_{0}
-independent, as we show in
section

F.3
.

H.2
Proof of
Theorem

5
: upper bounds for gate frequency with self-oscillators as control

We now state the main lemma for the upper bounds on orthogonalization time for self-oscillators. From this result,
Theorem

5
readily follows by using simplifying assumptions.

Lemma H.2

(Upper bounds on gate frequency in the nonequilibrium steady-state regime)
.

For all
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}\in[0,1)
, the frequency
f
at which the gate sequence
eq.

H.5
is implemented up to error
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}

during the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
isentropic time interval
[\tau_{l},t_{\max,l}+\tau_{l})
, is bounded as follows:

Case 1):

\displaystyle f\leq\frac{\sqrt{T_{0}\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})]}}{c_{0}T_{0}},

(H.14)

if
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\in\mathcal{C}^{\text{clas.,\,}l}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
.

Case 2):

\displaystyle f\leq\frac{\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})]}{\kappa},

(H.15)

otherwise.

Here
\mathcal{C}^{\text{clas.,\,}l}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
is the set of classical states defined in
section

A.3.4
and
\kappa>0,c_{0}>0
are numerical constants.

Proof.

The proof will follows similar lines of reasoning to those of
appendix

B
, but with additional complication due to the dynamics being give by a dynamical semigroup rather than a Hamiltonian, and the states during the computation not being exactly mutually orthogonal but merely close.

We start by setting up the metrology problem: Consider an observable

\displaystyle M=\sum_{k}t_{\textup{est}}(\xi_{k})\ket{k,l}\!\!\bra{k,l}_{\textup{L}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}}

(H.16)

where our signal estimates are chosen to be
t_{\textup{est}}(\xi_{j})=t_{j}+\Delta\,t_{1}=(j+\Delta)t_{1}
and
t_{\textup{est}}(\xi_{k})=(j-\Delta+1)t_{1}
for
k\neq j
. Note that for additional clarify, and in contrast to the rest of this
manuscript
, we have refrained from omitting the tensor product with the identity in
eq.

H.16
.
We will be interested in bounding the probabilities associated with getting outcome
\xi_{j}
given that the time is
t_{k}
, namely
P(\xi_{j}|t_{k})
when the state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
is evolved according to the unitary part of the dynamics:

\displaystyle P(\xi_{j}|t_{k}):=\textup{tr}\big[\ket{j,l}\!\!\bra{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}t_{k}H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t_{k}H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}}\big]=\big\|\ket{j,l}\!\!\bra{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{k})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}^{2}

(H.17)

where we have used the fact that
\ket{j,l}\!\!\bra{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}}
is a projector, and defined
\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}:=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}}\ket{(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}
, where
\ket{(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}
is a purification of
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
.

Let us start with the case
P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1})
. Employing the triangle inequality and
eq.

H.6
, we have

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=T(\ket{j,l}_{\textup{L}},\ket{j+1,l}_{\textup{L}})\leq T(\ket{j,l}_{\textup{L}},\tilde{\rho}_{\textup{L}}(t_{j+1}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}))+T\big(\tilde{\rho}_{\textup{L}}(t_{j+1}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}),\ket{j+1,l}_{\textup{L}}\big)

(H.18)

\displaystyle\leq T(\ket{j,l}_{\textup{L}},\tilde{\rho}_{\textup{L}}(t_{j+1}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}))+\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}.

(H.19)

Thus using the inequality
T(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\leq\sqrt{1-F(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})}
where
F
is the quantum Fidelity, we find

\displaystyle 1-(1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}})^{2}\geq F(\ket{j,l}_{\textup{L}},\rho_{\textup{L}}(t_{j+1}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}))=\textup{tr}[\ket{j,l}\!\!\bra{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\rho_{\textup{L}}(t_{j+1}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})]

(H.20)

\displaystyle=\textup{tr}[(\ket{j,l}\!\!\bra{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}\textup{P}})\ket{\rho(t_{j+1})}\!\!\bra{\rho(t_{j+1})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}]=\big\|(\ket{j,l}\!\!\bra{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}\textup{P}})\ket{\rho(t_{j+1})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}^{2}

(H.21)

\displaystyle=\frac{\big\|(\ket{j,l}\!\!\bra{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}\textup{P}})\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j+1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}^{2}}{\big\|\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j+1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}^{2}}

(H.22)

where
\ket{\rho(t_{j+1})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}:=\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j+1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}/\|\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j+1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\|_{2}
, and is a purification of
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t_{j+1}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}):=\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j+1}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}/\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j+1}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]
. We now solve
eq.

F.5
, to find

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tG}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tG^{\dagger}},\quad G:=H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}-\mathrm{i}V_{\textup{C}},

(H.23)

Therefore, it follows that

\displaystyle\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tG}\ket{\rho(0)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}=\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}+\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}},

(H.24)

where we have defined

\displaystyle\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}:=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}}\ket{(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}},\quad\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}:=\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}-\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}=(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tG}-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}})\ket{(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}.

(H.25)

By direct calculation,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\|_{2}^{2}=-2\,{}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\!\bra{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}V_{\textup{C}}\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\leq 0,

(H.26)

hence since
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
is normalised, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\|_{2}\leq 1

(H.27)

for
t\geq 0
. Similarly, since
\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}
evolves unitarily,

\displaystyle\|\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\|_{2}=1.

(H.28)

We can now derive a lower bound on norms of the form
\|\hat{p}\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\|_{2}
where
\hat{p}
is a projector. Via
eq.

H.28
, and the reverse triangle inequality, it follows

\displaystyle\big\|\hat{p}\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\big\|\hat{p}\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}+\hat{p}\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}\geq\big\|\hat{p}\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}-\big\|\hat{p}\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}

(H.29)

\displaystyle\geq\big\|\hat{p}\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}-\big\|\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}.

(H.30)

Thus from
eq.

H.22
and
eq.

H.27
it follows

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-(1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}})^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\big\|\ket{j,l}\!\!\bra{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j+1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}\geq\big\|\ket{j,l}\!\!\bra{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j+1})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}-\big\|\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j+1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}

(H.31)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1})}-\big\|\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j+1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2},

(H.32)

where we used definition
H.17
in the last line. It thus follows that

\displaystyle\sqrt{P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1})}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}(2-{\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}})}+\big\|\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j+1}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}.

(H.33)

We now proceed to bound
P(\xi_{j}|t_{j})
. Since one of the states in
eq.

H.6
is pure, we can use the inequality
1-F(\cdot,\cdot)\leq T(\cdot,\cdot)
to get

\displaystyle 1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}

\displaystyle\leq F\big(\tilde{\rho}_{\textup{L}}(t_{j}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}),\ket{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\big)={}_{\textup{L}}\!\braket{j,l|\rho_{\textup{L}}(t_{j}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})|j,l}_{\textup{L}}

(H.34)

\displaystyle=\frac{\big\|(\ket{j,l}\!\!\bra{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}\textup{P}})\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}^{2}}{\big\|\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}^{2}}

(H.35)

where we proceeded analogously to in
eq.

H.22
. To continue, we now employ
eq.

H.30
with
\hat{p}
the identity operator on
S
C
P
to bound the denominator, while we apply the triangle inequality to bound the numerator:

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\big\|(\ket{j,l}\!\!\bra{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}\textup{P}})\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}+\big\|(\ket{j,l}\!\!\bra{j,l}_{\textup{S}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{[\textup{S}\backslash\textup{L}]\textup{C}\textup{P}})\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}}{1-\big\|\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}}

(H.36)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{P(\xi_{j}|t_{j})}+\big\|\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}}{1-\big\|\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}}.

(H.37)

Hence

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-P(\xi_{j}|t_{j})}\leq\sqrt{1-\left(\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}}-(1+\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}})\big\|\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}\right)^{2}}

(H.38)

for
\big\|\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{j}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big\|_{2}\leq 1
(since this guarantees that the argument of the square-root is non-negative). We now proceed to bound
\|\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\|_{2}
using
Lemma

C.4
. Using the notation in
Lemma

C.4
, we set
\ket{\Phi_{0}}=\ket{\rho(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}
,
\ket{\Phi_{m}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(t/n)G}\ket{\Phi_{m-1}}
,
\ket{\Phi_{m}}=\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(t/n)G}\right)^{m}\ket{\Phi_{0}}=\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{m}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}
,
t_{m}=m(t/n)
,
\Delta_{k}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(t/n)H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\|_{2}=\|\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}-\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\|_{2}=\|\ket{\Phi_{n}}-\Delta_{n}\Delta_{n-1}\ldots\Delta_{1}\ket{\Phi_{0}}\|_{2}

(H.39)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=1}^{n}\|\ket{\Phi_{m}}-\Delta_{m}\ket{\Phi_{m-1}}\|_{2}=\sum_{m=1}^{n}\|\left[{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}-\mathrm{i}(t/n)(H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}-\mathrm{i}V_{\textup{C}})-{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}+\mathrm{i}(t/n)H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}+\mathcal{O}(t/n)^{2}\right]\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{m}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\|_{2}

(H.40)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=1}^{n}\left((t/n)\|V_{\textup{C}}\ket{\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{m}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\|_{2}+\mathcal{O}(t/n)^{2}\right)=\left(\sum_{m=1}^{n}(t/n)\sqrt{\textup{tr}[V_{\textup{C}}^{2}\,\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t_{m}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]}\right)+\mathcal{O}(t^{2}/n).

(H.41)

Therefore, taking the limit
n\to\infty
on both sites of the above we obtain a Riemann integral on the r.h.s.:

\displaystyle\|\ket{\delta\rho\bm{(\!\!\!(}t\bm{)\!\!\!)}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\|_{2}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}x\,\sqrt{\textup{tr}[V_{\textup{C}}^{2}\,\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}x+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}x\,\|\vec{V}_{\textup{C}}\|_{w,2}(x,l)=\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(t),

(H.42)

where in the last line we have written the integrand in terms of the weighted 2-norm defined in
eq.

H.2
. Thus, for

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(t_{j+1})\in[0,1]

(H.43)

from
eqs.

H.33
and
H.38
we have the bounds

\displaystyle\sqrt{P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1})}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}(2-{\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}})}+\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(t_{j+1}),

(H.44)

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-P(\xi_{j}|t_{j})}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{1-\left(\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}}-(1+\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}})\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(t_{j+1})\right)^{2}}.

(H.45)

Since the dynamics of
\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}
is unitary time evolution via a time-independent Hamiltonian, we can take advantage of the formalism developed in
[
28
]
—similarly to how we did in
appendix

B
. However, we will have to set up the measurement strategy differently this time. In particular, we will use the fact that our signal estimate
t_{\textup{est}}(\xi_{j})
in
eq.

H.16
, is degenerate. We start by evaluating the mean squared error of our signal.

\displaystyle\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}P(\xi_{k}|t)(t_{\text{est}}(\xi_{k})-t)^{2}

(H.46)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P(\xi_{j}|t)(t_{j}+\Delta\,t_{1}-t)^{2}+P(\xi_{k}|t)((j-\Delta+1)t_{1}-t)^{2}

(H.47)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(P(\xi_{j}|t)\left[(j+\Delta-\frac{t}{t_{1}})^{2}-(j-\Delta+1-\frac{t}{t_{1}})^{2}\right]+(j-\Delta+1-\frac{t}{t_{1}})^{2}\right)t_{1}

(H.48)

where in the penultimate line, we have used
\sum_{k}P(\xi_{k}|t)=1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t_{j})^{2}}}

\displaystyle=t_{1}\sqrt{(1-P(\xi_{j}|t_{j}))[(1-\Delta)^{2}-\Delta^{2}]+\Delta^{2}},

(H.49)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t_{j+1})^{2}}}

\displaystyle=t_{1}\sqrt{P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1})[(1-\Delta)^{2}-\Delta^{2}]+\Delta^{2}}.

(H.50)

Analogously to how we did in
appendix

B
, we now evaluate the criteria
eq.

B.5
for times
t_{j}
and
t_{j+1}
. Namely, we want to find solutions to the parameters such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}1)&\qquad\sqrt{\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t_{j})^{2}}}>0,\qquad\sqrt{\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t_{j+1})^{2}}}>0\\
2)&\qquad|t_{j}-t_{j+1}|=(\lambda+1)\left(\sqrt{\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t_{j})^{2}}}+\sqrt{\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t_{j+1})^{2}}}\right)\end{split}

(H.51)

where
\lambda=4.64
, holds. This will yield the criterion for being in the isentropic time interval. We start with criterion 2), which, using
eqs.

H.49
and
H.50
, holds iff:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\lambda+1)}=\sqrt{(1-P(\xi_{j}|t_{j}))[(1-\Delta)^{2}-\Delta^{2}]+\Delta^{2}}+\sqrt{P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1})[(1-\Delta)^{2}-\Delta^{2}]+\Delta^{2}}.

(H.52)

Now observer that this is continuous in
\Delta\in[0,1/2]
for all
P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1}),P(\xi_{j}|t_{j})\in[0,1]
. Furthermore, at
\Delta=1/2
, we have the the r.h.s. of
eq.

H.52
is 1 for all
P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1}),P(\xi_{j}|t_{j})\in[0,1/2]
. This is greater than the l.h.s. of
eq.

H.52
, since
1/(\lambda+1)\approx hhh
. At
\Delta=0
, the r.h.s. of
eq.

H.52
is

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-P(\xi_{j}|t_{j})}+\sqrt{P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1})}.

(H.53)

Therefore, due to continuity in
\Delta\in(0,1/2]
for all
P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1}),P(\xi_{j}|t_{j})\in[0,1]
, if

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda+1}>\sqrt{1-P(\xi_{j}|t_{j})}+\sqrt{P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1})},

(H.54)

there exists
\Delta\in(0,1/2)
such that
eq.

H.52
holds. Hence
eq.

H.54
gives a sufficient condition for 2) in
eq.

H.51
to hold. Moreover, from
eqs.

H.49
and
H.50
we see that condition 1) also holds for all
P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1}),P(\xi_{j}|t_{j})\in[0,1]
if
\Delta\in(0,1/2)
.
Consider the condition

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda+1}>\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}(2-{\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}})}+\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(t_{j+1})+\sqrt{1-\left(\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}}-(1+\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}})\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(t_{j+1})\right)^{2}}.

(H.55)

Due to
eq.

H.45
, condition
eq.

H.55
implies
eq.

H.54
and hence it also implies conditions 1) and 2) in
eq.

H.51
.
Moreover, since

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}(2-{\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}})}+\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(t_{j+1})+\sqrt{1-\left(\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}}-(1+\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}})\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(t_{j+1})\right)^{2}}

(H.56)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}(2-{\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}})+2\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(t_{j+1})\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}}},

(H.57)

we arrive at condition
eq.

H.8
by noting that the r.h.s. is a non-increasing function of
t_{j+1}
.
The conditions
eq.

B.5
in our case are conditions
eq.

H.51
, [which we have shown to be satisfied for
t_{j+1}\in[0,t_{\max,l})
], it thus follows from
[
28
]
that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t_{j+1})^{2}}}\geq\frac{\kappa}{\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j+1})}\!\!\bra{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j+1})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}]},

(H.58)

for
t_{j+1}\in[0,t_{\max,l})
, where
\kappa
is a numerical constant
\kappa\approx 0.091
. (Recall that
H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
has ground state energy equal to zero by definition, and hence the ground state does not appear on the r.h.s. of
eq.

H.58
.) Using
eq.

H.50
and noting that
P(\xi_{j}|t_{j+1})\in[0,1]
,
\Delta\in(0,1/2]
, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t_{j+1})^{2}}}\leq t_{1}.

(H.60)

Thus

\displaystyle t_{1}\geq\sqrt{\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t_{j+1})^{2}}}\geq\frac{\kappa}{\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j+1})}\!\!\bra{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j+1})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}]}=\frac{\kappa}{\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}{\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})}]},

(H.61)

from which
eq.

H.15
follows.

We now proceed to prove
eq.

H.14
. Recall that we have defined
c_{0}
be the numerical constant defined in
[
47
]
. Similarly to how we proceeded in the proof in
appendix

B
, one can repeat the above calculations for the dimensionless version of the time operator
M
in
eq.

H.16
, namely
M/T_{0}
, which has a root mean squared error which differs by a factor of
1/T_{0}
. Defining the dimensionless Hamiltonian via
\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}:=T_{0}H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
, it thus follows from
[
47
]
that

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t_{j+1})^{2}}}}{T_{0}}\geq\frac{c_{0}}{\sqrt{\textup{tr}\big[\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j+1})}\!\!\bra{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j+1})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big]}},

(H.62)

for
t_{j+1}\in[0,t_{\max,l})
and the states
\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}
and
\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j+1})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}
are not squeezed. (Note that using dimensionless time and Hamiltonian operators does not modify the definition of
\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j+1})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}
in the above equation since the factors of
T_{0}
in the dimensionless time
t/T_{0}
and dimensionless Hamiltonian
\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
cancel each other out in its definition, namely
eq.

H.25
.) Here, a state
\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{k})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}
,
k\in{j,j+1}
being not squeezed means it is a eigenstate of
\left(\bar{M}\lambda+\mathrm{i}\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\right)\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{P}}
, with
|\lambda|=1
and where
M
is given by
eq.

H.16
. Now, observe that

\displaystyle(\lambda\bar{M}^{(j,l)}_{\textup{L}}+\mathrm{i}\,\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}})-(\lambda\bar{M}+\mathrm{i}\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}})=t_{\text{est}}(\xi_{j}){\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}.

(H.63)

As such, the set of eigenvectors of
(\lambda\bar{M}^{(j,l)}_{\textup{L}}+\mathrm{i}\,\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}})\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{P}}
and
\left(\lambda\bar{M}+\mathrm{i}\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\right)\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}_{\textup{P}}
coincide. Therefore, by definition of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\cdot)
in
eq.

A.2
and since the proof holds for any purification
P
, it follows that all purifications
\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{k})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}
, are in
\mathcal{C}(\bar{M}_{\textup{L}}^{(k,l)},\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}})
for
k\in{j,j+1}
.
Finally, note that
\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{k})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}
is any purification of
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}+t_{k}|\tau_{l})
. Therefore, we have that
eq.

H.62
holds if
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}+t_{k}|\tau_{l})\in\mathcal{C}(\bar{M}_{\textup{L}}^{(k,l)},\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}})
for
k\in{j,j+1}
.

Finally, using
eqs.

H.50
and
H.60
, but now for the normalised time operator, it follows from
eq.

H.62
that

\displaystyle\frac{t_{1}}{T_{0}}\geq\frac{\sqrt{\braket{(t_{\text{est}}-t_{j+1})^{2}}}}{T_{0}}\geq\frac{c_{0}}{\sqrt{\textup{tr}\big[\bar{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\ket{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j+1})}\!\!\bra{\rho^{\textup{H}}(t_{j+1})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}\textup{P}}\big]}}=\frac{c_{0}}{\sqrt{T_{0}\textup{tr}\big[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\rho(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\big]}},

(H.64)

if
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\in\mathcal{C}^{\text{clas.,\,}l}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
. Rearranging
eq.

H.64
gives us
eq.

H.14
.
∎

Lemma H.3

(Mean-energy power-and-dissipation relationships)
.

The power and dissipation per cycle of a self-oscillator,
P^{\text{re}}
and
P^{\text{diss}}
respectively, are give by

\displaystyle P^{\text{in}}_{l}:=\frac{\braket{E^{\text{re}}}}{T_{0}}=\frac{\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})]}{T_{0}}+\frac{\delta E_{l}^{\text{re}}}{T_{0}},\quad P^{\text{diss}}_{l}:=\frac{\braket{E^{\text{no re}}}}{T_{0}}=\frac{\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})]}{T_{0}}-\frac{\delta E_{l}^{\text{no re}}}{T_{0}},

(H.65)

where

\displaystyle\delta E_{l}^{\text{re}}:=\mathrm{i}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\textup{d}s\,\textup{tr}\!\!\left[[\bar{H}_{\textup{S}},H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}]\,\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\right]\in{\mathbbm{R}},\quad\delta E_{l}^{\text{no re}}\!:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\textup{d}s\,P(s+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})\,\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]\geq 0,

(H.66)

with

\displaystyle\bar{H}_{\textup{S}}:=\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}],

(H.67)

and

\displaystyle\left|\delta E^{\text{re}}_{l}\right|\leq\frac{2}{T_{0}}\,\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}.

(H.68)

Proof.

We start by finding a representation for the map
D^{\text{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)
which can be assumed w.l.o.g. To start with, all completely positive and trace non-increasing maps which have a single output
\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}
, can be written as
D^{\text{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)=\textup{tr}[M\cdot]\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}
, where
M
is positive semi-definite matrix on
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{C}}
. (To see this, note that
D^{\text{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)
can be written in the form
p(\cdot)\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}
with
p(\cdot)\in[0,1]
by definition. Now equate to the Kraus form of any completely positive and trace non-increasing map, followed by taking the trace on both sides of the equality.) Thus using
eq.

F.3
, we require

\displaystyle\sum_{k}J_{\textup{C}}^{k}\rho_{\textup{C}}{J_{\textup{C}}^{k}}^{\dagger}=\textup{tr}[M\rho_{\textup{C}}]\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}.

(H.69)

Hence by taking the trace on both sides

\displaystyle\textup{tr}\left[\left(\sum_{k}{J_{\textup{C}}^{k}}^{\dagger}J_{\textup{C}}^{k}\right)\rho_{\textup{C}}\right]=\textup{tr}[M\rho_{\textup{C}}],

(H.70)

for all
\rho_{\textup{C}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{C}})
. Taking into account that both
M
and
\sum_{k}{J_{\textup{C}}^{k}}^{\dagger}J_{\textup{C}}^{k}
are Hermitian, and using the fact that it must hold for all
\rho_{\textup{C}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H}_{\textup{C}})
, we conclude that
\sum_{k}{J_{\textup{C}}^{k}}^{\dagger}J_{\textup{C}}^{k}=M
. (To see this, note that the trace forms an inner product for the real vector space of Hermitian matrices. As such, we can expand
M
and
\sum_{k}{J_{\textup{C}}^{k}}^{\dagger}J_{\textup{C}}^{k}
in an orthonormal basis in said space, and choose
\rho_{\textup{C}}
as the basis elements. The orthogonality then implies that the real expansion coefficients for
M
and
\sum_{k}{J_{\textup{C}}^{k}}^{\dagger}J_{\textup{C}}^{k}
must be the same.) We therefore have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\text{re}}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)=2\,\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}[V_{\textup{C}}\,\cdot]\,\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}.

(H.71)

Finally, it is necessary to check that there does indeed exist a set of operators
\{J_{C}^{k}\}_{k}
which realise
eq.

H.71
. (We will not need to prove uniqueness of the representation, since we will not use it in the proofs). For this task, let one write
\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}
and
V_{\textup{C}}
in diagonal form:
\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}=\sum_{q}p_{q}^{0}\ket{\rho_{q}^{0}}\!\!\bra{\rho_{q}^{0}}_{\textup{C}}
,
p^{0}_{q}\geq 0
,
\{\ket{\rho_{q}^{0}}_{\textup{C}}\}_{k}
an orthonormal basis, and similarly,
V_{\textup{C}}=\sum_{l}c_{l}\ket{l}\!\!\bra{l}_{\textup{C}}
,
c_{l}\geq 0
,
\{\ket{l}_{\textup{C}}\}_{k}
orthonormal basis. We can then define

\displaystyle J^{q,l}_{\textup{C}}=\sqrt{p_{q}^{0}\,c_{l}}\ket{\rho_{q}^{0}}\!\!\bra{l}_{\textup{C}},

(H.72)

where we have multi-indexed
k
via
q,l
(i.e. summing over
k
is equivalent to summing over both
q
and
l
independently). One can readily check that
\sum_{q,l}J^{q,l}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot){J^{q,l}_{\textup{C}}}^{\dagger}
is equal to
eq.

H.71
. We now proceed with the proof.

The first equation in
eq.

F.5
, is defined via the solution to

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}=\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\right)=-\mathrm{i}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}},\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]-\{V_{\textup{C}},\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\}.

(H.73)

Therefore,
\frac{d}{dt}\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]=-2\textup{tr}[V_{\textup{C}}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]
. Hence, from
eqs.

F.8
and
H.71
, it follows

\displaystyle P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})=2\textup{tr}[V_{\textup{C}}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]=-\frac{d}{dt}\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}].

(H.74)

For
P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})
to be a well defined probability distribution, it must be that the probability of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
renewal occurring at some point in time is one, thus,

\displaystyle 1=\int_{0}^{\infty}\textup{d}t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})=\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})]-\lim_{t\to\infty}\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]=1-\lim_{t\to\infty}\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}].

(H.75)

Therefore,
\lim_{t\to\infty}\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]=0
. Now, let us write
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}
in diagonalised form:
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}=\sum_{j}P_{j}(t,\tau_{l})\ket{(t,\tau_{l})}\!\!\bra{(t,\tau_{l})}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
. We thus have
\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{j}(t,\tau_{l})=0
for all
j
,
l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
. Hence it follows that for all linear operators
O_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\textup{tr}[O_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]=0,\quad l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}.

(H.76)

\displaystyle\braket{E^{\text{re}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\,\textup{tr}\!\!\left[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,2\,\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}[V_{\textup{C}}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]\otimes\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}\right]

(H.77)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\,\frac{d}{ds}\textup{tr}\!\!\left[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]\otimes\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}\right]-\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\,\textup{tr}\!\left[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}\big[[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}},\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]\big]\otimes\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}\right]

(H.78)

\displaystyle=\textup{tr}\!\left[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\right]-\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\,\textup{tr}\!\left[\bar{H}_{\textup{S}}\,[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}},\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]\right],

(H.79)

where in line
H.77
we have used
eq.

H.71
, while in line
H.78
, we have used
eq.

H.73
. In line
H.79
, we have used that
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})=\rho_{\textup{S}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\otimes\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}
and
eq.

H.76
. Finally, to conclude the proof of the expression for
\braket{E^{\text{re}}}
in
eq.

H.65
, now follows by noting that commutator in line
H.79
can be moved to the first two terms due to the cyclicity of the trace. We now derive the expression for
\braket{E^{\text{no re}}}
.

\displaystyle\braket{E^{\text{no re}}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,P({t+\tau_{l}},+1|\tau_{l})\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\text{no re}}(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)})]

(H.80)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,P({t+\tau_{l}},+1|\tau_{l})\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\frac{d}{ds}\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]

(H.81)

\displaystyle=\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})]-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,P({t+\tau_{l}},+1|\tau_{l})\,\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}],

(H.82)

where in line
H.81
, we have used
eq.

H.73
and the fact that
\textup{tr}\big[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}},\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]\big]=0
. And finally, in line
H.82
, we have used
eq.

H.75
.
We now calculate the bound on
\delta E_{l}^{\text{re}}
. First note that for any (normalised or sub-normalised) density matrix
{\bm{\rho}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\textup{tr}\!\left[[\bar{H}_{\textup{S}},H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}]\bm{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\textup{tr}\!\left[[\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}},\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}]\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\right]\right|\textup{tr}[\bm{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}]\leq 2\sqrt{\textup{tr}[\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}}^{2}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}]\textup{tr}[\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{2}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}]}\,\textup{tr}[\bm{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}]

(H.83)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\|\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}}^{2}\|_{1}\|\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{2}\|_{1}}\,\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}]=2\sqrt{\|\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}}^{2}\|_{1}\|\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{2}\|_{1}}\,\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}]

(H.84)

\displaystyle=2\|\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}}\|_{F}\|\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\|_{F}\,\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}]

(H.85)

where in line
H.83
we defined
\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}}:=\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}[\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}]
, (where
\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
is defined in
eq.

H.13
); defined the normalised state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}:=\bm{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}/\textup{tr}[\bm{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}]
, and used Cauchy-Schwartz inequality twice. In lines
H.84
and
H.85
we have used the definitions of the one-norm and Frobenius norm, followed by definition
H.13
.
Now observer that from
eqs.

H.74
and
H.75
we have that

\displaystyle\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}x+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]=\int_{x}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}tP(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l}).

(H.86)

Thus

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}x\,\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}x+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]=\left(\lim_{x\to\infty}x\int_{x}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}tP(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})\right)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l}),

(H.87)

where we have performed integration by parts. From
eq.

H.12
it follows that
\lim_{x\to\infty}F(1,x)\in{\mathbbm{R}}
, where
F(1,x)
is such that
dF(1,x)/dx=xP(x+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})
. We thus have,

\displaystyle 0\leq\lim_{x\to\infty}x\int_{x}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}tP(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})\leq\lim_{x\to\infty}\int_{x}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})=\lim_{x\to\infty}\left(\left[\lim_{y\to\infty}F(1,y)\right]-F(1,x)\right)=0.

(H.88)

Hence

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}x\,\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}x+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})=\mathbb{M}_{l}(1).

(H.89)

We thus conclude
eq.

H.68
from
eqs.

H.85
and
H.89
and definition
H.13
.
∎

We now state and prove
Theorem

5
, which is straightforward to prove given the lemmas developed in this section:

See
5

Proof.

Follows straightforwardly by combining
Lemmas

H.3
and
H.2
.
∎

H.3
Generalisation of
Theorem

5
to more general semi-classical states

We now generalise
Theorem

5
to the case where
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\notin\mathcal{C}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{clas.,l}
, but
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
is a probabilistic mixture of semi-classical states. Namely to the case where for some
l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
, there exists,

\displaystyle\Big\{\ \rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\in\mathcal{C}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{clas.,l}\Big\}_{m}\quad\text{and}\quad\Big\{\,p_{m}^{{l}}\,\Big|\,p_{m}^{{l}}>0,\,\sum_{m}p_{m}^{{l}}=1\Big\}

(H.90)

such that

\displaystyle\sum_{m}p_{m}^{{l}}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})=\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}).

(H.91)

Recall that the states
\{\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\}_{l}
are provided by the dynamics in
eqs.

F.5
and
F.7
. It thus follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{m}p_{m}^{{l}}(t)\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})=\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}),

(H.92)

where

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}):=\frac{\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}}{\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]},\qquad\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}:=\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})\right).

(H.93)

and

\displaystyle p_{m}^{{l}}(t):=\frac{\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]}{\sum_{n}p_{n}^{{l}}\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(n)}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]}p_{m}^{{l}}.

(H.94)

Before stating the generalization of
Theorem

5
, we need to introduced a few definitions. These are essentially previous definitions but specialised to the states
\tilde{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}
in the mixture. These are:

Power consumption from
\tilde{\rho}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}
. We evaluate
eq.

F.18
on
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}
rather than
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}

\displaystyle P^{\textup{in}{(m)}}_{l}:=\frac{1}{T_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\,\textup{tr}[H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}^{\text{re}}(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}\bm{(\!\!\!(}s+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)})].

(H.95)

We also need to specialise the definition of
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
isentropic time interval,
[\tau_{l},t_{\max,l}+\tau_{l})
, since now
t_{\max,l}
will depend on
m
, since said interval depends on how the states
\rho^{(m)}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
evolve. In particular, it is defined as follows: for a given
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}
, the quantity
t_{\max,l}^{(m)}\geq 0
is the largest constant such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4(1+\lambda)^{2}}=\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}(2-{\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}})+2\varepsilon^{\textup{quality},(m)}_{l}(t_{\max,l}^{(m)})\,\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}}

(H.96)

where
\varepsilon^{\textup{quality},(m)}_{l}(\cdot)
is defined by

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\textup{quality},(m)}_{l}(\tau)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathrm{d}t\,\|\vec{V}_{\textup{C}}\|_{w,2}^{(m)}(t,l),

(H.97)

\displaystyle\|\vec{V}_{\textup{C}}\|_{w,2}^{(m)}(t,l)

\displaystyle:={\left(\sum_{j}v_{j}^{2}p_{j}^{(m)}(t,l)\!\right)\!}^{1/2},\qquad p_{j}^{(m)}(t,l):=\braket{v_{j}|\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}|v_{j}}.

(H.99)

Since we are considering the dynamics of states during the
l
isentropic time interval,
[\tau_{l},t_{\max,l}^{(m)}+\tau_{l})
, instead of
[\tau_{l},t_{\max,l}+\tau_{l})
, different frequencies may be achievable. As such, we must define the gate frequency to depend on
m
. To do so, we still demand that
eq.

H.5
holds, but now
h_{l}
will be
m
dependent:
h_{l}^{(m)}:=\max\{j\in{\mathbbm{Z}_{>0}}\,|\,t_{\max,l}^{(m)}-jt_{1}^{(m)}>0\}
. As before, we assume that the gate frequency of our computer is the same for all gates it can implement. Furthermore, we quantify our tolerance to error in terms of trace distance
T
from
\rho_{\textup{L}}^{(m)}(t_{j}^{(m)}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
(instead of
\rho_{\textup{L}}(t_{j}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
as before), namely by demanding

\displaystyle T\big(\rho_{\textup{L}}^{(m)}(t_{j}^{(m)}+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}),\ket{j,l}_{\textup{L}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}\in[0,1/2),

(H.100)

where
t_{j}^{(m)}=jt_{1}^{(m)}\in(0,t_{\max,l}^{(m)})
,
j\in{\mathbbm{Z}_{>0}}
and

\displaystyle\rho_{\textup{L}}^{(m)}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})=\textup{tr}_{\textup{[S$\backslash$L]}\textup{C}}[\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})].

(H.101)

Note that we have also made the time interval between gates
m
-dependent, (denoted
t_{1}^{(m)}
). This is necessary because that times for which
eq.

H.100
can be satisfied may differ now that the state itself is
m
-dependent. Crucially though, this
m
-dependency of the state dynamics enters through the state at the beginning of each cycle, as opposed to the generator of the conditional dynamics, namely
\mathcal{L}^{\textup{no re}}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
. (These properties are due to
eq.

H.93
.)

Let us thus denote by
f^{(m)}
the gate frequency at which gates are applied during the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
isentropic time interval
[\tau_{l},t_{\max,l}+\tau_{l}]
. It is defined as

\displaystyle f^{(m)}:=1/t_{1}^{(m)}.

(H.102)

We also have to define the
m
dependent counterpart to
\varepsilon^{0}_{\textup{H}}
. For this, we define the
m
dependent upper bound on the 1st moment in analogy to
eq.

H.103
. We assume there exists
\mathbb{M}^{(m)}(1)<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t\,P^{(m)}(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})\leq\mathbb{M}^{(m)}(1)

(H.103)

for all
l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
, where (in analogy with
eq.

F.8
)

\displaystyle P^{(m)}(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l}):=\textup{tr}\left[\mathcal{D}^{\text{re}}_{\textup{C}}\left(\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}\right)\right].

(H.104)

With this quantity, we can define
\varepsilon^{0,(m)}_{\textup{H}}
analogously to
eq.

H.13
:
\varepsilon^{0,(m)}_{\textup{H}}
is any real number which upper bounds the l.h.s. of

\displaystyle{\left\|\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}[\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}]\right\|_{F}}{\|\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\|_{F}}2\mathbb{M}(1)T_{0}\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0,(m)},\quad\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}:=H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}-H_{\textup{C}},

(H.105)

where
\|\cdot\|_{F}
is the Frobenius norm.

Finally, note that we could also let the upper bound in
eq.

H.100
, namely
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}
, be
m
dependent, and the following theorem would hold equally well. We refrain from doing so simply to avoid extra complication.

The following theorem shows that the results of
Theorem

5
apply individually to each state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
in the mixture of states comprising
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
. Since Case 2) in
Theorem

5
already applies to all states
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
, we will only state the generalization for case 1) in
Theorem

5
.

Theorem H.1

(Generalization of
Theorem

5
to more general semi-classical states)
.

Consider a state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
in the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
isentropic time interval which is a mixture of semi-classical states, namely satisfies
eq.

H.91
,
(
l\in{\mathbbm{N}_{\geq 0}}
)
. Consider the dynamics of any state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
during its
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
isentropic time interval, namely
[\tau_{l},t_{\max,l}+\tau_{l})
. The gate frequency
f^{(m)}
during said regime is upper bounded for all
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}\in[0,1/2]
as follows:

\displaystyle f^{(m)}\leq\frac{\sqrt{T_{0}^{2}P^{\textup{in}{(m)}}_{l}+\varepsilon^{0,(m)}_{\textup{H}}}}{(\lambda+1)c_{0}T_{0}}.

(H.106)

Here
\lambda>0
,
c_{0}>0
are numerical constants.

Proof.

It follows identically to that of
Theorem

5
. One can check this by going though the proof step-by-step, adding the label
m
to the states etc where appropriate. At a higher level, one can appreciate why the proof still works, by noting that the gate frequency, for a given
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}
, is fully determined by applying the dynamical map
\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{\textup{no re}}}
to a normalised quantum state on
S
C
. In the case of
Theorem

5
, this state is
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
. In the case of
Theorem

H.1
, it is
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{(m)}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
. The quantities defined in
Section

H.3
, are analogous to those required to make the theorem statement on
Theorem

5
well-defined, but with the additional label
m
. Thus, the proof follows analogously.
∎

Let us now comment on how the gate frequency and power of the total dynamics relate to
\{f^{(m)}\}_{m}
and
\{P_{l}^{\textup{in},(m)}\}_{m}
respectively in the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle.

Observe from
eq.

F.18
that the total power,
P_{l}^{\textup{in}}
, is linear in the state.
Thus from
eqs.

H.91
and
H.95
, it follows that the total power per cycle is a probabilistic mixture of
P_{l}^{\textup{in}}
, namely

\displaystyle P_{l}^{\textup{in}}=\sum_{m}p_{m}^{l}P_{l}^{\textup{in},{(m)}}.

(H.107)

Since the state is a probabilistic mixture of states all with (potentially) different gate frequencies
f^{(m)}
, the gate frequency associated with the dynamics of the state
\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
in this case is not well-defined. However, we can study upper bound the average gate frequency of the mixture when the conditions of
Theorem

H.1
apply, we find

\displaystyle f^{\textup{ave}}:=\sum_{m}p^{l}_{m}f^{(m)}\leq\sum_{m}p^{l}_{m}\frac{\sqrt{T_{0}^{2}P^{\textup{in}{(m)}}_{l}+\varepsilon^{0,(m)}_{\textup{H}}}}{(\lambda+1)c_{0}T_{0}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{\sum_{m}p_{m}^{l}\left(T_{0}^{2}P^{\textup{in}{(m)}}_{l}+\varepsilon^{0,(m)}_{\textup{H}}\right)}}{(\lambda+1)c_{0}T_{0}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{T_{0}^{2}P^{\textup{in}}_{l}+\bar{\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}}}}{(\lambda+1)c_{0}T_{0}},

(H.108)

where in the penultimate line we used Jensen’s inequality, and we have defined the average instantaneous initial-cycle-state parameter error,

\displaystyle\bar{\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}}:=\sum_{m}p_{m}^{l}\varepsilon^{0,(m)}_{\textup{H}}.

(H.109)

Therefore, from
eq.

H.108
, we see that when the state at the begging of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
cycle is a mixture of semi-classical states in
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}^{clas.,l}
, that the average gate frequency achieved by the mixture still scales as the square-root of the power consumed in said cycle.

H.4
Proof of
Lemma

H.1

See
H.1

Proof.

The first and last equalities follow trivially from the definition
H.1
. The
1{{}^{\text{st}}}
inequality follows from Jensen’s inequality for the concave function
\sqrt{(\cdot)}
. For the second inequality, we note that the weighted one-norm and the weighted 2-norm can both be written in the from

\displaystyle\|\vec{V}_{\textup{C}}\|_{w,1}(t,l)

\displaystyle=\textup{tr}[V_{\textup{C}}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}],

(H.110)

\displaystyle\|\vec{V}_{\textup{C}}\|_{w,2}(t,l)

\displaystyle=\left(\textup{tr}[V_{\textup{C}}^{2}\rho_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]\right)^{1/2}=\left(\textup{tr}[\rho^{1/2}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}V_{\textup{C}}^{3/2}V_{\textup{C}}^{1/2}\rho^{1/2}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]\right)^{1/2},

(H.111)

where we have used the positive semi-definiteness of
V_{\textup{C}}
and
\rho^{1/2}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}
. The last inequality now follows from two steps: first apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to the r.h.s. of
eq.

H.111
, and followed by noting the bound
\textup{tr}[V_{\textup{C}}^{3}\bm{\rho}]\leq\|V_{\textup{C}}^{3}\|\leq\|V_{\textup{C}}\|^{3}
holds for all normalised (or sub-normalised) positive semi-definite operators
\bm{\rho}
. Second, apply Jensen’s inequality to the integral, noting that
d\mu:=dt/\tau
is a probability density (the uniform one) for a probability measure on
[0,\tau]
.
∎

H.5
Bounds for the range of the self-oscillator’s isentropic time interval and instantaneous initial-cycle-state parameter for
Theorem

4

Here we calculate the length
t_{\max,l}
, of the
l{{}^{\text{th}}}
isentropic time interval,
[\tau_{l},\tau_{l}+t_{\max,l})
, and
\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}
for
Theorem

4
.

In this section, we will calculate the parameters
t_{\max,l}
and
\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}
for the quantum frequential computer which achieves the bounds in
Theorem

4
. By doing so, we show how the requirements of
Theorem

5
can be readily met.
See
1

The proof can be found at the end of this section, (and is reachable via this
​​hyperlink
). See below
Lemma

1
for technical comments. On a more technical note, while the interval
eq.

VI.3
readily does not depend on the value of
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}\in\big[0,\,1-\sqrt{1-1/(4(1+\lambda)^{2})}\,\big)
, its actual value within said interval will depend on
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}
(this necessity can be seen from
eq.

H.8
).

We will now bound the instantaneous initial-cycle-state parameter,
\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}
, introduced in
eq.

H.13
.
See
2

Recall that
{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(\cdot)
is defined in
eq.

III.7
.

Proof.

From the defining relation for
\varepsilon_{\textup{H}}^{0}
, namely
eq.

H.13
, we observe that we need to bound the quantities
\left\|\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}[\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\,\rho^{0}_{\textup{C}}]\right\|_{F}
,
\|\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\|_{F}
,
\mathbb{M}(1)
, where
\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}:=H_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}-H_{\textup{C}}
.
Recalling
eq.

G.1
, we find that in the case of
Theorem

4
, the Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}
is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{H^{\prime}}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}:=H^{\prime}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}-H_{\textup{C}}=\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}-1}I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)}\otimes I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}.

(H.112)

Furthermore, recall that the output state of the channel
\mathcal{D}_{\textup{C}}(\cdot)
, namely
\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}
, in the case of
Theorem

4
, is given by
\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}=\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{\textup{C}}=\ket{\Psi(0)}\!\!\bra{\Psi(0)}_{\textup{C}}
.
Thus

\displaystyle\left\|\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}\!\!\left[\tilde{H^{\prime}}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}\,\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}\right]\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\textup{tr}\left[\left(\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}\!\!\left[\tilde{H^{\prime}}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}\,\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}\right]\right)^{2}\right]}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{l,k=1}^{N_{g}-1}\left|\textup{tr}\left[I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)}I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(k)}\right]\textup{tr}[I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}]\textup{tr}[I_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}]\right|}

(H.113)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sum_{l,k=1}^{N_{g}-1}\sqrt{\textup{tr}\!\left[{I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)^{\textstyle{2}}}}\right]\textup{tr}\!\left[{I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(k)^{\textstyle{2}}}}\right]}\left|\textup{tr}[I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}]\textup{tr}[I_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}]\right|}

(H.114)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l,k=1}^{N_{g}-1}\sqrt{\textup{tr}\!\left[{I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)^{\textstyle{2}}}}\right]}\,\textup{tr}[I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}]\leq N_{g}\max_{l\in\{1,\ldots,N_{g}-1\}}\left\{\sqrt{\textup{tr}\!\left[{I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)^{\textstyle{2}}}}\right]}\left|\braket{\Psi(0)|I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}|\Phi(0)}_{\textup{C}}\right|\right\}

(H.115)

\displaystyle\leq f^{\prime}(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}^{\prime}(d)\,d^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}\sqrt{\max_{l\in\{1,\ldots,N_{g}-1\}}\left\{\textup{tr}\!\left[{I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)^{\textstyle{2}}}}\right]\right\}},

(H.116)

for all
\eta\in(0,1]
,
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
,
d\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
. In line
H.114
, we have used the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, and
eq.

C.144
in line
H.116
. Recall that the terms
\{I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)}\}_{l}
are finite-dimensional, have bound spectrum, and are
d
-independent. As such, the factor

\displaystyle\max_{l\in\{1,\ldots,N_{g}-1\}}\left\{\textup{tr}\!\left[{I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)^{\textstyle{2}}}}\right]\right\},

(H.117)

from the last line of the above inequality, has an upper bound which is
d
-independent even though
N_{g}
appearing in the set which
l
belongs to, is
d
dependent.
Thus recalling the linear relationship between
d
and
P^{\textup{in}}
, we find

\displaystyle\left\|\textup{tr}_{\textup{C}}\!\!\left[\tilde{H^{\prime}}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}\,\rho_{\textup{C}}^{0}\right]\right\|_{F}\leq f^{\prime}(\eta,\bar{\varepsilon})\,\textup{poly}^{\prime}(P^{\textup{in}})\,{P^{\textup{in}}}^{-3/\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}}}.

(H.118)

Thus to prove the lemma, the only remaining challenge is to prove that the terms
\|\tilde{H}_{\textup{S}\textup{C}}\|_{F}
,
\mathbb{M}(1)
grow at most polynomially in
d
. (Since recall that
d
is itself directly proportional to
P^{\textup{in}}
).

Similarly to above, we have that

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{H^{\prime}}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\textup{tr}\left[\tilde{H^{\prime}}_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}^{2}\right]}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{l,k=1}^{N_{g}-1}\left|\textup{tr}\left[I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)}I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(k)}\right]\textup{tr}[I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}I_{\textup{C}}^{(k)}]\right|}\leq\sum_{l=1}^{N_{g}-1}\sqrt{\textup{tr}\!\left[I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)^{\textstyle{2}}}\right]\textup{tr}\!\left[I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)^{\textstyle{2}}}\right]}

(H.119)

\displaystyle\leq N_{g}\sqrt{\max_{l=1,\ldots,N_{g}-1}\left\{\textup{tr}\!\left[I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)^{\textstyle{2}}}\right]\right\}\max_{l=1,\ldots,N_{g}-1}\left\{\textup{tr}\!\left[I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)^{\textstyle{2}}}\right]\right\}}

(H.120)

\displaystyle\leq N_{g}\sqrt{d\max_{l=1,\ldots,N_{g}-1}\left\{\textup{tr}\!\left[I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)^{\textstyle{2}}}\right]\right\}\max_{l=1,\ldots,N_{g}-1}\left\{\left({E.V.}_{\max}\,I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}\right)^{2}\right\}},

(H.121)

where
{E.V.}_{\max}\,I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}
is the maximum eigenvalue of the positive semi-definite operator
I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}
. In
eq.

H.135
an upper bound for all
l\in{1,\ldots,N_{g}}
and for all eigenvalues of
I_{\textup{C}}^{(l)}
was found which is independent of
l
. It was shown to grow at most polynomially in
d
(and thus in
P^{\textup{in}}
) for all
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
. The prefactor

\displaystyle\max_{l=1,\ldots,N_{g}-1}\left\{\textup{tr}\!\left[I_{{\textup{M}_{0}}\textup{L}\textup{W}}^{(l)^{\textstyle{2}}}\right]\right\}

(H.122)

is finite and
d
-independent, as reasoned around
eq.

H.117
.

All that is left, is to show that
\mathbb{M}(1)
, defined in
eq.

H.12
, grows sufficiently slowly such that the product of all three terms is still decaying with
P^{\textup{in}}
. By definition of
\mathbb{M}(1)
, we need to obtain an upper bound on the 1st moment of
P(t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})
which holds for all
l
. We proceed as follows

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{l}(1)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\textup{tr}[\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}\bm{(\!\!\!(}t+\tau_{l}|\tau_{l}\bm{)\!\!\!)}]

(H.123)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\textup{tr}[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}-tV_{\textup{C}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}-tV_{\textup{C}}}\rho_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}(\tau_{l}|\tau_{l})]

(H.124)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\left\|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}-tV_{\textup{C}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}-tV_{\textup{C}}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\left\|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}-tV_{\textup{C}}}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}-tV_{\textup{C}}}\right\|_{\infty}

(H.125)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\left\|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}\textup{C}}}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\mathrm{e}^{-tV_{\textup{C}}}\right\|_{\infty}^{2}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{e}^{-2tE.V._{\min}V_{\textup{C}}}

(H.126)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{e}^{-t\varepsilon_{b}/T_{0}}=\frac{T_{0}}{\varepsilon_{b}},

(H.127)

where in line
H.123
we have used
eq.

H.89
but now specialise the system to the case setup in
Theorem

4
, namely
\textup{S}=\textup{M}_{0}\textup{L}\textup{W}
. In line
H.124
, we have used
eq.

G.4
. In line
H.125
, we used the Schatten
p=\infty
norm. In line
H.126
, we have used the Golden-Thompson inequality for the operator norm
[
7
]
and defined
E.V._{\min}V_{\textup{C}}
to be the minimum eigenvalue of the positive semi-definite operator
V_{\textup{C}}
. In line
H.127
, we have used
eq.

G.6
and the fact that
\gamma_{0}I_{\textup{C}}^{N_{g}}\geq 0
.

The upper bound on
\mathbb{M}_{l}(1)
is
l
-independent and holds for all
l
. It thus constitutes an upper bound on
\mathbb{M}(1)
too, by definition of
\mathbb{M}(1)
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}(1)\leq\frac{T_{0}}{\varepsilon_{b}}.

(H.128)

Now, using
eqs.

G.74
and
G.90
we thus find

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}(1)\leq\frac{T_{0}}{\left(\sum_{q=1}^{N_{g}}\tilde{d}(\mathbb{m}_{l,q})\right)}\,E_{0}^{1/(2\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}})}\leq T_{0}\,E_{0}^{1/(2\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}})}=T_{0}\left(P^{\textup{in}}-\delta E^{\text{re}}_{1}/T_{0}\right)^{1/(2\sqrt{\bar{\varepsilon}})}.

(H.129)

Thus recalling that
|\delta E^{\text{re}}_{1}|
is a decaying function for all
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
(see
eq.

G.89
).

This from
eqs.

H.118
,
H.121
and
H.129
we conclude
eq.

VI.4
.
∎

We now prove
Lemma

1
:

Proof.

Due to the data processing inequality (with the partial trace as the data processing channel), it follows from
eq.

V.10
in
Theorem

4
, that the gate error
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}
(defined in
eq.

H.6
) in the case of
Theorem

4
converges to zero as
P^{\textup{in}}\to\infty
.

Clearly,
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}\to 0
as
P^{\textup{in}}\to\infty
implies
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}\in\big[0,\,1-\sqrt{1-1/(4(1+\lambda)^{2})}\,\big)
as
P^{\textup{in}}\to\infty
(since recall that by definition
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}\geq 0
and
\lambda>0
is a dimensionless constant). Therefore, in the following proof, we will show that
\varepsilon^{0}_{\textup{H}}
satisfies the lemma statement (i.e.
eq.

VI.3
) for all
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}\in\big[0,\,1-\sqrt{1-1/(4(1+\lambda)^{2})}\,\big)
.

Note that the upper limit of the interval for
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}
, namely
1-\sqrt{1-1/(4(1+\lambda)^{2})}
, is the solution to
eq.

H.8
when
\varepsilon_{l}^{\textup{quality}}(t_{\max,l})=0
. Furthermore, recall that
\varepsilon_{l}^{\textup{quality}}(t_{\max,l})\geq 0
is a monotonically increasing function of
t_{\max,l}
. Therefore, note that a value of
\varepsilon_{l}^{\textup{quality}}(t_{\max,l})
which tends to zero (as
P^{\textup{in}}\to\infty
) is a solution to
eq.

H.8
(for sufficiently large
P^{\textup{in}}
and a value of
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}
which tends to
1-\sqrt{1-1/(4(1+\lambda)^{2})}
as
P^{\textup{in}}\to\infty
). Thus, for every
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}\in\big[0,\,1-\sqrt{1-1/(4(1+\lambda)^{2})}\,\big)
, a lower bound, denoted
\tau^{*}
, on
t_{\max,l}
in the large
P^{\textup{in}}
limit, can be found by finding a value of
\tau^{*}
such that
\varepsilon_{l}^{\textup{quality}}(\tau^{*})\to 0
as
P^{\textup{in}}\to\infty
.

Likewise, an upper bound, denoted
\tau^{**}
, on
t_{\max,l}
can be found for all
\varepsilon^{\textup{gate}}\in\big[0,\,1-\sqrt{1-1/(4(1+\lambda)^{2})}\,\big)
in the large
P^{\textup{in}}
limit, by finding a value
\tau^{**}
such that
\varepsilon_{l}^{\textup{quality}}(t_{\max,l})\geq 1
as
P^{\textup{in}}\to\infty
. This follows from the same continuity and monotonicity arguments as above in addition to noting that
eq.

H.8
has no solution for
\varepsilon_{l}^{\textup{quality}}(t_{\max,l})\geq 1/4
.

We start by proving the upper bound. For
\tau^{**}=T_{0}
, we have, using
Lemmas

H.1
and
V.9

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{l}^{\textup{quality}}(T_{0})\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T_{0}}\mathrm{d}t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{T_{0}-t_{1}}^{T_{0}}\mathrm{d}t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})=\frac{1-\varepsilon_{r}}{2},

(H.130)

where
\varepsilon_{r}
is given by
eq.

V.9
in
Theorem

4
, and decays super-polynomially [i.e. it belongs to the previously-defined in
eq.

III.7
class of functions
{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(P^{\textup{in}})
]. This thus proves the upper bound in
eq.

VI.3
.

For the lower bound, we choose
\tau^{*}=T_{0}-t_{1}
and use
Lemmas

H.1
and
V.9
to show

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(T_{0}-t_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left(\|V_{\textup{C}}\|\,(T_{0}-t_{1})\right)^{3/4}}{2^{1/4}}\left(\int_{0}^{T_{0}-t_{1}}\mathrm{d}t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})\right)^{1/4}

(H.131)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left(\|V_{\textup{C}}\|\,T_{0}\right)^{3/4}}{2^{1/4}}\left(1-\int_{T_{0}-t_{1}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})\right)^{1/4}\leq\frac{\left(\|V_{\textup{C}}\|\,T_{0}\right)^{3/4}}{2^{1/4}}\left(1-\int_{T_{0}-t_{1}}^{T_{0}}\mathrm{d}t\,P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})\right)^{1/4}

(H.132)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\|V_{\textup{C}}\|\,T_{0}\right)^{3/4}}{2^{1/4}}\left(\varepsilon_{r}\right)^{1/4}

(H.133)

where in line
H.132
we have used the fact that the integration of
P(t+\tau_{l},+1|\tau_{l})
over
{\mathbbm{R}_{\geq 0}}
is one due to normalization of the probability distribution.

We now need to upper bound
\|V_{\textup{C}}\|
to show that it does not grow too quickly with
P^{\textup{in}}
. Recall that
P^{\textup{in}}
is proportional to
d
in the case of the quantum frequential computer in the proof of
Theorem

4
. Therefore, it suffices to upper bound how it scales with
d
for large
d
. The operator norm of a positive semi-definite matrices is give by the largest eigenvalue of said matrix. We now find an upper bound which holds for all the eigenvalues of
V_{\textup{C}}
and hence also for its largest. From
eq.

V.9
and definitions
eqs.

G.11
,
G.11
and
G.17
, we find that the
j{{}^{\text{th}}}
eigenvalue of
V_{\textup{C}}\geq 0
is given by

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}\left[\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}\bar{V}_{0}\left(\frac{2\pi}{d}j\right)\Big|_{x_{0}x_{0}^{(l)}}\right]+\frac{\varepsilon_{b}}{2T_{0}},

(H.134)

j\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(k_{0})
, for
l=N_{g}
.
Let us first bound the term in square brackets for any
l
since it will be of independent use later: Thus for all
j\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(k_{0})
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}\bar{V}_{0}\left(\frac{2\pi}{d}j\right)\Big|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{(l)}}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}\left[\max_{x\in[-\pi,\pi]}\bar{V}_{0}\left(x\right)\Big|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{(l)}}\right]=\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}\left[\max_{x\in[-\pi,\pi]}\bar{V}_{0}\left(x\right)\right]

(H.135)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi nA_{0}}{T_{0}}\left[\left(\max_{x\in[-\pi,\pi]}V_{B}\left(nx\right)\right)+\left(\max_{x\in[-\pi,\pi]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=-\infty\\
p\neq 0\end{subarray}}^{\infty}V_{B}\left(n(x+2\pi p)\right)\right)\right]

(H.136)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi nA_{0}}{T_{0}}\left[1+\left(\max_{x\in[-\pi,\pi]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=-\infty\\
p\neq 0\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\left(\pi n(x+2\pi p)\right)^{2N}}\right)\right]

(H.137)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi nA_{0}}{T_{0}}\left[1+\max_{x\in[-\pi,\pi]}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\left(\pi n(x+2\pi p)\right)^{2N}}+\frac{1}{\left(\pi n(-x+2\pi p)\right)^{2N}}\right]

(H.138)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi nA_{0}}{T_{0}}\left[1+2\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\left(\pi n(-\pi+2\pi p)\right)^{2N}}\right]\leq\frac{2\pi nA_{0}}{T_{0}}\left[1+\frac{1}{\left(\pi^{2}n\right)^{2N}}\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}\right]

(H.139)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi nA_{0}}{T_{0}}\left[1+\frac{1}{4n^{2}}\right],

(H.140)

where we have used basic properties of the functions involved, e.g. the
2\pi
-periodicity of
\bar{V}_{0}
, the boundedness of
V_{B}(\cdot)\leq 1
and the fact that
N\in{\mathbbm{N}_{>0}}
. Recall that
A_{0}
is
d
-independent (since
N
is also
d
-independent by design). Recall that
n
on the other hand, is
d
dependent. As stated in the proof of
Theorem

4
, its dependency is the same as that used in the proof of
Theorem

and hence given by
eq.

C.115
. Thus since
\epsilon_{5}\in(0,1)
and
\sigma=d^{\epsilon_{6}}
with
\epsilon_{6}\in(\epsilon_{5},1)
, and all other quantities appearing on the r.h.s. of
eq.

C.115
are independent of
d
and
\bar{\varepsilon}
, we conclude that
n\leq d^{2}
for sufficiently large
d
and independently of
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
.

Therefore, from
eq.

H.134
, we find that the maximum eigenvalue of
V_{\textup{C}}\geq 0
is bounded by

\displaystyle\max_{j\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(k_{0})}\left\{\gamma_{0}\left[\frac{2\pi}{T_{0}}\bar{V}_{0}\left(\frac{2\pi}{d}j\right)\Big|_{x_{0}=x_{0}^{(l)}}\right]+\frac{\varepsilon_{b}}{2T_{0}}\right\}\leq\,\frac{2\pi\gamma_{0}nA_{0}}{T_{0}}\left[1+\frac{1}{4n^{2}}\right]+\frac{\varepsilon_{b}}{2T_{0}}.

(H.141)

The
\gamma_{0}
quantity is given by
eq.

G.17
, where
\bar{\gamma}_{0}
is independent of
d
and
\bar{\varepsilon}
. It scales with
d
as
\gamma_{0}\sim d^{{\bar{\varepsilon}}^{2}}
. Finally, recall that
\varepsilon_{b}
is given by
eq.

G.74
and hence tends to zero as
E_{0}
tends to infinity for all
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
. Recall that
E_{0}
is directly proportional to
P^{\textup{in}}
for all
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
, and therefore
\varepsilon_{b}
tends to zero, for all
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
, as
P^{\textup{in}}
tends to infinity.

Therefore, we conclude that the largest eigenvalue
v_{\textup{C}}
is upper bounded by an
\bar{\varepsilon}
-independent polynomial in
P^{\textup{in}}
. And thus
\|V_{\textup{C}}\|
is also upper bounded by an
\bar{\varepsilon}
-independent polynomial in
P^{\textup{in}}
. Hence since
\varepsilon_{r}
belongs to the function class
{\textup{SupPolyDecay}_{\displaystyle{\,\bar{\varepsilon}}}\,}(P^{\textup{in}})
, (introduced in
eq.

III.7
), it follows from
eq.

H.133
that
\varepsilon^{\textup{quality}}_{l}(T_{0}-t_{1})\to 0
for all fixed
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,1/6)
as
P^{\textup{in}}\to\infty
. From our above argument, this thus establishes the lower bound in
eq.

VI.3
.
∎
